# DNA: 1Q84 (Japanese Books 1-2 in 2009, Book 3 in 2010, Shinchosha; English 2011, trans. Jay Rubin for Books 1-2 and Philip Gabriel for Book 3. Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1Q84)
Type: novel (three books in one volume in this edition: Book 1 April-June, Book 2 July-September, Book 3 October-December)

## Engine in one paragraph
A woman steps off a jammed expressway down an emergency stairway and lands in a version of 1984 that is almost the same but slightly wrong. She names it 1Q84, and two moons hang in its sky. In alternating chapters, a math tutor and would-be novelist ghost-rewrites a teenage girl's strange novella, and the novella turns out to describe the forces that changed the world: the Little People, a cult called Sakigake, and the second moon. The two protagonists last met at age ten, when one of them held the other's hand in an empty classroom. As their threads close in on each other, the world itself presses back on them, through the cult's people and Ushikawa, the investigator who becomes the third viewpoint in Book 3. The question underneath it all is whether a world is real only if you believe in it and love someone in it. The novel answers by reuniting the pair under the two moons and sending them back up the same stairway.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. The switched track: a world with a question mark — level: premise
- What it is: The whole novel runs on a small, quiet change to reality. Aomame climbs down an expressway emergency stair, and afterward police guns are a different model, the news mentions events she has no memory of, and eventually there are two moons. She reasons her way to the idea that the world, not her mind, has changed. She names the new year 1Q84, with Q standing for a question mark. Later, Leader confirms that the track was switched, that the door opens only one way, and that the two moons are the sign. Most people cannot see that sign.
- Why it is specific to this work: The premise is tied to one historical year and to Orwell's title, and the change is marked by a visible, countable sign (a second moon). The book's own title is the name the heroine gives to that altered year.
- In-text refs: [1Q84 p.29] (the taxi driver warns her before she takes the stairs), [1Q84 p.156-157] (she decides the world itself has changed), [1Q84 p.162] (she names it 1Q84), [1Q84 p.272-273] (first sight of two moons), [1Q84 p.613-614] (Leader explains the switched track and the moons as its sign)
- External: https://www.koreajoongangdaily.com/lifestyle/for-haruki-murakami-the-surreal-is-often-more-real/11797315 — Murakami says he wanted to write the opposite of Orwell's book, a novel about the recent past showing "how things could have been," and that he always doubts whether the world he is in is the real one. The article also explains the Q/9 pun.

### 2. Alternating threads that converge, with a third voice in Book 3 — level: structure
- What it is: Books 1 and 2 alternate strictly between chapters headed "Aomame" and "Tengo", told in close third person. The two never meet, but their stories mirror and feed each other. Book 3 adds a third rotating viewpoint, Ushikawa, the man hunting them. Through his chapters the reader watches the gap between the lovers shrink from outside. The last chapter is headed with both of their names together.
- Why it is specific to this work: The alternation is the love story's form. Two people in parallel chapters who are in fact in the same city, and the heading of the final chapter, is where that form resolves.
- In-text refs: [1Q84 p.21] (Ch.1 "Aomame"), [1Q84 p.35] (Ch.2 "Tengo"), [1Q84 p.782] (Book 3 Ch.1 "Ushikawa"), [1Q84 p.1195] (Ch.31 "Tengo and Aomame"); the table of contents shows Book 1 and Book 2 with 24 chapters each and Book 3 with 31 [1Q84 p.15-17]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1Q84 — The article describes Books 1-2 as a dual narrative alternating Tengo and Aomame until the threads merge, with Ushikawa added as a third point of view in Book 3. It also reports a link between the 48 chapters and Bach's Well-Tempered Clavier, but that claim is the article's own and I have not traced it to Murakami.

### 3. Air Chrysalis: the rewritten book that comes true — level: device
- What it is: Tengo secretly rewrites Fuka-Eri's novella Air Chrysalis, about a girl in a mountain commune where the Little People weave a chrysalis that holds her double (maza and dohta) and where a second moon appears. His editor even asks him to describe the two moons in more detail. Later Tengo looks up and finds the same two moons in the real sky. He understands that he is living inside the story he polished, and the novella's terms (Perceiver, Receiver, dohta) turn out to be how the cult actually works.
- Why it is specific to this work: The world-change of strand 1 comes from a ghostwritten manuscript, which makes writing, and rewriting, the cause of the story's events. This is different from Murakami's usual story-within-a-story that only sheds light on the main plot.
- In-text refs: [1Q84 p.108] (Tengo's expand-and-cut rewrite), [1Q84 p.242] (the editor asks for fuller two-moon description), [1Q84 p.701-702] (Aomame reads the novella and senses Tengo's hand in it), [1Q84 p.712] (maza, dohta, Perceiver/Receiver inside the novella), [1Q84 p.722] (Tengo sees two moons and realizes it matches Air Chrysalis), [1Q84 p.1040] (Komatsu sums up the novella as a key)
- External: https://thecommononline.org/review-1q84/ — Emily Grecki's review notes that the altered two-moon world resembles a fiction Tengo is writing, which suggests the characters may have entered a fiction. The QUEST article (strand 4 URL) lists Air Chrysalis and the politics of authorship as one theme but does not develop it.

### 4. Little People instead of Big Brother: the closed group and violence against girls — level: theme
- What it is: The book keeps circling a sealed religious community (Sakigake) and an invisible power behind it. The Little People cannot be classed as good or evil, and they work through a Perceiver (a girl) and a Receiver (Leader). The cost falls on girls' bodies: Tsubasa's injuries, and Leader's claim that his acts with the girls happened only on the level of concepts. Aomame is a killer of abusive men who is sent to kill Leader. He asks her to do it for the sake of balance. The Professor explicitly sets the Little People against Orwell's Big Brother, as an unseen force in place of a visible tyrant.
- Why it is specific to this work: The Orwell inversion (Big Brother replaced by tiny, faceless agents) and the Perceiver/Receiver mechanism only exist in 1Q84. The book links them to Murakami's work on the Aum cult.
- In-text refs: [1Q84 p.323] (the Professor contrasts Big Brother with the Little People), [1Q84 p.327] (Tsubasa names the Little People; the dowager describes her injuries), [1Q84 p.360] (Sakigake and the Yamanashi gun battle), [1Q84 p.593] (Leader asks to be killed to keep the world's balance), [1Q84 p.617] (Perceiver and Receiver; Leader's evasions about his daughter)
- External: https://therumpus.net/2009/06/29/long-interview-with-murakami-translated-into-english — Murakami says the novel draws on his interviews with Aum victims and members and is about fundamentalism. He compares borrowed ready-made beliefs to junk food. Supporting: https://www.theology.cuhk.edu.hk/quest/index.php/quest/article/download/58/27 — Guevarra (QUEST, 2018) reads the Little People as the replacement for Big Brother, an invisible power whose every use calls up a counterforce.

### 5. A twenty-year love proved by a shared sky (the paper moon) — level: theme
- What it is: The emotional engine is a single moment. At age ten, Aomame held Tengo's hand in an empty classroom while both looked at a daytime moon, then ran away. Twenty years later, each is looking for the other. At the reunion, the question is not whether they love each other but whether they see the same thing: two moons, which means they are in the same world. Leader's song "It's Only a Paper Moon" links belief and love to reality. Aomame recalls it as she climbs back up the stairs, and the book ends with the moon fading to a paper moon at dawn.
- Why it is specific to this work: The love is tied to the moon motif and to the book's question about reality, so the proof of love and the proof of the world are the same act. That is the novel's resolution, not a side theme.
- In-text refs: [1Q84 p.696-697] (Tengo recovers the classroom memory and the daytime moon), [1Q84 p.614] (Leader on belief, love and the paper moon song), [1Q84 p.1185-1186] (reunion: she asks whether they see the same thing; two moons), [1Q84 p.1199] (Aomame recalls the song as she climbs the stairs), [1Q84 p.1210] (last image: a gray paper moon)
- External: https://globalvoices.org/2009/06/16/japan-1q84-the-new-bestseller-by-haruki-murakami/ — reports Murakami telling Time the book is "a very long, weird love story." Supporting: the QUEST article (strand 4 URL) argues that the pure bond between Aomame and Tengo is the key to the novel, and cites Leader's line that a world without love is phony.

### 6. Janáček's Sinfonietta as the door and the thread — level: device
- What it is: The novel opens with the Sinfonietta on a taxi radio, and the piece gives Aomame a bodily shock and knowledge she should not have. The track switch happens while it plays. Tengo turns out to have played its timpani part in a high-school band and listens to it every morning as a talisman. When Aomame retraces the taxi ride, the piece is noticeably missing. Even Ushikawa feels his vision cloud when it is announced on the radio. One piece of music links all three viewpoints to the crossing point.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is not generic Murakami jazz or classical background. One named piece works as the doorway into 1Q84 and as a hidden link between characters who have not yet met.
- In-text refs: [1Q84 p.24] (she recognizes it by reflex and feels a strange wrench), [1Q84 p.29] (the final movement begins as she decides to leave the cab), [1Q84 p.251] (Tengo's timpani part), [1Q84 p.441] (his morning ritual with the record), [1Q84 p.751] (the piece is absent on the return ride), [1Q84 p.963] (Ushikawa's clouded vision when it is announced)
- External: https://symphony.org/new-book-spurs-sales-of-cleveland-orchestra-cd/ — a July 2009 report that the novel's use of the Sinfonietta set off a sales spike for the Cleveland Orchestra recording (about 6,000 orders) in Japan. Wikipedia (strand 1 URL) also says the piece recurs at crucial points.

## Weak candidates
- The Town of Cats and the NHK-collector father. Tengo reads a story about a town of cats you cannot leave to his father, a retired NHK fee collector. A late Tengo chapter is titled after leaving that town, and the father is cremated in his NHK uniform. A door-knocking fee collector also menaces Aomame, Fuka-Eri and Ushikawa in Book 3 [1Q84 p.537], [1Q84 p.549], [1Q84 p.845], [1Q84 p.1122]. This is strong and recurring, but it belongs to Tengo's father subplot and Book 3's menace rather than the engine. It may be a strand of its own, about fathers and places you can't come back from. External: none found.
- Ushikawa as a narrative stance. His chapters bring an outsider's view of the lovers' convergence, with comic and pitiful tones, and a late omniscient aside in which the narrator lists what characters missed [1Q84 p.782], [1Q84 p.1022-1023]. I folded this into strand 2 because its function is structural.
- The flat, procedural treatment of the uncanny: a dog blown apart and cleaned up with paper towels, a clock rising off a table [1Q84 p.401]. This is central to the book's tone but is close to a general Murakami habit, so I did not list it as a strand.

## Why 6 strands
1Q84 is a three-book novel built from a premise (the switched year), a form (alternating threads that meet), two specific devices (the self-fulfilling novella and the Sinfonietta as doorway), and two themes (the invisible power of the closed group, and love as proof of a shared reality). Each of these is named or staged explicitly in the text. The remaining recurring material (the cat town, the fee collector, the flat uncanny) supports these six rather than driving the book.
# DNA: A Wild Sheep Chase (1982, Japanese original *Hitsuji o meguru bōken*, Kodansha; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Wild_Sheep_Chase, and the book's copyright page [A Wild Sheep Chase p.286]; English translation by Alfred Birnbaum, 1989, same sources)
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
An unnamed, thirtyish Tokyo ad man has put a dull pastoral photo into an insurance brochure. Then a right-wing kingpin's secretary tells him one sheep in that photo has a star on its back, and orders him to find it within two months or be ruined. What follows looks like a hard-boiled detective chase, a deliberately absurd one, but it is really a journey north and backward. It goes from Tokyo to Hokkaido and from 1978 back through Japan's imperial century. It also goes from adulthood back to the narrator's last link with his youth, his friend the Rat. The sheep is a will that settles inside a human host and uses that person to build power. The novel's moral climax comes when the Rat refuses it by killing himself with the sheep still inside him. The narrator is nameless, a little too passive, and does it all with deadpan logistics. He ends up as the only survivor of a generation's story, crying on what is left of a beach.

## DNA strands (7)

### 1. The impossible errand: find one sheep in all of Japan — level: premise
- What it is: The plot runs on an absurd ultimatum. One photograph, one star-marked sheep, a two-month deadline, and the threat of ruin. The narrator is pushed into a detective-style quest he never applied for. At the end it turns out the secretary had planned the route all along and used the narrator's ignorance and blankness as bait to draw out the sheep's next host.
- Why it is specific to this work: Other Murakami searches look for a wife, a cat or a girl. Here the target is a single farm animal hidden in a photo. That turns the hard-boiled quest into a parody of itself.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.40] (the chase officially begins with the phone call about sheep), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.58] (he counts the sheep in the photo and the recount comes out one higher), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.112] (the two-month ultimatum, even if no such sheep exists), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.274] (the secretary reveals he used the narrator's innocence as part of a program)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/the-paper/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — Julian Loose (LRB, 1992) says the novel borrows the form of a Chandleresque detective story. Its hero plays Marlowe a little self-consciously, and the book adds a thriller's cause-and-effect drive to Murakami's musings. Supporting source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Wild_Sheep_Chase calls it a postmodern detective novel in which dreams and hallucinations matter more than clues.

### 2. The possessing sheep: a will that uses people to build power — level: device / theme
- What it is: The sheep is no animal. It is a force that enters a human host. It shows up as a blood cyst in the brain, gives the host terrible charisma and a single overriding will, and then drops the person, who is left empty. It passed from the Sheep Professor (who was purged for saying a sheep was inside him) to the Boss, who used it to build a hidden postwar empire across politics, finance and media. Next it chose the Rat. What it offers is described as unity, beauty and the dissolving of the self, which is also the language of totalitarian power.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book's central symbol works as a political mechanism, a body-snatching ideology. That is different from Murakami's later, more private uncanny forces.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.103] (the Boss's brain cyst, found by US Army doctors before the war-crimes tribunal), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.107–108] (the underground kingdom and the will that cannot be shared, only passed on whole or lost), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.110] (the secretary's hypothesis that the sheep entered the Boss in 1936), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.167–168] (the Sheep Professor's interrogation: a sheep is inside him), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.264–265] (the Rat on what the sheep wanted from him and what it offered)
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/a-wild-sheep-chase-1982 — Glynne Walley reads the sheep as a force that dissolves the individual into something larger, close to the fascist ideal of strength through unity, and active in right-wing politics and hidden control. Also https://www.lrb.co.uk/the-paper/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — Loose calls the book distinctly allegorical, with the sheep annexing nearly every part of Japanese society.

### 3. Sheep as the thread of Japan's imperial history — level: theme
- What it is: The novel ties the sheep to modern Japanese history in documented detail. Sheep were pushed onto Hokkaido settlers so the military could have wool for its wars on the continent. The Sheep Professor's career runs through the colonial agriculture bureaucracy in Korea and Manchuria. The Boss's power starts in a 1930s prison and passes through China, and the US Occupation takes over a sheep ranch. On the way north the narrator reads a local town history, and its central story is an Ainu guide whose son dies in an army wool coat in the Russo-Japanese War. Following the sheep means following the line of empire from the frontier to the postwar back rooms.
- Why it is specific to this work: In this book Murakami's later interest in Japan's wartime past takes the form of livestock policy and a settler-town chronicle. No other novel of his does that.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.189] (the government gives the village sheep for military wool as the Russo-Japanese War approaches; the Ainu shepherd's son is killed), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.168] (chronology: the Japan-Manchuria Sheep Scheme, the Occupation taking the ranch), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.105–106] (the Boss's 1930s imprisonment, his rebirth in 1936, his move to the Chinese mainland), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.183–184] (the founding of Junitaki by debt-fleeing settlers led by an Ainu youth)
- External: https://www.themodernnovel.org/?p=4868 — the reviewer reads the sheep as the spirit of right-wing Japan and says the book is ultimately about the soul of Japan. Also https://www.lrb.co.uk/the-paper/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — Loose identifies the creature as a mutant Manchurian sheep.

### 4. Chasing a sheep, finding the Rat: a friend's death as the real destination — level: premise / theme
- What it is: The sheep hunt is a disguised search for the narrator's lost friend. The Rat sent the photo as a coded call for help, and his letters draw the narrator to the town and then the mountain. The resolution is a conversation in total darkness with the Rat, who is already dead. He explains that he hanged himself while the sheep slept inside him so that it would die with him. He chose his own weakness, and small ordinary pleasures, over limitless power. His last request sets up the explosion that kills the secretary. The money from the job then goes to J's Bar, which makes the Rat and the narrator co-partners.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is the closing book of the Rat trilogy. It ends a friendship that runs through three novels, and it makes the refusal of power an act of loyalty to weakness.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.67] (the Rat's first letter from the snow), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.261–263] (the Rat confirms he is dead, he died with the sheep inside him, the photo was a code), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.266] (why he refused the sheep), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.268] (the clock-wire request and the twelve o'clock rendezvous), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.275] (the explosions seen from the train), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.278–279] (the check, co-partnership at J's Bar)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/the-paper/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — Loose says the Rat survives from the individualistic Sixties, his moral weakness makes him a host, and he kills himself to stop the sheep. Supporting source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Wild_Sheep_Chase describes the novel as the third book in the Trilogy of the Rat.

### 5. After the Sixties: a dated, belated narrator — level: stance
- What it is: The book is told by a first-person narrator near thirty who already feels finished. The parts carry date headings, starting with November 25, 1970, the day Mishima's face is on the student-lounge TV. From there it moves forward in dated jumps (eight years later, two months later). Everything is seen from after the 1960s collapsed. A forgotten girl who died at 26, a divorce, and a bar town filled in over the sea are all reported in a flat, deflated voice. The villain even diagnoses that generation's attempt to expand consciousness as a failure. The stance is generational mourning, made to look like indifference.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book's calendar is fixed to the day of Mishima's death, and it is built as a reckoning with the narrator's generation. The deadpan voice is Murakami's, but here it serves to bury an era.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.4] (Part One headed November 25, 1970), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.10] (Mishima on the muted lounge TV), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.6] (the end of the sixties as the backdrop to the dead girl), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.81] (J and the narrator agree his generation is over), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.109] (the secretary on the failed expansion of consciousness at the end of the sixties)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Wild_Sheep_Chase — notes that all three Rat novels begin from or refer back to November 25, 1970, the day of Mishima's seppuku. Also https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/a-wild-sheep-chase-1982 — Walley opens with that date as the background to the narrator's post-student-movement disillusionment and calls the Rat a specimen of late-'70s boomer malaise.

### 6. A world without names — level: device
- What it is: Almost nobody in the novel has a proper name: the narrator, his ex-wife, the girlfriend, the partner, the secretary, the Boss. The narrator cannot remember the name of the dead girl from the opening. The rule gets a comic set piece of its own. The chauffeur argues over whether a nameless old cat deserves a name, christens him Kipper, and then debates why ships get names and planes don't. That makes the namelessness a theme people in the book discuss, not only a stylistic habit.
- Why it is specific to this work: Namelessness runs through early Murakami, but this book stages it as an argument and ties naming to being alive and having a will. That links back to the sheep's erasure of selves.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.6] (he has forgotten her name and chooses not to look it up), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.138–139] (the nameless cat named Kipper), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.140] (why ships have names and planes don't)
- External: https://windupbird.substack.com/p/the-weird-world-of-names-in-murakami — discusses the Kipper scene and reports that in *Novelist as Vocation* Murakami said he long could not give his characters names (nicknames like Rat or J were the exception). Also https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/wild-sheep-chase-haruki-murakami — EBSCO links the nameless narrator to his weak hold on identity.

### 7. The Sheep Man and the mirror: masks and doubles on the mountain — level: device
- What it is: Alone in the Rat's mountain house, the narrator is visited by the Sheep Man. He is small, wears a sheepskin with real horns, talks in run-together words, and says he went into hiding as a sheep because he did not want to go to war. The narrator senses another self in the stair mirror. The mirror shows the room without the Sheep Man in it, and smashing the guitar finally forces the truth out. The Sheep Man was the Rat wearing a borrowed form, and he could only speak face to face in the dark. Doubles stand in for the man the narrator cannot reach directly.
- Why it is specific to this work: The Sheep Man appears again later, but this is his origin. Here he is a war-refusing mask for the dead friend, and the mirror test is how the narrator finds that out.
- In-text refs: [A Wild Sheep Chase p.232–233] (the Sheep Man arrives: costume, speech, whiskey), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.246–247] (he says he hid as a sheep to avoid going to war), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.254] (the guitar is smashed; the mirror shows the room without the Sheep Man), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.267] (the Rat admits he took the Sheep Man's form), [A Wild Sheep Chase p.271] (the narrator says goodbye to his mirror self)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/the-paper/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — Loose describes the figure as a deranged deserter in a sheep suit. Also https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/wild-sheep-chase-haruki-murakami — EBSCO notes that the narrator discovers the Rat and the Sheep Man are the same person.

## Weak candidates
- The girlfriend's ears (signature device). Her unblocked ears make her overwhelmingly beautiful and let her foresee the sheep phone call, so they do start the chase [A Wild Sheep Chase p.35–36], [A Wild Sheep Chase p.39]. But she leaves the story at the mountain [A Wild Sheep Chase p.226], and the Rat says her power is gone [A Wild Sheep Chase p.267–268]. She works as a launcher and guide, not as the engine. External: https://www.themodernnovel.org/?p=4868 mentions that he falls in love with her ears, without analysis. It may deserve full strand status if the user sees the ears as iconic.
- History delivered as found documents (structure). Backstory comes in as an interrogation transcript, a year-by-year chronology whose last entry is the narrator himself, US Army medical records, and a local history read on the train [A Wild Sheep Chase p.167–169], [A Wild Sheep Chase p.183–191], [A Wild Sheep Chase p.103–105]. This is distinctive, but it may be a technique serving strand 3 rather than a separate strand. External: none found.
- The uncanny reported flatly (the sheep recount that comes out one higher, the dead friend saying plainly that he is dead) [A Wild Sheep Chase p.58], [A Wild Sheep Chase p.261]. Central here, but too generic to Murakami to count as this book's DNA.

## Why 7 strands
The book is a hybrid. It is a parody detective plot (1), a political-historical allegory with its own mechanism (2, 3), and the elegiac end of a trilogy (4, 5). It also has two signature devices that only this book uses in this way (6, 7). Merging any two would hide a level a reader of the book would recognize as essential.
# DNA: After Dark (2004 in Japan, Kodansha; English 2007, tr. Jay Rubin — source: copyright page [After Dark p.179]; also https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_Dark_(novel))
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
One autumn night in Tokyo, from a minute before midnight to first light, is watched by a disembodied "we" that moves like a film camera. Two sisters split the novel between them. Mari, the younger one, stays awake in an all-night Denny's and gets drawn, one stranger at a time, into the city's hidden night: a trombonist, a love-hotel manager, a beaten Chinese prostitute she interprets for. Eri, the older one, sleeps an unnatural sleep in a room where an unplugged TV switches on and carries her to an empty office watched over by a faceless man. The two threads never meet in plot. They are held together by objects and violence passed from hand to hand (a pencil, a phone, a photo), by the feeling that the wall between ordinary people and brutality is paper-thin, and by Mari's final return home to lie down beside her sister as morning comes. The book does not solve its mystery. Its question is whether one night of contact with strangers can bring a person, and a sister, back across the gap.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. One night, midnight to dawn, on the clock — level: structure
- What it is: The whole novel runs inside a single night. Time stamps and clocks mark its progress: a digital clock turns from 11:59 to 0:00 just as the TV wakes up, and a kitchen clock reads 4:31 and then 4:33 deep in the night. The book ends as the light gets stronger and the narration says the new day's time is taking over from the night's. Each chapter is a continuous stretch of real time, and the chapters cut between places.
- Why it is specific to this work: No other Murakami novel is confined to a single night tracked by the clock, and the ending is simply the sun coming up.
- In-text refs: [After Dark p.26] (11:59 p.m. turns to 0:00), [After Dark p.137], [After Dark p.138] (4:31, then 4:33, time running on without a break), [After Dark p.174] (morning light makes the night's time recede), [After Dark p.177] (the night opens up; there is time before the next dark)
- External: https://januarymagazine.com/fiction/afterdark.html — the reviewer notes the book runs from just before midnight to dawn, mostly in continuous real-time scenes cut between locations. (Glynne Walley's review at https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=338 adds that each chapter carries a start time. The ebook text used here does not show those headings, so that detail is not confirmed in-text.)

### 2. "We," the viewpoint camera — level: stance
- What it is: The narrator is a collective "we" that calls itself a camera floating in midair. It drops from a bird's-eye view of the city into a Denny's, then into Eri's room. It sets itself rules: it is an invisible intruder that watches and does not intervene, like a time traveler who must not change anything. It also admits what it cannot know. The rule is broken once, when "we" shout at Eri to run. The final pages call it a pure point of view.
- Why it is specific to this work: Murakami usually writes in the first person ("boku"). Here the telling is a self-aware cinematic "we," almost like a screenplay, with its own rule of non-intervention that it then breaks.
- In-text refs: [After Dark p.4] (eyes of a night bird taking in the city from the air), [After Dark p.24] (we become one point of view, a camera in midair), [After Dark p.26] (we observe but do not intervene), [After Dark p.137] ("we" forget the neutrality rule and shout), [After Dark p.177]
- External: https://www.npr.org/transcripts/12319069 — Alan Cheuse's review credits Murakami with the term "viewpoint camera" and ties it to the sleeping-sister sections. Also https://www.csmonitor.com/2007/0515/p16s01-bogn.html — calls the narration an impassive camera that observes without interpreting or intervening.

### 3. Two sisters: one awake in the night, one asleep at home — level: premise
- What it is: The novel is built on a split. Mari, the "smart" sister, wanders the night with her eyes open. Eri, the beautiful model sister, has withdrawn into a sleep so deep it seems unnatural. Mari's threads and Eri's threads alternate, and the reader learns about Eri mostly through other people: Takahashi guesses that Eri envied Mari's independence, and the hotel maid Korogi urges Mari to dig up one memory of real closeness. That memory turns out to be the two girls holding each other when an elevator got stuck in the dark. At dawn Mari goes home, gets into bed beside Eri, kisses her and asks her to come back, and Eri's lips move for an instant.
- Why it is specific to this work: The emotional arc of the book is the gap between two sisters (their names differ by one syllable), crossed only at the very end. It is not a lost-lover quest.
- In-text refs: [After Dark p.24] (Eri introduced as Mari's sister, asleep), [After Dark p.116] (Takahashi's reading of Eri's role and envy), [After Dark p.151] (Korogi tells Mari to recall a moment with no gap between them), [After Dark p.169], [After Dark p.170] (the elevator in the dark; the sisters drift apart afterward), [After Dark p.173] (Mari in Eri's bed; one syllable's difference; "come back"), [After Dark p.177] (Eri's lips tremble)
- External: https://spectator.com/article/children-of-the-night/ — the review frames the book around Eri's withdrawn, hikikomori-like sleep and Mari, who cannot bear to watch it and stays out all night. Also https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=338 — notes that Mari feels overshadowed by Eri the model.

### 4. The unplugged TV as a circuit to the other side — level: device
- What it is: At midnight the TV in Eri's room comes on, though its plug has been pulled. On the screen is a bare room where a man sits in a chair, his face hidden by a mask. Later Eri's bed is found inside the screen with her in it, while an identical empty bed stays in her room. She wakes in that empty room, which looks like Shirakawa's office, and picks up a pencil with the same company name as the ones on his desk. The screen behaves like a circuit that can connect and break. When it breaks, the two worlds come apart. At the end the narrator thinks the screen flickers once, but sees nothing.
- Why it is specific to this work: Murakami often uses an "other side," but here it is reached through an electrical appliance. It is filmed and broadcast like a live feed, and it is tied to a real man in the waking story through one dull pencil.
- In-text refs: [After Dark p.26] (TV comes on unplugged at 0:00), [After Dark p.27] (the room and the man in the chair), [After Dark p.81] (the same bed is on the screen; the room's bed is a stand-in), [After Dark p.102] (Eri finds the veritech pencil; the room resembles Shirakawa's office), [After Dark p.121] (Shirakawa's dull veritech pencil next to six sharp ones), [After Dark p.137] (the circuit shakes and the two worlds divide), [After Dark p.174] (the screen seems to flash, then shows nothing)
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=338 — describes the unplugged TV and the masked man watching Eri. The reviewer identifies the room as Shirakawa's deserted office, and notes the novel never confirms that Shirakawa is the man.

### 5. A relay of strangers, linked by things passed hand to hand — level: structure
- What it is: The plot moves by chain reaction among people who mostly never meet. Mari's Chinese brings her into the Alphaville love hotel to interpret for a prostitute that Shirakawa has beaten. Kaoru pulls his photo from the security camera and hands it to the gang behind the girl. Shirakawa leaves the girl's phone on a 7-Eleven shelf. That phone rings in Takahashi's hand with a threat meant for Shirakawa. The threads are tied by objects and calls rather than by characters meeting, and the culprit is never caught on the page.
- Why it is specific to this work: The cast's lives touch only through objects passed along in one night, so the link stays secondhand and unresolved. That is the novel's way of showing a city where people sit side by side and still miss each other.
- In-text refs: [After Dark p.36] (Mari speaks Chinese to the beaten woman), [After Dark p.68] (Kaoru offers the security-camera photo), [After Dark p.124] (Shirakawa leaves the phone beside the cheese), [After Dark p.158], [After Dark p.159] (Takahashi answers; the threat is aimed at someone else)
- External: https://januarymagazine.com/fiction/afterdark.html — describes Mari's chance meeting with Takahashi setting off a chain of encounters across the night city, with links between threads that are tenuous and dreamlike, not explained.

### 6. A paper-thin wall between ordinary life and violence — level: theme
- What it is: The book keeps asking how far "normal" people really are from brutality and collapse. Takahashi describes sitting in on trials and realizing that the wall between himself and the defendants might be made of papier-mâché. He pictures the system as a giant deep-sea octopus that wipes out people's names. Shirakawa is the proof: a neat, overtime-working engineer who works to Bach and then calmly goes through the bloody clothes he stripped from the girl. Korogi, on the run, says the ground can drop out from under anyone.
- Why it is specific to this work: Here the theme is carried by a perfectly ordinary office worker who never faces consequences, and it is spelled out in Takahashi's trial story. That makes it the moral core of this book, not just Murakami's general darkness.
- In-text refs: [After Dark p.88], [After Dark p.89] (the papier-mâché wall; the octopus that strips people of names), [After Dark p.73] (Shirakawa's orderly late-night work to Bach), [After Dark p.121] (he goes through the bloodstained clothes as if they were worthless garbage), [After Dark p.142] (Korogi: the ground can drop away), [After Dark p.117] (Mari: the Chinese girl was nineteen too; the worlds are too different)
- External: https://januarymagazine.com/fiction/afterdark.html — calls Shirakawa a seemingly normal salaryman capable of brutality. https://www.csmonitor.com/2007/0515/p16s01-bogn.html — notes that Murakami withholds moral judgment even from the man who did the beating.

## Weak candidates
- Mirror that keeps Mari's image after she leaves the Skylark restroom [After Dark p.59], [After Dark p.60]. It is striking, and the Spectator review links it to the screens and inversions. But it happens once and works as an echo of strand 4, not as a separate engine.
- The title tune: Curtis Fuller's "Five Spot After Dark" is the trombonist's origin story, and Mari hums the next eight bars [After Dark p.19], [After Dark p.20]. It plants the title and the bond between Mari and Takahashi, but music runs through all of Murakami. Here it decorates the book more than it drives it.
- Memory as fuel: Korogi's idea that every kind of memory feeds the fire that keeps you alive [After Dark p.152]. It is important, but it serves strand 3 because it sets up the elevator memory. External: none found.
- Deep-sea / creature imagery: the city as one organism, the night as a deep trench, Creatures of the Deep on Shirakawa's TV [After Dark p.4], [After Dark p.81], [After Dark p.137]. It is consistent, but it is an imagery system, not a mechanism. Its strongest use is the octopus, which is folded into strand 6.

## Why 6 strands
The novel is short, but it is built from separable engines: a clock-bound frame (1), an unusual "we" camera stance (2), the two-sisters premise (3), the TV portal device (4), the object-relay plot (5) and the thin-wall theme (6). Each one carries weight that the others do not. Everything else checked (mirror, jazz, memory, sea imagery) turned out to serve one of these six.
# DNA: The City and Its Uncertain Walls (2023 Japan, Shinchosha; English 2024, trans. Philip Gabriel. Sources: https://lithub.com/haruki-murakamis-latest-novel-is-an-expansion-of-a-story-he-started-over-40-years-ago/ (Japan, 13 April 2023) and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_City_and_Its_Uncertain_Walls (English, November 2024))
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A seventeen-year-old boy and a sixteen-year-old girl invent a walled town together. She tells him her real self lives there and the girl beside him is only a stand-in, a shadow. Then she vanishes. Decades later the narrator gets into that town by giving up his shadow, and he works in its bookless library reading old dreams, with a version of her who doesn't remember him. He then finds himself back in the ordinary world, running a small mountain library whose dead former director keeps visiting him, until a strange boy who has memorized the town takes his place inside the walls. Everything turns on one question that the book keeps asking and keeps refusing to settle: which self is the real one, and which is the shadow? The question is put each time through the same choice, whether to stay inside the wall or to leave. The engine is grief for a first love that disappeared without explanation. The town is where that grief can go on forever, because the town has no time.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. Self and shadow, real and stand-in — level: premise
- What it is: The book starts from the girl's claim that the self in front of him is only a shadow and her true self lives in the walled town. To enter the town the narrator has his own shadow cut away. From then on, the line between "real self" and "shadow" keeps blurring and reversing. Near the end, his shadow tells him that everything it argues is really the narrator talking to himself. The narrator wakes up outside the wall with a shadow attached, though he chose to stay inside. Koyasu and later the boy tell him that self and shadow can trade roles, and that each is the other's necessary other half.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole plot is a series of separations and reunions between a person and their shadow, and the novel ends by declining to say which half is the original.
- In-text refs: [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.17], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.151], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.155], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.297], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.450], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.486]
- External: https://local10.com/entertainment/2023/04/12/in-new-book-murakami-explores-walled-city-and-shadows — AP report in which Murakami says the shadow works as a form of his subconscious that helps him understand himself, and that the choice between staying inside the wall and going beyond it now feels larger than ever. Also https://strangehorizons.com/wordpress/non-fiction/the-city-and-its-uncertain-walls-by-haruki-murakami/ — the reviewer says the book refuses to fix what shadow or "real" self mean, and counts that as a strength.

### 2. The vanished first love as a lifelong wound — level: theme
- What it is: The emotional core is a girl who, at sixteen, disappears without warning or explanation. The narrator lets that loss shape the next thirty years: no close ties, no marriage, a fear that anyone he loves will vanish the same way. The novel repeats this pattern of vanishing in other people. Koyasu loses his wife and then his small son. The Yellow Submarine Boy vanishes without a trace, described in the same image of smoke. Koyasu names the wound outright: a pure love that is cut off becomes both supreme happiness and a curse. In late Part 2 the narrator's careful, middle-aged feeling for the coffee-shop owner is set explicitly against the all-consuming love he felt at seventeen.
- Why it is specific to this work: Unlike the earlier versions of this material, this novel follows the loss through decades of an adult life and doubles it in a mentor (Koyasu) who carries the same wound.
- In-text refs: [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.127], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.136], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.295], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.296], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.398], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.441]
- External: https://strangehorizons.com/wordpress/non-fiction/the-city-and-its-uncertain-walls-by-haruki-murakami/ — calls the lasting effect of this first loss the book's most moving and effective theme, tied to wider grief and loneliness. Also https://www.bostonglobe.com/2024/11/12/arts/with-his-dreamlike-love-letter-to-libraries-murakami-is-back — traces how her disappearance stays with him through college, his avoidance of marriage and his publishing career.

### 3. Second-person address to the lost girl — level: stance
- What it is: The narrator tells the teenage romance, and his time with her double in the town, to "you", the girl herself. The story reads like a long letter to someone who will never read it. At the worst point of the grief, "you" briefly becomes general, so the reader is the one being abandoned. In the adult summary of his twenties to forties, the narrator still addresses her as "you".
- Why it is specific to this work: The address to "you" is the voice of the first-love strand, so the reader feels the girl as an absence that is spoken to directly.
- In-text refs: [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.16], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.20], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.127], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.131], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.137]
- External: https://strangehorizons.com/wordpress/non-fiction/the-city-and-its-uncertain-walls-by-haruki-murakami/ — notes that the first love, addressed as "you", is the emotional center and the most developed of the female characters.

### 4. The library of old dreams and its line of keepers — level: device
- What it is: The town's library holds no books. Its shelves hold egg-shaped old dreams, and the Dream Reader's job is to coax them out in his hands. The book mirrors this with a real library in the Fukushima town of Z**. Its former director is the ghost of Mr. Koyasu, who has no shadow and wears a handless watch. He mentors the narrator in a warm basement room at night. The office then passes down a line: Koyasu to the narrator to the Yellow Submarine Boy. The boy reads everything, draws an accurate map of the walled town, and finally merges with the narrator by biting his ear so that he can read dreams in his place.
- Why it is specific to this work: The paired libraries and their passing of the job from one keeper to the next are what connect the realistic half of the book to the fable half.
- In-text refs: [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.19], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.168], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.218], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.232], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.322], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.342], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.465], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.472], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.487]
- External: https://www.bostonglobe.com/2024/11/12/arts/with-his-dreamlike-love-letter-to-libraries-murakami-is-back — reads the novel as a paean to books, reading and libraries, and describes the bookless library and the Dream Reader. Also https://dev.australianbookreview.com.au/1023-abr-online/2024/december-2024-no-471/14239-cassandra-atherton-review-the-city-and-its-uncertain-walls-by-haruki-murakami — treats the boy and his memorized books as a third library, and calls the subterranean talks with Koyasu's ghost the most engaging part of Part 2.

### 5. Time without hands — level: theme
- What it is: The walled town has no time in the usual sense. Rooms are described by what they lack, ending each time on the missing clock. The clock tower has no hands. Every goodbye there is permanent and also just the usual nightly goodbye, because everything is eternal. Koyasu's watch, also without hands, carries the town's timelessness into the real-world library. The narrator's nightly "see you tomorrow" to the girl, in a town where tomorrow doesn't exist, becomes a "farewell" when he decides to leave. In late Part 2, walking upstream in the river literally takes his body back to age seventeen.
- Why it is specific to this work: In this book stopped time is what grief looks like. The town keeps the sixteen-year-old forever, and leaving means letting time start again.
- In-text refs: [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.33], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.57], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.133], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.218], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.447], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.454], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.488], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.489]
- External: https://strangehorizons.com/wordpress/non-fiction/the-city-and-its-uncertain-walls-by-haruki-murakami/ — reads the town's timelessness as a stagnant half-life that resembles grief. The review does not go into detail on the handless clock.

### 6. Three-part shape that replays one choice — level: structure
- What it is: Part 1 alternates short chapters: the teenage romance in the real world, and the walled town told in a fable's plain style. It ends with the narrator refusing to escape through the pool and staying inside while his shadow leaves. Part 2 immediately reverses this: he is back in the ordinary world with a shadow and can't explain it. It then runs as a linear realist story in a mountain library. Part 3 is short and returns to the town, where he makes the same stay-or-leave decision again and this time leaves, by blowing out a candle. The Afterword says this is Murakami's third answer to a 1980 novella, after Hard-Boiled Wonderland. He meant it to stand beside the earlier version rather than overwrite it.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book's form is a rewrite. It re-stages the ending of its own source, and that becomes the plot of Parts 2 and 3.
- In-text refs: [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.20], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.23], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.148], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.154], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.155], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.452], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.491], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.493], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.494]
- External: https://dev.australianbookreview.com.au/1023-abr-online/2024/december-2024-no-471/14239-cassandra-atherton-review-the-city-and-its-uncertain-walls-by-haruki-murakami — describes the three parts (love story, middle-aged narrator, a shorter return to the town) and the earlier 1982 "double feature" attempt that became Hard-Boiled Wonderland. Also https://lithub.com/haruki-murakamis-latest-novel-is-an-expansion-of-a-story-he-started-over-40-years-ago/ — the 1980 story became the novel's first part; Murakami began it in 2020 and likened it to taking something out from the back of a drawer.

## Weak candidates
- The river as passage (the Kubla Khan epigraph about a sacred underground river, the riverbank opening, the underground river-maze of realities, the walk upstream that reverses age). It is strongly present [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.14], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.16], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.154], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.443], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.447]. But it seems to serve strands 1 and 5 rather than drive anything on its own. External: none found.
- Chapters joined by an echoed line (a chapter ends on a phrase and the next one opens by repeating or crossing it), e.g. the "uncertain wall" hinge [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.442], [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.443]. It is a real and frequent habit, but a stylistic one rather than an engine. The ABR review mentions the repeated line that opens and closes Chapters 36 and 37, as a criticism.
- The coffee-shop owner as a second, adult love. She is important in Part 2 [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.441], but she is best read as a counterweight inside strand 2.
- Music's absence from the town (no one inside the wall can hear any music, so the boy's Yellow Submarine parka means nothing there) [The City and Its Uncertain Walls p.454]. Telling, but it appears on only a few pages.

## Why 6 strands
The novel stands on a premise (self and shadow), a wound (the vanished girl), a voice for that wound (the "you"), a place where the wound is kept (the library of dreams and its keepers), a rule of that place (no time), and a form that rewrites its own source by making the same choice twice. Each is recognizable on its own, and nothing else in the book carries that much weight.
# DNA: Colorless Tsukuru Tazaki and His Years of Pilgrimage (2013, Japan; English 2014, tr. Philip Gabriel — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorless_Tsukuru_Tazaki_and_His_Years_of_Pilgrimage ; also the copyright page [CT p.10])
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A quiet 36-year-old engineer who designs railway stations was, at twenty, cut off without explanation by the four friends who made up his perfect high-school group of five, and he nearly died of it. He sealed the wound instead of opening it. A new woman, Sara, tells him he cannot hide from his own history and hands him the addresses, so the book becomes a pilgrimage: one visit after another (Nagoya, then Finland), each friend handing back a piece of the old story. Running under the investigation is a second, darker current that is never cleared up: dreams, a vanished friend, a strange tale of death, and the possibility that some part of Tsukuru was guilty of things he cannot remember. A single Liszt piano piece ties all the lost people together, and the station he builds becomes the image for the self he thinks is empty. The novel ends before Sara gives her answer.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. The unexplained expulsion — level: premise
- What it is: The engine is one hard, unexplained event: the four friends suddenly refuse all contact, no one will say why, and the young man is left with a near-fatal wish to die. Sixteen years later the same unanswered "why" is still the thing he carries, and the whole plot is the delayed asking of it.
- Why it is specific to this work: Unlike Murakami's usual vanished wife or girlfriend, the loss here is a whole group's verdict on him, and the question is not "where did she go" but "what did I do."
- In-text refs: [CT p.13], [CT p.31], [CT p.36], [CT p.184]
- External: https://english.ahram.org.eg/News/70947.aspx — at his 2013 Kyoto appearance Murakami described the hero as trying to learn why his four close friends rejected him 16 years earlier, and said he understood that pain from a milder experience of his own.

### 2. Colors in names, and the colorless one — level: device
- What it is: Each of the four friends has a color in the family name and goes by it (red, blue, white, black); Tsukuru alone has none, and from that accident of naming he builds his whole self-image as blank, an empty container. Later figures continue the code (a gray-named friend, a green-named stranger), and the friends he revisits keep telling him he was never as empty as he believes.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title itself is the device; the novel's identity question is staged as a literal color chart of a group, and its emotional resolution is someone telling him he is not colorless.
- In-text refs: [CT p.17], [CT p.22], [CT p.113], [CT p.203], [CT p.206], [CT p.231]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorless_Tsukuru_Tazaki_and_His_Years_of_Pilgrimage — lists the color meanings of Aka, Ao, Shiro, Kuro, plus Haida ("gray") and Midorikawa ("green"), and notes Tazaki has no color. Also https://lareviewofbooks.org/article/limits-knowing/ — reads the novel as centrally concerned with names and arbitrary origins.

### 3. Pilgrimage of visits, set in motion by Sara — level: structure
- What it is: The past is first told across a bar table and dinners to Sara, who says he cannot erase the history behind his memories and then researches where the four now live. The book then runs as a sequence of stations on a route: a visit to Ao in Nagoya, to Aka, then a flight to Finland to see Kuro/Eri, each interview revealing a different piece and a different version of the friend. It ends at the last stop with the answer still pending: he waits for Sara's decision, and the reader never learns it.
- Why it is specific to this work: It is the most investigative, step-by-step quest structure in Murakami's realist mode, literally a "years of pilgrimage" with one destination per chapter-run, ending on an open verdict.
- In-text refs: [CT p.22], [CT p.36], [CT p.95], [CT p.113], [CT p.191], [CT p.232]
- External: https://english.ahram.org.eg/News/70947.aspx — Murakami said Sara urges Tsukuru to face his past, and that she also pushed him (the author) to write more about the four friends. Also https://lareviewofbooks.org/article/limits-knowing/ — calls the quest structure a kind of detective story.

### 4. "Le mal du pays" as the thread through the lost — level: device
- What it is: A single Liszt piece from the Years of Pilgrimage suite is named and explained by Haida, instantly brings back a vivid memory of Shiro playing it, and survives as a record set Haida left behind that Tsukuru keeps an old turntable to play. It returns at each turning point, and in Finland Eri plays it on a different recording, leading to the book's emotional climax. The novel's title borrows the suite's name.
- Why it is specific to this work: Music in Murakami is usually atmosphere; here one named piece is the binding object that links the vanished friend, the dead girl and the pilgrimage itself, and gives the novel its title.
- In-text refs: [CT p.50], [CT p.51], [CT p.157], [CT p.193]
- External: https://lareviewofbooks.org/article/limits-knowing/ — argues that the different performances of the Liszt piece embody the book's method of variation on a theme.

### 5. The station as self — level: theme
- What it is: Tsukuru's one fixed interest is building railway stations, and the book keeps using the station as his way of being in the world: a container people pass through, a place he sits and watches others leave, and finally the metaphor he uses to declare love (first picture the station, then trains can stop). The Shinjuku chapter opens with an essay on the station before he appears, and the last image is consciousness leaving like the final express train.
- Why it is specific to this work: The protagonist's profession is not decoration but the novel's controlling metaphor for an "empty" self that still lets others arrive.
- In-text refs: [CT p.47], [CT p.216], [CT p.218], [CT p.227], [CT p.232]
- External: https://lareviewofbooks.org/article/limits-knowing/ — reads stations as recurring access points, a metaphor for connection.

### 6. The unaccountable shadow: dream guilt and unsolved side-stories — level: theme
- What it is: Alongside the clear investigation runs a dark current that is never resolved. Haida tells a secondhand tale of a man carrying a death token, then appears in a vivid night half-dream and vanishes from Tsukuru's life; Tsukuru files such questions in a mental drawer marked "Pending." The friends' accusation and Shiro's later murder meet his own erotic dreams, and he comes to feel that some unknown part of him may be responsible, while Eri names what hunted Shiro an evil spirit.
- Why it is specific to this work: The realist detective plot uncovers facts about the friends but leaves the question of Tsukuru's hidden, dreaming self deliberately open, which is what gives the book its unease.
- In-text refs: [CT p.65], [CT p.85], [CT p.91], [CT p.193], [CT p.200], [CT p.229]
- External: https://lareviewofbooks.org/article/limits-knowing/ — says the unanswered threads (Haida's disappearance, the Midorikawa story) are intentional. Also https://japaneselit.net/2013/10/24/colorless-tsukuru-tazaki-and-his-years-of-pilgrimage/ — notes the sleep-paralysis scene and supernatural elements are never explained, and calls Tsukuru's responsibility central.

## Weak candidates
- Hedging close third person: narration stays tight on Tsukuru and keeps correcting itself ([CT p.20], [CT p.54], [CT p.223] per notes). Real and pervasive, but close, self-qualifying narration is common in later Murakami, so not clearly unique. external: none found.
- Routine as lifeline (swimming set distances, laundry, ironing, [CT p.42] and notes p.14, 150): strong texture, but disciplined routine is a general Murakami habit.
- "Alone but not lonely" and other refrains ([CT p.28], Sara's history line repeated at [CT p.184]): good callbacks, but they serve strands 1 and 3 rather than standing as a separate mechanism.
- Six fingers motif (stationmaster's lost property, dream page-turner; notes p.137-140, 214): striking, but it hangs off strand 6 and is too minor to stand alone. Not reopened for this pass.

## Why 6 strands
The novel stands on a premise (the expulsion), a naming device (colors), a quest structure (Sara's pilgrimage), a binding musical object (Liszt), a professional metaphor (stations) and an unsolved dark counter-current (dream guilt); removing any one of them would make it a different book. The other techniques in the notes are surface style serving these six.
# DNA: Dance Dance Dance (1988, Japanese first edition, Kodansha; English translation by Alfred Birnbaum, 1994 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_Dance_Dance_(novel) ; Kodansha 1988 also given at http://www.exorcising-ghosts.co.uk/dance.html)
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A burned-out 34-year-old freelance writer, four and a half years after the events of A Wild Sheep Chase, keeps dreaming that the shabby Dolphin Hotel in Sapporo is calling him, and that the woman who vanished from it (Kiki) is crying for him there. He goes back and finds the old hotel replaced by a gleaming corporate tower that has kept the name. In a hidden, pitch-dark floor of it the Sheep Man tells him his life has come loose from everything, and that the only way to be reconnected is to keep dancing, keep moving, without asking why. What follows is a loose mystery whose clues come from a film, a psychic teenager and a famous actor. It plays out against 1980s Japanese consumer excess, and it leaves a trail of bodies: Mei, Dick North, Gotanda and, it turns out, Kiki herself. A room of six seated skeletons in Honolulu stands for this death count and for the narrator himself. The book ends when he chooses this side of the wall: a real woman, real clock time and morning light.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. The hotel that summons him back — level: premise
- What it is: The novel starts from a recurring dream in which the Dolphin Hotel is less a building than a situation the narrator belongs to, and somebody there is weeping for him. He decides the story can only restart where it broke off. On arrival he finds that the shabby hotel has been bought out and rebuilt as a high-tech luxury hotel that kept the old name. The name turns out to have been kept deliberately so that he would come back.
- Why it is specific to this work: It is a sequel premise. The engine is going back to a place from an earlier book and finding its outer shell replaced while its inner core (the dark floor and the Sheep Man's room) is still there.
- In-text refs: [Dance Dance Dance p.8], [Dance Dance Dance p.9], [Dance Dance Dance p.11], [Dance Dance Dance p.12], [Dance Dance Dance p.80]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_Dance_Dance_(novel) — describes it as a sequel to A Wild Sheep Chase, set four and a half years after it. https://www.kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/dance-dance-dance/ — summarizes the setup: dreams of Kiki calling him, a return to Sapporo, and the old Dolphin replaced by a new building.

### 2. Dance to stay connected (the Sheep Man as switchboard) — level: device / thematic engine
- What it is: In a candle-lit room on the hidden floor, the Sheep Man (who speaks in run-together words) says this place is the knot where everything the narrator has lost or will lose is tied together. His job is to connect the narrator back to it. The instruction he gives is the title: keep dancing for as long as the music plays, don't stop to think, because stopping is how a person gets stuck. The rest of the novel is this rule in action. The narrator lets himself be carried from one encounter to the next, and the book ends with him deciding to stay on the near side of the wall with Yumiyoshi.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title, the central instruction and the resolution all come from this one rule, and it reshapes the Sheep Man from A Wild Sheep Chase into the keeper of a connection hub.
- In-text refs: [Dance Dance Dance p.79], [Dance Dance Dance p.80], [Dance Dance Dance p.82], [Dance Dance Dance p.348], [Dance Dance Dance p.352]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_Dance_Dance_(novel) — says the Sheep Man's role is to connect things and lists the eventual discovery of a human connection among the themes. https://ejournal.usm.my/kajh/article/view/kajh_vol21-no-1-2014_3 — Jamshidian & Pirnajmuddin (2014) treat the dance image as central but argue that it is a dance with the shadows of a simulacral world, and that the Sheep Man offers only passivity.

### 3. Advanced capitalism and cultural snow-shoveling — level: theme
- What it is: The narrator makes his living writing filler copy and describes it, over and over, as shoveling snow: useless work that somebody has to do. He sees the society around him as advanced capitalism, where waste is a virtue, even good and bad come in fashionable and unfashionable versions, and a conglomerate's land grab is too ordinary to scandalize anyone. The same logic covers people's private lives. Gotanda's agency buys his condo and charges call girls to the company as business expenses, and the narrator senses that it is all images that would vanish if someone pulled the plug.
- Why it is specific to this work: No other Murakami novel turns the late-1980s bubble economy (expense accounts, brand names, the rebuilt hotel as the front line of a real-estate scheme) into the substance of the plot rather than its background.
- In-text refs: [Dance Dance Dance p.13], [Dance Dance Dance p.18], [Dance Dance Dance p.54], [Dance Dance Dance p.55], [Dance Dance Dance p.138], [Dance Dance Dance p.143]
- External: https://journals.iium.edu.my/asiatic/index.php/ajell/article/download/412/387/776 — Jonathan Dil, Asiatic 4.2 (2010), reads the novel as both a critique of late-capitalist Japan and complicit with it, since the narrator's ways of surviving keep him tied to the system. https://panoramajournal.org/issues/issue-16-encounters/encounters-the-ghosts-of-capitalism-a-review-of-murakamis-dance-dance-dance/ — reads the novel as exposing capitalism's hidden victims and notes the running snow-shoveling joke.

### 4. Gotanda: the film clue and the glamorous double — level: structure / device
- What it is: The mystery opens up when the narrator sees Kiki in a one-minute bed scene in a mediocre film, Unrequited Love, whose star is his former classmate Gotanda. He watches it again and again, and each time the scene is identical. Gotanda becomes his friend and his mirror: rich and famous where the narrator is anonymous, and trapped in roles where the narrator is free. In the end Gotanda half-confesses that he may have strangled Kiki without being sure he did, and soon afterward he drives his Maserati into Tokyo Bay.
- Why it is specific to this work: The detective plot runs through a screen image and through a double whose emptiness is the cost of celebrity. That structure belongs to this novel alone.
- In-text refs: [Dance Dance Dance p.96], [Dance Dance Dance p.98], [Dance Dance Dance p.136], [Dance Dance Dance p.318], [Dance Dance Dance p.319], [Dance Dance Dance p.324]
- External: https://journals.iium.edu.my/asiatic/index.php/ajell/article/download/412/387/776 — Dil names "enjoying through the Other," embodied by the celebrity Gotanda, as one of the narrator's survival strategies, and says it is recognized as damaging only too late. https://panoramajournal.org/issues/issue-16-encounters/encounters-the-ghosts-of-capitalism-a-review-of-murakamis-dance-dance-dance/ — describes Gotanda's luxury as masking emptiness and notes that he envies the narrator's anonymity and modest Subaru.

### 5. The death tally and the six skeletons — level: device / theme
- What it is: Following Kiki's apparition into an empty Honolulu building, the narrator finds a room with six human skeletons posed at home as if they were still living: on a sofa, at a dinner table, in front of a TV, and one in bed missing an arm. After Gotanda dies he counts the dead (the Rat, Kiki, Mei, Dick North, Gotanda), makes it five, and dreads who the sixth will be. In a later dream Kiki tells him the room and the skeletons are him. On the last page he still does not know who number six is.
- Why it is specific to this work: The room of skeletons is a one-off image that turns the novel's string of deaths into a countdown and a portrait of the self, and the novel deliberately leaves the count open at the end.
- In-text refs: [Dance Dance Dance p.246], [Dance Dance Dance p.247], [Dance Dance Dance p.324], [Dance Dance Dance p.325], [Dance Dance Dance p.332], [Dance Dance Dance p.352]
- External: https://journals.iium.edu.my/asiatic/index.php/ajell/article/download/412/387/776 — Dil reads the six skeletons, together with the Sheep Man's idea of "tendencies", as pointing to death and to meaning made only in hindsight.

### 6. Reluctant guardian to a psychic thirteen-year-old — level: stance / relationship engine
- What it is: The narrator takes charge of Yuki, a thirteen-year-old whose famous photographer mother has abandoned her, first out of awkwardness and then as a paid companion. He drives her around, shares oldies with her, flies with her to Hawaii, and lectures her about fairness to the dead. Yuki can see things. She senses where he has been, and she is the one who sees, while watching the film, that Gotanda killed the woman in the bed scene. She gives the mystery its key and gives the narrator someone to be responsible for.
- Why it is specific to this work: In no other Murakami novel is the narrator's main companion a child he must parent, and here that child is also the medium through which the hidden truth arrives.
- In-text refs: [Dance Dance Dance p.107], [Dance Dance Dance p.186], [Dance Dance Dance p.247], [Dance Dance Dance p.280], [Dance Dance Dance p.310]
- External: http://www.exorcising-ghosts.co.uk/dance.html — the publisher's blurb names a psychic 13-year-old dropout among the core cast. https://panoramajournal.org/issues/issue-16-encounters/encounters-the-ghosts-of-capitalism-a-review-of-murakamis-dance-dance-dance/ — describes Yuki as precocious, psychic and neglected by her mother.

## Weak candidates
- The uncanny told first as hearsay and then lived: Yumiyoshi tells of stepping out of the lift into a black, silent, moldy sixteenth floor, and the narrator later retraces it beat by beat [Dance Dance Dance p.44], [Dance Dance Dance p.74]. It is a strong technique, but it serves strand 1 (the hidden floor) rather than standing as its own engine.
- The comic police pair Fisherman and Bookish, and Mei's murder investigation [Dance Dance Dance p.153], [Dance Dance Dance p.165], [Dance Dance Dance p.324]. This is a memorable set piece and the source of the mock-detective feel, but it is an episode rather than a defining mechanism. external: none found that treats it as central (the Panorama review mentions the pair only in passing).
- Music as constant soundtrack. Music is everywhere and ties to the dance command, but heavy pop and jazz listing is a general Murakami habit. Only the Sheep Man's rule makes it specific, and that is already strand 2.
- Writing as healing: Murakami reportedly said writing this book was a healing act after his fame from Norwegian Wood and that he enjoyed it more than any other (Rubin, Haruki Murakami and the Music of Words, as cited at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_Dance_Dance_(novel); I could not see the primary text). This is relevant context for the narrator's recovery arc, but it is about the author, not a strand of the text.

## Why 6 strands
The novel stands on a sequel premise (going back to the hotel) and one governing rule (dance to stay connected). Three engines drive its plot and meaning: the bubble-economy critique, Gotanda as double and film clue, and Yuki as ward and medium. The six-skeleton death count holds them together. Everything else in the notes is technique serving these six, not a separate mechanism.
# DNA: Hard-Boiled Wonderland and the End of the World (1985 Japanese, Shinchosha; 1991 English, tr. Alfred Birnbaum — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard-Boiled_Wonderland_and_the_End_of_the_World)
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
Two stories run side by side in alternating chapters: a wisecracking data-encryption agent (a Calcutec) caught in a near-future Tokyo spy war, and a nameless newcomer in a silent walled Town where he must give up his shadow and spend his evenings reading old dreams out of unicorn skulls. Midway the novel reveals that the Town is the Calcutec's own core consciousness, edited and wired into his brain by a rogue scientist, and that in a fixed number of hours his waking mind will drop into it for good. From then on the Tokyo half is a countdown to the end of a self, and the Town half becomes a question of whether to escape with the shadow or remain in a perfect, mindless place that he himself made. The book runs on that double pressure: one man spending his last day on small pleasures, and his inner self deciding what kind of eternity to accept.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. Two alternating worlds that turn out to be one mind — level: structure
- What it is: Odd chapters are the Tokyo thriller, even chapters are the Town; they alternate strictly and at first seem unrelated. The Professor's explanation reveals the Town as the visual rendering of the narrator's own core consciousness, so the two threads are the outer and inner side of the same man. They converge at the end, when the Tokyo narrator falls asleep and the Town narrator makes his final choice.
- Why it is specific to this work: The alternation is not two characters or two eras (as in later Murakami) but one consciousness seen from outside and from inside, with the hinge explained in-story as a brain experiment.
- In-text refs: [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.8] (first Tokyo chapter opens), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.16] (Tokyo chapter ends, first Town chapter begins on the same page), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.225] (Professor says the world that will end is the one inside his mind), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.295] (the narrator describes his core as a walled town with a river), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.326] (the shadow says the narrator created the whole Town)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard-Boiled_Wonderland_and_the_End_of_the_World — odd chapters are Hard-Boiled Wonderland, even chapters The End of the World, two alternating narratives. Also https://lrb.co.uk/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — the fantasy is revealed as the Calcutec's inner drama, and the two narratives converge.

### 2. One narrator, two voices: hard-boiled past tense vs. hushed present tense — level: stance
- What it is: Both halves are "I", but the Tokyo chapters use a quick, joking, brand-and-movie-filled past tense in the manner of a detective novel, while the Town chapters use a slow, formal present tense with capitalized common nouns (the Town, the Wall, the Library, the Gatekeeper) and almost no proper names. Even chapter titles differ: Tokyo chapters carry a list of three concrete nouns, Town chapters a single place or image.
- Why it is specific to this work: The voice split is the form of the book's idea (outer, ironic, noisy self vs. inner, timeless, mindless self), and in Japanese it was marked by two different first-person pronouns.
- In-text refs: [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.8] (three-noun title, joking inventory of the elevator), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.16-17] (switch to the present-tense Town chapter on the golden beasts), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.36-37] (Professor's banter cut to a formal description of the Town's plazas and Library)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard-Boiled_Wonderland_and_the_End_of_the_World — formal watashi in Hard-Boiled Wonderland vs. intimate boku in End of the World; Birnbaum used present tense for the Town to reproduce the effect. Also https://lrb.co.uk/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — the Calcutec meets bizarre events with hard-boiled cool.

### 3. Countdown to the end of consciousness — level: premise
- What it is: The Professor has installed a third "circuit" holding an edited version of the narrator's core consciousness; once it is engaged it cannot be switched back, and within a set number of hours his mind will move into that inner world permanently. The narrator converts the deadline into everyday units (shaves, morning papers) and spends the final day on laundry, a rental car, cassettes, a meal, a night with the librarian, and then falls asleep in the car by the sea.
- Why it is specific to this work: The "end of the world" of the title is literally the end of one man's waking self on a clock, and it turns the thriller into a last-day-of-life novel.
- In-text refs: [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.167] (thirty-six hours left, measured in domestic repetitions), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.225] (no override for the third circuit), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.227] (he will be stuck there with no return), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.279] (last-day errands: cleaners, cigarettes, cakes, video shop), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.324] (falling asleep in the parked car with Dylan on repeat)
- External: https://lrb.co.uk/v14/n02/julian-loose/sheeped — the rewired Calcutec has a fixed time left and spends it on ordinary things before his one-way trip into the inner world.

### 4. The shadow cut away: mind as the price of a perfect town — level: theme
- What it is: To enter the Town the narrator must have his shadow severed, and with it his memory and, eventually, his mind. The Town is calm and perfect because its people have no mind; the beasts absorb what mind leaks out and carry it beyond the Wall, dying of its weight each winter. The shadow keeps pushing for escape, the narrator falls for the mindless Librarian, and at the end he lets the shadow leave through the Pool while he stays, accepting responsibility for the world he made and choosing the Woods.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole moral argument of the book (peace without self vs. painful selfhood) is carried by the literal separation of a man from his shadow, an element Murakami says came from his unpublished 1980 story and stayed with him for decades.
- In-text refs: [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.58] (the shadow is cut off and protests that people cannot live without shadows), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.59] (the fable-like line closing the loss of the shadow), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.276] (shadow explains that the beasts carry mind out and die of it, the price of perfection), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.326-327] (he refuses to leave; the shadow dives into the Pool alone)
- External: https://www.canberratimes.com.au/story/8164027/like-a-fishbone-stuck-in-the-throat/ — Murakami says the wall and the shadow were the key elements of the 1980 story that fed into this 1985 novel. Also https://nippon.com/en/japan-topics/bg900467 — in the original story residents have no shadows and newcomers must have theirs removed. Also https://jbvelasquez.substack.com/p/reading-dreams-from-unicorn-skulls — reads the ending as a choice between the Librarian and the escaping Shadow, left open.

### 5. Dreamreading unicorn skulls — level: device
- What it is: The Gatekeeper marks the narrator's eyes so he cannot bear daylight, and he is sent to the Library to read old dreams from rows of unicorn skulls, which hold scattered fragments of the townspeople's minds. In Tokyo the Professor gives him a unicorn skull and the librarian reads him a mock-scholarly history of unicorns. The device pays off when music makes the skulls glow and he can feel a trace of the Librarian's mind in them.
- Why it is specific to this work: The skulls are the book's own invented object, the physical place where lost mind is stored, and they appear in both halves.
- In-text refs: [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.37] (told to go to the Library to read old dreams), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.39] (eyes cut, he loses the light of day and enters the Library), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.289] (old dreams are mind the beasts breathed in, scattered among many skulls), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.303] (the skulls light up and he senses her mind)
- External: https://jbvelasquez.substack.com/p/reading-dreams-from-unicorn-skulls — the narrator works as a dreamreader extracting dreams from the Library's unicorn skulls.
