Also https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/hard-boiled-wonderland-and-end-world-haruki-murakami — each narrator has a unicorn skull and a librarian.

### 6. Objects that leak across the two worlds — level: device
- What it is: Small things appear in both threads before the reader knows why: paperclips (on the Town Library counter and everywhere in Tokyo), unicorn skulls, walls, two librarians, and the song Danny Boy. The Tokyo narrator notices the repeats as irritating little alarms and some get a comically practical explanation (paperclips as cheap repellent against the INKlings). Danny Boy is the climax of this device: sung along to a record on the last night in Tokyo, then slowly recovered note by note on an accordion in the Town, where it wakes the whole Town and the skulls.
- Why it is specific to this work: The leaks are how the reader is trained to feel the two worlds are one mind before the plot confirms it, and the song is the only thing that fully crosses.
- In-text refs: [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.39] (paperclips on the Town Library counter), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.68] (paperclips everywhere in Tokyo; the narrator notes a pattern after skulls), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.240] (Professor explains the paperclips), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.300] (Danny Boy sung along with Bing Crosby), [Hard-Boiled Wonderland p.303] (Danny Boy found on the accordion; the Town comes alive)
- External: https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/hard-boiled-wonderland-and-end-world-haruki-murakami — the two halves mirror each other (skull and librarian in each) and comment on each other. No external source found that discusses the paperclips or Danny Boy specifically.

## Weak candidates
- The underground journey through INKling tunnels (Tokyo chapters, roughly p.170-250): a long, central set piece, but it reads more as the thriller's action engine than a defining mechanism; it supports strand 3 rather than standing alone. [checked p.178]
- The uncanny handled as paperwork (the granddaughter left sound-removed, bureaucratic non-answers): strong in this book [p.36], but it is a general Murakami habit rather than specific to this work.
- Building a map of the Town for the shadow [p.93, p.101]: a recurring task that drives the Town plot, but it is a sub-part of strand 4 (the shadow's escape plan).
- Murakami's Paris Review remark that his style is close to this book (https://www.theparisreview.org/interviews/2/the-art-of-fiction-no-182-haruki-murakami) concerns style in general, not a specific strand, so it is not used as support.

## Why 6 strands
The novel is built from two linked inventions (a brain-experiment countdown and a walled town without shadows) delivered through a strict alternating form and a split voice; the skulls and the cross-world leaks are the two devices that bind them. The last-day errands and the ending choice are kept inside strands 3 and 4 because they resolve those strands rather than adding new engines.
# DNA: Kafka on the Shore (2002 in Japanese as Umibe no Kafuka; English translation by Philip Gabriel, 2005 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafka_on_the_Shore)
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A fifteen-year-old runs away from home to outrun a curse his father laid on him: that he will kill his father and sleep with his mother and his sister. The harder he runs, the more the curse comes true, but sideways. His father is stabbed in Tokyo by a simple-minded old man, Nakata, while the boy, hundreds of kilometres away in Takamatsu, wakes up with someone's blood on his shirt and no memory. The book splits this one fate across two threads that never quite meet. The boy's thread is told in first person, present tense. The old man's thread is told in third person, past tense, and is mixed with official documents. What joins the two threads is not the plot but objects and passages: a pop song and a painting both called "Kafka on the Shore," and a stone that opens a way into another world. Over all of it hangs one question: are you guilty of what you did in a dream, or in a metaphor? An inner voice, the boy named Crow, coaches the boy in second person from the first page to the last.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. The Oedipal curse as runaway engine — level: premise
- What it is: The boy's father, a famous sculptor, has told him he will kill his father and be with his mother and his older sister. The whole journey is an attempt to escape that prophecy. Every step away from it brings him closer, as in the Greek original. He picks up the photo of himself and his sister and the father's things on the way out, and the curse is set up as something built into his genes.
- Why it is specific to this work: no other Murakami novel is built on a declared mythic prophecy that the hero knowingly flees and partly fulfills.
- In-text refs: [Kafka on the Shore p.8] (leaving home with items from his father's study, the photo of himself and his sister), [Kafka on the Shore p.193] (he tells Oshima the prophecy and says his father called it unavoidable, like a timer in his genes), [Kafka on the Shore p.187] (newspaper report that the father has been stabbed to death and that the 15-year-old son is missing)
- External: https://www.bookbrowse.com/author_interviews/full/index.cfm?author_number=1103 — Murakami says the Oedipus myth is only one of several motifs. His starting idea was a fifteen-year-old fleeing a sinister father to look for his mother, and the Oedipal link came after that. External also: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/118718/kafka-on-the-shore-by-haruki-murakami/9781400079278/readers-guide/ — the publisher's guide frames the book around how his attempts to escape the fate draw him toward it.

### 2. Two parallel threads, one fate split between them — level: structure
- What it is: Chapters alternate between Kafka (first person, present tense) and Nakata, an old man who can talk to cats but cannot read (third person, past tense). The two threads never share a scene. They converge on the same places and acts, and Nakata ends up doing what the prophecy assigned to Kafka. Each thread ends in Takamatsu, around the same library and the same woman, without the two leads ever meeting.
- Why it is specific to this work: unlike the separate worlds of Hard-Boiled Wonderland, here the alternation divides one person's destiny between two bodies, so that one man's act is the other's guilt.
- In-text refs: [Kafka on the Shore p.8] (Chapter 1: first-person present), [Kafka on the Shore p.45] (Chapter 6: third-person past, Nakata chatting with a cat), [Kafka on the Shore p.368] (Nakata tells Miss Saeki he killed a man in Nakano because he took the place of the fifteen-year-old boy who should have been there)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafka_on_the_Shore — odd-numbered chapters follow Kafka and even-numbered chapters follow Nakata. https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/kafkaots.htm — notes that the two lines converge without fully merging, and cites Steven Kellman calling the "crosscutting structure" closer to Bulgakov than to Kafka.

### 3. "In dreams begin responsibilities": guilt for a crime you did not physically commit — level: theme
- What it is: Kafka blacks out, wakes behind a shrine with fresh blood on his shirt, and later learns his father was killed that same night in Tokyo. A pencil note by Oshima in a book about Eichmann quotes Yeats' line that responsibility begins in dreams. Kafka takes it to heart: he may have killed his father through some dream circuit, not with his hands. Oshima says no court would hold him responsible, and Kafka replies that he is not talking about law. The book keeps testing where the line runs between literal and metaphorical acts.
- Why it is specific to this work: the question of moral responsibility for a dream-murder that is also literally carried out by someone else is the novel's central knot, and it is spelled out on the page through Yeats and Eichmann.
- In-text refs: [Kafka on the Shore p.67] (blood on the white T-shirt, washed in a shrine restroom), [Kafka on the Shore p.127] (Oshima's note on Yeats and Eichmann; Kafka imagines being tried for the blood), [Kafka on the Shore p.194] (Kafka tells Oshima he may have killed his father not metaphorically but through a dream; Oshima answers with science and law), [Kafka on the Shore p.142] (Nakata stabs Johnnie Walker and ends up soaked in blood)
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=334 — Glynne Walley makes the Yeats line central to agency in the book, and reads Nakata as the vessel through which the father-killing half of the prophecy is carried out.

### 4. The boy named Crow: a second-person inner voice that frames the book — level: stance
- What it is: The book opens and closes with "the boy named Crow," a tougher alter ego who talks to Kafka, pushes him, and slips into addressing him as "you" (the sandstorm-of-fate passage, the pep talks, the final lines). Inside the first-person chapters, the narration slides into "you" at moments of panic and guilt. In one separate interchapter, Crow appears as an actual bird and attacks the man in the silk hat in a limbo between worlds. "Kafka" is Czech for a crow-like bird, so the narrator and his voice share a name.
- Why it is specific to this work: the double stance of an "I" narrator and a "you" voice named after the narrator's own alias is the frame of this novel alone, with its own titled chapters.
- In-text refs: [Kafka on the Shore p.4] (opening chapter "The Boy Named Crow"), [Kafka on the Shore p.5] (Crow's sandstorm-of-fate game), [Kafka on the Shore p.68] (narration shifts into a second-person voice after the blood), [Kafka on the Shore p.407] (Crow as a bird confronting the man in the silk hat), [Kafka on the Shore p.440], [Kafka on the Shore p.441] (Crow's last words and the closing second-person sentences)
- External: https://journals.library.brandeis.edu/index.php/PAJLS/article/download/1667/1055/3660 — Michele Eduarda Brasil de Sá ("Fate, Transmission and the Boy Named Crow," PAJLS 20, 2019) reads Crow first as an alter ego and then as a messenger between the two protagonists, and treats his second-person closing lines as a kind of spell.

### 5. "Kafka on the Shore" — a song and a painting that freeze one woman's past — level: device
- What it is: The title belongs inside the book, to two objects. One is a 1970-era hit single that Miss Saeki wrote and sang at nineteen, her only song, with surreal lyrics and two odd chords. The other is a painting of a boy by the sea that hangs in Kafka's room at the library. Her fifteen-year-old self appears at night as a living ghost, gazing at the painting. Kafka decodes the lyrics against his own life, and at the end carries the painting home with him.
- Why it is specific to this work: the novel names itself after an in-world artwork that is both a clue machine (lyrics that foreshadow the plot) and the anchor of a stopped clock (Saeki's frozen youth).
- In-text refs: [Kafka on the Shore p.151] (Oshima explains the million-selling single, around 1970, and that it shares Kafka's name), [Kafka on the Shore p.216] (Kafka plays the record three times and is unsettled by the two unusual chords), [Kafka on the Shore p.228] (the young Saeki at 3 a.m. gazing at the painting), [Kafka on the Shore p.440] (the wrapped painting at his feet on the bullet train)
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=334 — Walley notes the song was named after the painting in her dead lover's room, and that she has lived on its royalties; he sees it as the link between the painting, Kafka's identity and Saeki's lost youth. Also https://www.bookbrowse.com/author_interviews/full/index.cfm?author_number=1103 — Murakami says he does not know what the lyrics mean.

### 6. The entrance stone and the world beyond the forest — level: device
- What it is: A round stone, once opened by Miss Saeki long ago, is opened again by Nakata (with Hoshino's help) and must be closed after he dies. A slug-like white creature tries to crawl in, and Hoshino kills it. On Kafka's side, two WWII soldiers who deserted decades ago guide him through the forest into a small, timeless town where the dead or half-dead live. There he meets Saeki one last time and is sent back to live. Nakata and Saeki each have only half a shadow, the price of having crossed over.
- Why it is specific to this work: other Murakami books have wells and underworlds, but here the passage is a physical stone that two separate threads open, use and seal, and that makes the split plot work.
- In-text refs: [Kafka on the Shore p.247] (Hoshino tells Colonel Sanders he is looking for an entrance stone), [Kafka on the Shore p.368] (Nakata and Saeki discuss opening the stone; both have half a shadow), [Kafka on the Shore p.393] (the soldiers show Kafka the hidden town in the basin), [Kafka on the Shore p.427] (Hoshino attacks the white creature heading for the stone)
- External: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/118718/kafka-on-the-shore-by-haruki-murakami/9781400079278/readers-guide/ — the guide describes Nakata's quest for a stone whose meaning he does not understand, a mountain refuge where ordinary time does not apply, and Colonel Sanders' job of checking how different worlds line up.

## Weak candidates
- Official documents as a narrative frame (the declassified 1946 U.S. Army report on the 1944 Rice Bowl Hill incident, the teacher's later letter, the newspaper article): distinctive and structural [Kafka on the Shore p.14], [Kafka on the Shore p.60], [Kafka on the Shore p.187]. It serves strands 2 and 6, though, by explaining how Nakata was emptied and keeping the even thread at a distance, so it may be part of the structure rather than its own strand. External: Murakami declined to say whether the investigations draw on real events (https://www.bookbrowse.com/author_interviews/full/index.cfm?author_number=1103).
- Metaphor as an open subject (characters keep saying that things are metaphors, or are not) [Kafka on the Shore p.56], [Kafka on the Shore p.393], [Kafka on the Shore p.417]: strong and recurring, but folded into strand 3 as the literal-vs-metaphorical guilt question.
- Hoshino's awakening through Beethoven's Archduke Trio (the truck driver who becomes the second protagonist of the Nakata thread; per the reading notes, around pp. 305-310 and 389): memorable and specific, but it belongs to the side plot, not the engine. Pages not reopened for this pass.
- The private library as sanctuary (Komura Memorial Library, Oshima as mentor): central setting, but a library as refuge is a recurring Murakami place, and its role here overlaps with strand 5.

## Why 6 strands
The novel's identity rests on one premise (the curse) and one moral question (dream-guilt). These are delivered through a split two-thread structure with a second-person inner voice, and held together by two signature devices: the title song and painting, and the entrance stone. Removing any one of the six would make it a different book. The other candidates either serve these six or are general Murakami habits.
# DNA: Killing Commendatore (2017 in Japan, two volumes from Shinchosha; English 2018, tr. Philip Gabriel and Ted Goossen — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_Commendatore ; Japanese 2017 / English 2018 also confirmed on the copyright page [Killing Commendatore p.4])
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A portrait painter in his mid-thirties, left by his wife and unable to paint, house-sits the mountain home of a famous Japanese-style painter who is now old and has dementia. In the attic he finds a hidden painting that sets Mozart's Don Giovanni in ancient Japan. Opening a stone pit behind a small shrine releases a bell, then an "Idea" that borrows the shape of the painted Commendatore. From there, three hidden things press into the story together: his rich neighbour Menshiki's possible daughter, watched across the valley; the old painter's buried war history (Vienna 1938, his brother at Nanjing); and the narrator's own dark side and his dead sister. The only way through is for the narrator to make paintings and then to make a literal descent. He has to kill the Idea and crawl through a land of Metaphors and a narrow tunnel to bring a missing girl back. The whole thing is narrated years later by a man who now has a daughter he believes in without needing proof.

## DNA strands (7)

### 1. The hidden painting that comes alive — level: premise / device
- What it is: The novel's title belongs to a painting inside the novel. Tomohiko Amada's canvas, wrapped and hidden in the attic, moves the opening murder of Don Giovanni into Asuka-period Japan and adds a figure who isn't in the opera: a long-faced man peering out of a square hole in the ground. The painted figures then leave the canvas and enter the plot one by one: the Commendatore sits on the sofa, Long Face guards the underworld, and Donna Anna guides the narrator. In the climax the narrator re-enacts the painted killing.
- Why it is specific to this work: no other Murakami novel is built so that one picture is at once the plot's source, its cast list and its final action.
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.71] (the wrapped painting with its title tag found in the attic), [Killing Commendatore p.74], [Killing Commendatore p.75] (the composition, including the long-faced figure in the hole whose lid matches the attic hatch), [Killing Commendatore p.76], [Killing Commendatore p.77] (he identifies the scene as Don Giovanni's opening), [Killing Commendatore p.246], [Killing Commendatore p.248] (the Commendatore appears, explains he has only borrowed the painted shape, and that the canvas is unchanged)
- External: https://worldliteraturetoday.org/2018/november/killing-commendatore-haruki-murakami — Papinchak (World Literature Today) says the attic canvas, a Don Giovanni scene, holds the plot together, and that the two-foot Commendatore steps out of it.

### 2. Ideas and Metaphors as literal beings — level: device
- What it is: The book's two halves are named for its two invisible categories, the Idea made visible and the shifting Metaphor. An Idea (the Commendatore) can only take a shape it borrows and must be invited in. A Metaphor (Long Face) is a timid functionary who links things to words. Beneath both lurk Double Metaphors, predators that live inside the narrator and feed on his true feelings. The narrator links that inner darkness to the man with the white Subaru Forester, whose portrait he has to leave unfinished.
- Why it is specific to this work: other novels have spirit-guides, but only here are they given a philosophical taxonomy (Idea, Metaphor, Double Metaphor) that also names the parts of the book.
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.8] and [Killing Commendatore p.359] (part titles), [Killing Commendatore p.249] (the Commendatore says he is not a spirit but an Idea, with limits on the forms he can take), [Killing Commendatore p.584] (Long Face calls himself a Metaphor, describes the Path of Metaphor and warns of Double Metaphors), [Killing Commendatore p.611] (Donna Anna says Double Metaphors live within him, and he thinks of the Subaru man), [Killing Commendatore p.657] (he tells Mariye that the Subaru man may be a borrowed form, or a Double Metaphor, and that the painting must stay unfinished)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_Commendatore — the two Japanese volumes are titled The Idea Made Visible and The Shifting Metaphor.

### 3. A blocked portraitist who paints his way through — level: premise / theme
- What it is: The narrator is a commercial portrait painter who, once alone, faces a blank canvas he can't fill. The plot moves forward through the paintings he goes on to make. The Menshiki portrait catches the man's hidden nature rather than his face. The Subaru Forester man brings up his own violence. Mariye's portrait gathers his lost sister and his wife into one face. Painting is how hidden truths come out, and the book treats a finished painting as something that knows more than its maker does.
- Why it is specific to this work: it is Murakami's only novel with a painter narrator, and the creative process itself, here in pictures rather than words, is what drives the plot.
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.53] (the blank canvas and his inability to begin), [Killing Commendatore p.203] (the Menshiki painting turns out to be no portrait but a work he made for himself, beyond what he understands), [Killing Commendatore p.490] (his dead sister and his ex-wife slip into Mariye's portrait), [Killing Commendatore p.657] (the Subaru painting is turned to face the wall)
- External: https://www.thejakartapost.com/life/2018/12/03/killing-commendatore-haruki-murakami-devastation-and-transformation — Partogi (Jakarta Post) calls art a central theme, with a painter stuck on commissions whose creative drive comes back after he sees the hidden painting.

### 4. War history buried inside the art — level: theme
- What it is: The hidden painting covers up real historical violence. In Vienna after the Anschluss, Amada took part in a failed student plot against a Nazi official. His younger brother, a gentle pianist, was drafted, sent to the Nanjing campaign, ordered to behead prisoners, and killed himself when he came home, and the family hid the suicide. When the narrator kills the Commendatore at Amada's deathbed, he asks whether he is repeating the order given at Nanjing. He wonders whose death the old man is really watching.
- Why it is specific to this work: the novel ties its central fantasy act directly to 1938 Vienna and the Nanjing massacre, rather than to Manchukuo (Wind-Up Bird) or a generic past.
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.407] (Menshiki sets out 1938: the Anschluss, Kristallnacht, the fall of Nanjing), [Killing Commendatore p.418], [Killing Commendatore p.419] (Masahiko tells of his uncle's war, the censored letters, and the hidden suicide), [Killing Commendatore p.574], [Killing Commendatore p.576] (the killing, framed against the Nanjing order and the Vienna plot)
- External: https://newsinfo.inquirer.net/885823/murakami-calls-for-fight-against-history-revisionism — Murakami (AFP, from a Mainichi interview, April 2017) explained why the book touches the Holocaust and Nanjing: "history is the collective memory of a nation." Also https://lists.osu.edu/pipermail/mclc/2017-March/005737.html — Global Times report on the Nanjing passage and the reaction in China.

### 5. Fatherhood that can't be verified: believing instead of testing — level: theme
- What it is: The novel sets up two paternity puzzles side by side. Menshiki bought a mansion across the valley to watch through binoculars a girl who may be his daughter, and he refuses to find out. The narrator's estranged wife falls pregnant at a time when he can't biologically be the father. The book then suggests he fathered the child in a dream, as an Idea or a Metaphor. In the epilogue he refuses a DNA test as well. Unlike Menshiki, he says, he does not need to balance between the two possibilities, because he is able to believe.
- Why it is specific to this work: the Gatsby-style watcher across the water is turned to a question of paternity, and the book's closing claim is about choosing to believe rather than about knowing.
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.290] (Menshiki shows the binoculars and the girl's house), [Killing Commendatore p.296] (he says he could test, but doesn't want to), [Killing Commendatore p.474] (Yuzu is seven months pregnant and he can't be the father), [Killing Commendatore p.711] (Yuzu herself is unsure who the father is), [Killing Commendatore p.721], [Killing Commendatore p.722] (no DNA test, the dream-conception, his capacity to believe set against Menshiki's balancing)
- External: https://worldliteraturetoday.org/2018/november/killing-commendatore-haruki-murakami — Papinchak calls Menshiki the narrator's Jay Gatsby, the man across the valley watching a teenage girl through special binoculars. Also https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/killing_commendatore.htm — the summary notes that Menshiki has no interest in confirming that he is Mariye's father and prefers not knowing.

### 6. The pit, the bell and the narrow passage: a lost sister and claustrophobia — level: device / theme
- What it is: A stone chamber behind the shrine, which a bell rings out of at night, is opened with a backhoe. All that is inside is the bell, and its opening is what lets the Idea into the house. The pit echoes an Edo-period tale by Ueda Akinari that the narrator reads, about a monk dug up while still ringing a gong. Under this lies the narrator's childhood memory of his sister Komi crawling into a cave hole on a trip. He came to believe her life was taken there, two years before her heart killed her, and since then he has had claustrophobia. To save Mariye, who is close to Komi's age, he has to crawl through a tunnel too small for him, with Komi's voice guiding him, and he comes out in the same pit.
- Why it is specific to this work: unlike the generic Murakami well, this hole is a man-made, sealed, bell-ringing chamber. It is also wired to one particular grief, the lost sister and the narrow space, and the climax runs it in reverse (he gets in by the tunnel and out by the pit).
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.168] (the Ueda Akinari story mirrors the night gong), [Killing Commendatore p.179], [Killing Commendatore p.180] (the opened chamber, empty except for the bell), [Killing Commendatore p.250] (the Idea could enter only after the pit was opened and the bell taken out), [Killing Commendatore p.268] (Komi at the cave, his belief that she was lost there), [Killing Commendatore p.612] (Komi's voice in the tunnel), [Killing Commendatore p.617] (he finds himself in the pit behind the shrine), [Killing Commendatore p.635] (Donna Anna and Komi helped him past his claustrophobia, perhaps as one figure)
- External: https://booksonasia.net/2019/04/29/killing-commendatore-a-self-portrait-of-murakamis-literary-landscape/ — Lucas-Hall (Books on Asia) reads the pit and bell as darkness and confinement, and calls the narrator's claustrophobia integral to the plot. https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/killing_commendatore.htm notes that Komi died at twelve and that Mariye is close to that age.

### 7. Told from years later: a nine-month bracket closed by 3/11 — level: stance / structure
- What it is: The narrator tells everything in retrospect. The prologue jumps ahead to a later visit from the faceless man. The first chapter announces at once that he lived on the mountain from May until early the next year and later went back to his wife. Chapters end with hints from that future vantage. The epilogue skips several years ahead to the March 2011 earthquake and tsunami striking the coast he had once wandered, and ends with him walking his small daughter home. So the story is told from a settled point, and the uncanny months are framed as a nine-month gestation that is already over.
- Why it is specific to this work: the time bracket is an actual pregnancy term and ends in a real public disaster, both of which set how every strange event in between is read.
- In-text refs: [Killing Commendatore p.9], [Killing Commendatore p.11] (the prologue, with the promise to someday paint the faceless man), [Killing Commendatore p.13] (the separation and reconciliation announced up front), [Killing Commendatore p.51] (a hindsight note that the painting would change his world), [Killing Commendatore p.715], [Killing Commendatore p.716] (the 3/11 epilogue and Muro)
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/killing_commendatore.htm — cites Daniel Morales (Japan Times) noting that the book is told in retrospect from a future vantage. https://worldliteraturetoday.org/2018/november/killing-commendatore-haruki-murakami — Papinchak notes the narration is retrospective and that a nine-month gestation period frames the story.

## Weak candidates
- Don Giovanni and opera as a scaffold (Rosenkavalier as Menshiki's clock, the Commendatore statue coming to dinner): very present, but its central use is already captured by strand 1. Outside the painting, the music works more as texture than as engine (see the music lists in the notes).
- The Great Gatsby as a separate strand: reviewers stress it, and the house across the water is real [Killing Commendatore p.290], but in the book it serves the paternity theme rather than standing alone, so it is folded into strand 5.
- The faceless man and the penguin charm: they frame the book [Killing Commendatore p.9], [Killing Commendatore p.721], but they act more as a bookend motif than as an independent mechanism. One could argue for them as a device, but here they are treated as part of strands 2 and 7.
- The man with the white Subaru Forester (the narrator's violence, the night in the love hotel): important, but the book itself names him as a Double Metaphor and an unfinishable painting, so he is folded into strands 2 and 3.

## Why 7 strands
This is a 700-page, two-part novel that runs several separate engines at once (a living painting, the Idea/Metaphor system, the painter's process, buried war history, unknowable paternity, the pit and the sister, and a retrospective frame), and each one is set up on its own pages and pays off in the climax or epilogue. Merging any two would lose a mechanism readers of this book would recognize, and the leftover motifs were demoted to weak candidates rather than counted.
# DNA: Norwegian Wood (1987, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Wood_(novel))
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A 37-year-old man hears a soft orchestral cover of the Beatles song on a plane landing in Hamburg, and it knocks him back to 1968-70 in Tokyo. He writes the book to keep a promise he made to Naoko never to forget her, and he admits the memory is already fading. The story he recovers is a love triangle in which one corner is already dead. Kizuki, his best friend and Naoko's first love, kills himself at seventeen. That death teaches Watanabe that death sits inside life, not across from it. Naoko is bound to Kizuki and withdraws into a mountain sanatorium that is sealed off from the world. Midori, loud and physical and alive, pulls him toward the future. The novel is Murakami's one deliberate attempt at full realism, so the uncanny survives only in small cracks: a well that may not exist, a night visit that may be a dream, and a last phone call from a place that is no place.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. The promise-to-remember frame (song-triggered, fading memory) — level: stance
- What it is: The whole novel is a memoir written by Watanabe at 37, eighteen years after the events. A piece of music sets it off on a plane. He says outright that he writes in order to understand, and to keep a vow to Naoko that he would never forget her, while he watches her face take longer and longer to come back. The opening chapter ends by turning that tender promise against itself: he concludes that she never loved him.
- Why it is specific to this work: In other Murakami novels the first-person narrator usually tells events as they happen. Here the act of remembering, and the fear of forgetting, is the reason the book exists and its subject.
- In-text refs: [Norwegian Wood p.2], [Norwegian Wood p.3], [Norwegian Wood p.4], [Norwegian Wood p.7]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Wood_(novel) — says the orchestral cover heard on landing in Hamburg sets off the memories, and names nostalgia and loss as central themes. Also https://www.litcharts.com/lit/norwegian-wood/themes — treats memory, nostalgia and regret, triggered by a song on a plane, as a main theme.

### 2. Death as part of life — level: theme
- What it is: Kizuki's suicide comes without warning, right after an ordinary afternoon of pool. It leaves Watanabe with a knot inside him that later hardens into one conviction: death is not the opposite of life but part of it. The book keeps testing that conviction against a chain of suicides: Kizuki, Naoko's sister, Hatsumi (told in a flash-forward) and finally Naoko. After Naoko's death he finds that the conviction cannot heal the grief, and he imagines a place where he lives with the dead.
- Why it is specific to this work: Here the theme is stated as a philosophy inside the book and then revised under pressure. It is the moral spine of the plot, not just a mood.
- In-text refs: [Norwegian Wood p.18], [Norwegian Wood p.19], [Norwegian Wood p.209], [Norwegian Wood p.212]
- External: https://windupbird.substack.com/p/traumatic-realism-norwegian-wood — David S. Wills reads the novel as trauma passed on in cycles through deaths, suicides and abandonments. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Wood_(novel) — lists death and suicide as forces shaping the characters' lives.

### 3. Two women, two worlds (a triangle with a dead third) — level: premise
- What it is: Watanabe's love is split between Naoko and Midori. Naoko is still tied to the dead Kizuki, whom she grew up with, and she cannot say she loves Watanabe. Midori insists on her own living, bodily presence and demands that he choose her alone. In his own letter Watanabe describes the first love as quiet and transparent and the second as something that breathes and drags him into the future. The plot is the slow, guilty move from one to the other.
- Why it is specific to this work: The love-choice is also a choice between the world of the dead and the world of the living. The rival for Naoko is a dead boy, and Watanabe ends up handing her back to him.
- In-text refs: [Norwegian Wood p.88], [Norwegian Wood p.202], [Norwegian Wood p.206], [Norwegian Wood p.212]
- External: https://www.litcharts.com/lit/norwegian-wood/themes — frames Toru as torn between Naoko from his troubled past and the lively Midori. https://windupbird.substack.com/p/traumatic-realism-norwegian-wood — argues that Toru and Midori are resilient enough to endure and Naoko is not.

### 4. Ami Hostel, the sealed sanatorium — level: device (place)
- What it is: Naoko recovers in a mountain sanatorium with its own rules. There are no bars, the gate is open, there is no TV or radio, the residents farm, and patients and staff help each other on equal terms. The one condition is total honesty. Reiko has lived there for seven years and is afraid of the outside. Watanabe's visits feel like crossing into another world, where candlelight, guitar music and moonlit woods replace Tokyo.
- Why it is specific to this work: In a novel committed to realism, the sanatorium plays the role that the other-world plays in Murakami's fantastic novels. It is a gentle place outside time that can heal people but also hold them, and Naoko leaves it only to die.
- In-text refs: [Norwegian Wood p.75], [Norwegian Wood p.76], [Norwegian Wood p.85], [Norwegian Wood p.88]
- External: https://windupbird.substack.com/p/traumatic-realism-norwegian-wood — describes the mental health facility as a protected space set against harsh city life, and suggests that Naoko's talk of the real, outside world plays on that contrast.

### 5. The title song as a lost-in-the-woods motif — level: device
- What it is: The Beatles song "Norwegian Wood" opens the book, as the cover version on the plane. At the sanatorium it is Naoko's favourite, and she has a rule of paying a coin into a cat-shaped bank each time she asks Reiko to play it. She says it makes her picture herself alone in a cold, dark forest with no one coming to rescue her. Naoko later hangs herself in the woods. At the makeshift funeral Reiko and Watanabe hold for her, the song is played again.
- Why it is specific to this work: The song is more than a soundtrack here. It foretells Naoko's death: her private image of being lost in the forest comes true. The Japanese title, which means "Norwegian forest", fixes that image on the cover.
- In-text refs: [Norwegian Wood p.2], [Norwegian Wood p.85], [Norwegian Wood p.212], [Norwegian Wood p.221]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Wood_(novel) — notes that the Japanese title uses mori ("forest") rather than the material wood, that the song is Naoko's favourite, and that it triggers the opening memory.

### 6. A deliberately realist novel with a few uncanny cracks — level: stance
- What it is: Murakami set aside the surreal machinery of his earlier books and wrote a fully realistic coming-of-age love story. The strange survives only in small, unresolved cracks. The field well is described in exact detail, but Watanabe says it may have existed only in Naoko's mind. A night visit may or may not have been a dream. The final scene ends unresolved: Midori asks on the phone where he is, and he cannot say, standing among strangers in a place that seems to be no place.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is the one Murakami novel built as an experiment in realism. Its power comes from keeping the other world mostly out, so the few cracks hit much harder.
- In-text refs: [Norwegian Wood p.4], [Norwegian Wood p.224], [Norwegian Wood p.225], [Norwegian Wood p.226] (translator's note on the book as a deliberate departure)
- External: https://www.theparisreview.org/interviews/2/haruki-murakami-the-art-of-fiction-no-182-haruki-murakami — Murakami says he chose to write a fully realistic book to prove he could: "I wanted to break into the mainstream." https://windupbird.substack.com/p/traumatic-realism-norwegian-wood — calls it his only mainly realist novel, and reads the field well and the placeless ending as its few breaks from realism.

## Weak candidates
- The 1968-69 student strikes as backdrop: Watanabe scorns the strike leaders who return to class as if nothing happened [Norwegian Wood p.37]. Wikipedia and Wills both note that the book treats the movement as hypocritical. It shapes his detachment, but it stays background rather than engine.
- Letters as a second voice: long letters from Naoko and Reiko carry much of the sanatorium plot, and Watanabe's own letter puts the two-women dilemma into words [Norwegian Wood p.206], [Norwegian Wood p.224]. They are important to the structure, but they mainly serve strands 3 and 4. external: none found that treats them as defining.
- The firefly: a gift firefly released on the dorm roof leaves a glow he cannot reach [Norwegian Wood p.37]. Wikipedia says the novel's first section was adapted from the earlier story "Firefly", so the image is the book's seed. In the finished novel, though, it is one set-piece, not a running engine.
- Reiko's story told across several nights, Scheherazade-style, in the sanatorium storehouse (from the notes, p.92 and p.117; not reopened here). It is a strong episode but a subplot.

## Why 6 strands
The novel stands on a memory frame (1), a stated philosophy of death (2) and a two-worlds love triangle (3). Two concrete devices carry those ideas: the sanatorium (4) and the title song (5). The realist stance (6) is what separates it from every other Murakami novel. Everything else in the notes is technique or texture that serves these six.
# DNA: South of the Border, West of the Sun (1992, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_of_the_Border,_West_of_the_Sun ; also https://www.lrb.co.uk/v21/n19/lorna-sage/a-simpler-more-physical-kind-of-empathy)
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
A comfortable, successful man of thirty-seven tells his life in order, from his birth in 1951 through his adolescence to his present as a jazz-bar owner with a wife, two daughters and money supplied by a shady father-in-law. Into that well-furnished but faintly hollow life walks Shimamoto, the only other only child he knew as a boy and the person who once made him feel whole. She will not say where she has been, comes on rainy nights, answers him with hedges, and vanishes again, so the novel becomes a test of whether he will walk away from everything toward her. Behind her stands the other woman from his past, Izumi, whom he damaged as a teenager, and whose blank face finally ends the spell. The two halves of the title give the book its map: one is a childhood dream of a wonderful place over the horizon, the other is the deadly compulsion to walk past the sunset. In the end he falls back into his own ordinary life, emptied out and uncertain, trying to begin again.

## DNA strands (6)

### 1. The returned first love who fills the missing part — level: premise
- What it is: The narrator grows up feeling that being an only child means something is missing in him; the one girl who shared that condition, Shimamoto, seemed to complete him at twelve, and he keeps an empty reserved place for her ever after. When she reappears in his bar decades later, she reawakens the sense that his happy family life lacks something only she can supply, and the book becomes his slide toward choosing her over everything.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole plot is one adult reunion with a childhood soulmate, and the "only child = incomplete person" idea is the explicit mechanism that makes her irreplaceable.
- In-text refs: [South of the Border p.6], [South of the Border p.14], [South of the Border p.67], [South of the Border p.122]
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v21/n19/lorna-sage/a-simpler-more-physical-kind-of-empathy — Sage reads the only-child narrator's lack of conviction as the existential crisis, and Shimamoto as a fatal figure standing for the lives he did not live.

### 2. Chronological self-indictment by a man named "Beginning" — level: stance
- What it is: The story is told in first person by a middle-aged narrator who runs through his life in order (childhood, high school, college, the lost twenties, marriage) in compressed chapters that each open with a verdict on a life stage, before slowing down for the Shimamoto present. He hedges about memory, inserts hindsight judgments against his younger self, and openly concludes that he is a person capable of harming others. His name means "beginning," and the book closes on him resolving to begin again with his wife while unable to get up from the kitchen table.
- Why it is specific to this work: The memoir-like, life-in-sequence shape and the name-to-ending circle (born at the "first" of everything, ending back where he started) are the frame of this novel rather than a general Murakami habit.
- In-text refs: [South of the Border p.5], [South of the Border p.14], [South of the Border p.33], [South of the Border p.143], [South of the Border p.144]
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/southotb.htm — collects reviewers describing the narrator as matter-of-fact, the style as spare, and the book as more introspective and less surreal than his other work.

### 3. Izumi: the damage done just by living — level: theme
- What it is: As a teenager the narrator promises his girlfriend Izumi he will not hurt her, then sleeps with her cousin, and the adult narrator states that this taught him he could wound someone beyond repair. Izumi returns only as rumor (an old classmate refuses to explain why children fear her face) and finally as a face in a taxi window emptied of all expression, which shatters his sense of self and breaks Shimamoto's hold on him.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book is built as a two-woman diptych in which the lost ideal love is answered by a ruined real one, and the climax is not the night with Shimamoto but the silent encounter with Izumi.
- In-text refs: [South of the Border p.20], [South of the Border p.33], [South of the Border p.54], [South of the Border p.137], [South of the Border p.138], [South of the Border p.139]
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v21/n19/lorna-sage/a-simpler-more-physical-kind-of-empathy — Sage notes his unfaithfulness to Izumi while chasing his own dream, and frames Shimamoto as the indirect avenger of that wrong.

### 4. Two elsewheres: south of the border vs. west of the sun — level: device
- What it is: As a boy, listening to a Nat King Cole record at Shimamoto's house, the narrator misunderstands the song "South of the Border" as naming some marvelous place beyond the horizon. As an adult, Shimamoto tells him of a Siberian farmer's illness in which something inside breaks and the farmer walks west past the sunset until he dies, and says that place is different from south of the border. After she disappears, he sorts the whole relationship by this map: hopeful uncertainty belongs to the southern dream, but the western direction admits no maybes and leads to death.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title itself is the book's central metaphor, splitting longing into a childhood fantasy and an adult death-drive.
- In-text refs: [South of the Border p.12], [South of the Border p.120], [South of the Border p.121], [South of the Border p.133]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_of_the_Border,_West_of_the_Sun — explains that the farmer story of hysteria siberiana supplies the "west of the sun" half of the title.

### 5. Shimamoto as an unverifiable presence ("probably," rain, vanishing) — level: device
- What it is: Shimamoto comes on rainy nights, refuses to tell anything about her life, and answers whether he will see her again with "probably" and "for a while," which the two of them later name as their magic words. Each exit leaves only small physical traces (a glass and lipstick-marked cigarette butts) or none at all: after Hakone even the record she gave him is gone. A parallel episode does the same: years earlier he tailed a woman who might have been her, was handed an envelope of cash by a stranger, kept it as proof the event was real, and later finds it has vanished, which sends him into a reflection that reality only holds as a chain of mutual confirmations.
- Why it is specific to this work: Instead of overt fantasy machinery, this novel produces its uncanniness through one woman's withheld story, a recurring hedge word and disappearing evidence, so the reader cannot be sure she was ever fully there.
- In-text refs: [South of the Border p.44], [South of the Border p.67], [South of the Border p.116], [South of the Border p.128], [South of the Border p.136]
- External: https://salon.com/1999/02/24/sneaks_128 — the review raises and leaves open whether the adult Shimamoto is real or a phantom of Hajime's need.

### 6. The borrowed, well-furnished life — level: theme
- What it is: The narrator's success rests on capital and stock tips from a father-in-law whose money system slips through laws and taxes, and he feels he is living someone else's life inside a BMW with Schubert on the stereo, a betrayal of his generation's student-movement refusal of capitalism. His bars are self-built imaginary spaces he sells to customers looking for their own private dream place, and his list of possessions (condo, cottage, two cars, happy family) sits beside the confession that he feels stranded on an airless moon. His wife later reveals that she too threw away dreams and is chased by what she lost.
- Why it is specific to this work: The emptiness here is tied to a precise late-1980s affluence and to money he did not earn, which gives Shimamoto's pull its social as well as romantic charge.
- In-text refs: [South of the Border p.47], [South of the Border p.48], [South of the Border p.49], [South of the Border p.72], [South of the Border p.106], [South of the Border p.143]
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/southotb.htm — summarizes that the bars run on the father-in-law's backing and that he draws Hajime into shady dealings and insider trading.

## Weak candidates
- Origin in The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle drafts: often said, but I could not find a source I opened that confirms it (Wikipedia and the Maynard "lost chapters" paper did not state it), so it is not used. external: none found.
- Shimamoto's lame leg as identity marker: established early and used in the false sighting (the woman drags the opposite leg, and Shimamoto's leg has since been fixed) [South of the Border p.6], [South of the Border p.137]. Real, but it serves strand 5 rather than standing alone.
- Songs as emotional clocks ("Star-Crossed Lovers" played for him, then retired as a beautiful corpse of a song) [South of the Border p.139]: vivid, but music as backdrop is a general Murakami habit; the title songs are covered in strand 4.
- The closing image of rain on an empty sea with no one to see it [South of the Border p.145]: a strong signature ending (Sage compares it to Joyce's "The Dead"), but it reads as the endpoint of strands 2 and 5 rather than a separate mechanism.

## Why 6 strands
This is a short, compact novel, but it runs on a premise (the returned first love), a frame (chronological self-indicting memoir), a counterweight (Izumi), a title-map device, an uncanny-presence device and a social theme (the borrowed life); each is separately recognizable and removing any one would change the book, while the remaining features feed into these six.
# DNA: Sputnik Sweetheart (1999 in Japanese, Kodansha; English tr. Philip Gabriel 2001 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_Sweetheart ; also the copyright page [Sputnik Sweetheart p.4])
Type: novel

Page refs are JSON page indexes (page N = pages[N-1]), not the printed page numbers inside the text.

## Engine in one paragraph
A chain of love that runs one way and never comes back: the narrator K loves his friend Sumire, a would-be novelist; Sumire falls hard for Miu, an older married woman; and Miu can no longer desire anyone, because years earlier, on a Ferris wheel in Switzerland, half of her went over to "the other side" of a mirror. When Sumire finally reaches for Miu on a Greek island and is gently refused, she vanishes, and K, who had been telling "her" story from the sidelines, is pulled into the centre and has to make sense of it from her hidden writings and from his own brush with the other side. The whole thing is held together by one image from the title: satellites (and the dog Laika) circling in the dark, crossing paths briefly and never touching. What makes the book run is a desire that cannot be answered, and a split between this world and another one that swallows whoever tries to close the gap.

## DNA strands (7)

### 1. A one-way chain of love that ends in a vanishing — level: premise
- What it is: Every love in the book points at someone who cannot return it. K loves Sumire, who has no desire for him. Sumire loves Miu, whose body cannot respond. Miu's own desire is gone. The pressure in the chain finally breaks when Sumire makes her move on the island, is turned down, and disappears.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole plot is this chain and its breaking point. Each link is a different reason love cannot be answered (indifference, a body that will not respond, a self cut in half).
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.6] (the opening announces Sumire's love for an older married woman), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.57] (K's unreturned longing for Sumire), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.113] (Miu's body cannot respond to Sumire), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.164] (Sumire is never found)
- External: https://people.willamette.edu/~rloftus/sputreview.html — Francie Lin (2001) reads the novel as a study of desire that persists without release, with the narrator loving Sumire and Sumire loving the married Miu. Also https://www.lrb.co.uk/v23/n20/theo-tait/lonely-metal-souls — the LRB review calls it a melancholy love "quadrangle," counting K's affair.

### 2. The side-line narrator who gets pulled into the centre — level: stance
- What it is: K insists that this is Sumire's story and he is only the eyes it is told through. He stops partway to account for himself as "the narrator," wary of how he edits himself. Once Sumire is gone the book becomes his story: he crosses to the island, has his own uncanny night, splits as he reads, and comes home a different person. He tells it all from a later point and slips into present tense at the end.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book makes a point of its own narrative stance (a whole chapter opens on "now about me"). The slide from bystander to subject is the book's real arc.
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.53] (chapter 5 opens by turning to the narrator himself), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.54] (he explains the distance he keeps from others), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.154] (reading Sumire's document, he feels himself split and asks who he is), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.170] (the night before leaving the island, he says he will be a different person tomorrow)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v23/n20/theo-tait/lonely-metal-souls — compares K to Nick Carraway: involved in the drama but at its edge. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_Sweetheart — notes K is a markedly different protagonist from Murakami's usual ones.

### 3. Sputnik, Laika, and satellites as the picture of love — level: device (also theme)
- What it is: The book opens with an encyclopedia note on Sputnik I and II and the dog Laika, who was sent up and never brought back. Miu mixes up "beatnik" with "Sputnik," so Sumire privately calls her "Sputnik Sweetheart" and thinks of Laika looking out at empty space. Near the end K lies on a rock and pictures satellites as lonely metal souls that meet, pass and part without a word. That is the book's definition of what happened between the three of them.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title, the epigraph, a key comic exchange and the main reflection near the end all use the same image. It gives this novel its own model of intimacy: a brief crossing of orbits.
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.5] (epigraph on Sputnik and Laika), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.10] (the beatnik/Sputnik mix-up and the private nickname), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.170] (satellites passing without words), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.13] (Earth as "the third planet," the cosmic zoom applied to Sumire's life)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v23/n20/theo-tait/lonely-metal-souls — says the satellite metaphor organises the relationships (the review is titled with the "lonely metal souls" image). https://people.willamette.edu/~rloftus/sputreview.html — links the title to Laika and to the narrator's satellite musing as the book's emblem of isolation.

### 4. The mirror between this side and the other side: a split self — level: device (also theme)
- What it is: Stuck overnight on a Ferris wheel, Miu watches through binoculars as her own self in her apartment is with a man she loathes. She wakes with white hair, and is sure that half of her (her desire, her periods, her will to make music) went to the other side of a mirror she can never cross. K's theory is that Sumire went through that mirror to find the other Miu. K himself is drawn up a mountain at night by music that may not exist and briefly feels out of his own body.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book's supernatural layer is not a well or a hidden world. It is one sheet of glass that splits a person in two, and it is what drives Miu's coldness, Sumire's disappearance and K's own split.
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.47] (Miu says she became half a person fourteen years ago), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.150] (the white hair), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.151] (the half that went to the other side of the mirror), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.158] (K's theory that Sumire went through), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.163] (the music on the mountain that may never have existed)
- External: https://digitalcommons.kean.edu/keanquest/vol4/iss2/5 — Katelyn Morris (Kean Quest, 2022) argues Miu's split sends her desire to the other side and that Sumire vanishes to join her there. https://www.lrb.co.uk/v23/n20/theo-tait/lonely-metal-souls — treats the doppelganger as the book's central figure of uncertainty.

### 5. The key backstory comes only through Sumire's hidden documents — level: structure
- What it is: The heart of the mystery is held in a found text. K opens Sumire's locked suitcase (the code is his own phone area code) and finds a floppy disk with two untitled files. The book then hands over to Sumire's voice: her thoughts on writing and Miu, then Miu's Ferris-wheel story as Miu told it to her and as she wrote it down. Only after that does K come back to react.
- Why it is specific to this work: Miu's past reaches us through three layers (Miu tells it, Sumire writes it, K reads it). The vanished writer finally gets her real writing done only in documents for one reader.
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.124] (the padlock opens with K's area code; the diary and disk), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.125] (Document 1 begins in Sumire's voice), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.127] (Sumire on why she stopped writing after meeting Miu), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.154] (chapter 13: K rereads both documents and notes their more restrained tone)
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/sputnik-sweetheart — the disks found on Sumire's computer are the clue that she has moved on to "the other side."

### 6. A gate needs blood: the bone-gate parable of fiction — level: device (also theme)
- What it is: Early on K tells Sumire about old Chinese city gates built from battlefield bones and brought to life with fresh dog's blood. A story, he says, likewise needs a baptism that links this world with the other one. She says she doesn't want to kill an animal, and he tells her it is a metaphor. The image comes back at the very end, when Sumire on the phone says she thinks she cut something's throat to make her gate. K then checks his palms for blood, and has already said on the island that blood has been shed inside him.
- Why it is specific to this work: The parable ties writing, love and crossing to the other side together with one rule (something must be given up). It echoes the sacrificed Laika of the epigraph.
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.18] (the gate parable and "it's a metaphor"), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.198] (Sumire's call returns to the gate and the cut throat), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.199] (K looks at his palms for blood), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.170] (blood has been shed and something in him has gone)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v23/n20/theo-tait/lonely-metal-souls — names the Chinese gates among the recurring symbols that hint at a crossing without spelling it out.

### 7. The late-night phone call from a phone box — level: device
- What it is: Sumire and K's friendship mostly happens through calls she makes from a phone box at hours like 3 or 4 a.m., talking about signs and symbols, writing and desire. The crisis arrives as a 2 a.m. call from Greece. The novel ends with K waking at 3 a.m. and staring at the phone until it rings. Sumire says she is back, in "our" phone box, and the line cuts off. We never learn if it was real.
- Why it is specific to this work: The phone is the line between K and Sumire across distance and maybe across worlds. The book's open ending rests on one call that cuts off.
- In-text refs: [Sputnik Sweetheart p.53] (after a 3:30 a.m. call from the phone box he cannot sleep), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.58] (an exhausted K is woken by Sumire's call), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.196] (he wakes at 3 a.m. and imagines her dialling), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.197] (the phone rings; she says she is in the phone box), [Sputnik Sweetheart p.198] (the call cuts off and he waits)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_Sweetheart — notes that the book ends on the telephone, a recurring motif used for calls from distant, unclear places.

## Weak candidates
- The Carrot subplot (K's lover's son caught shoplifting; K tells him about loneliness and drops the storeroom key into a ditch) [Sputnik Sweetheart p.185], [Sputnik Sweetheart p.186]. It mirrors K's own loneliness and gives him a small act of release, and the Kirkus and LRB reviews mention it. But it reads more as an echo of strands 1 and 2 than as its own engine, and some critics (Taipei Times, https://www.taipeitimes.com/News/feat/archives/2001/06/17/0000090451) suspect the late parts were bolted on.
- Mozart's "Das Veilchen" (Sumire's name; a pretty tune with lyrics about a trampled violet), which K later plays over and over [Sputnik Sweetheart p.19] plus the reading notes. It is a strong motif for Sumire, but it works inside strand 1 (love that gets crushed) rather than as a separate mechanism. External: none found.
- The moon as a changing witness (orphan, priest, stone ball, mouldy half-moon), which K blames for all the splits [Sputnik Sweetheart p.163], [Sputnik Sweetheart p.199]. It is clearly patterned, but the moon is a common Murakami fixture, and here it serves strand 4 more than it stands alone. External: none found.

## Why 7 strands
It is a short novel, but it has an unusually clear set of interlocking parts: a premise (the one-way love chain), a deliberately foregrounded narrative stance, two signature images (satellites, the mirror), a found-document structure, a parable that comes back as the ending's key, and the phone-box line that carries the open ending. Each one is something a reader of this book would recognise and would not find the same way in Murakami's other novels. No source with Murakami's own comments on this novel was found (Paris Review/Rubin searches turned up nothing book-specific). External support is from critics and one academic article.
# DNA: The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle (Japan 1994-1995 in three volumes; Jay Rubin's English translation 1997. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind-Up_Bird_Chronicle and the Vintage copyright page [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.5])
Type: novel

## Engine in one paragraph
An out-of-work man in a Tokyo suburb is cooking spaghetti when a stranger phones him, and from that moment his small, orderly life comes apart. First the cat goes missing, then his wife Kumiko. He is a man who reacts more than he acts, and the only way he can get her back is to stop waiting. He goes down: into a dry well in an empty house, through a wall, into a dark hotel room where the hidden part of his wife is being held. On the way, other people pour their stories into him. Old soldiers tell of Manchuria and Nomonhan, women tell of things done to their bodies and selves, and letters, magazine pieces and computer files turn up. Slowly those stories show that his private loss is tied to a public chain of violence. At the centre of it is his brother-in-law, Noboru Wataya, who violates people in a way that empties them out. The book runs on one pattern: a passive man is pushed downward, takes on other people's histories and marks, and finally has to commit an act of violence himself to bring someone back.

## DNA strands (7)

### 1. The missing cat that becomes a missing wife — level: premise
- What it is: The novel opens on a tiny domestic errand. Kumiko sends her unemployed husband to look for their lost cat, which is named after her brother. It turns into the story of finding out that he never really knew the woman he married, and of trying to get her back. The cat's disappearance is the first crack, and the wife's is the real one. The whole book is a search.
- Why it is specific to this work: The trivial errand (the cat) slides into the existential one (the wife), and the wife leaves without explanation behind a closed door of her own secret life. That is the motor of this novel and of no other.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.10], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.18], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.204], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.316]
- External: https://www.theparisreview.org/interviews/2/the-art-of-fiction-no-182-haruki-murakami — The interviewer's introduction (not Murakami's own words) describes the book as starting plainly, as a man's search for his missing wife, before it turns into something much stranger. Also https://www.kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/the-wind-up-bird-chronicle/ — Kirkus says losing Kumiko is what drives everything that follows.

### 2. The well as a passage to Room 208 — level: device
- What it is: The narrator climbs into the dry well of an abandoned house nearby. In the dark at the bottom he passes into another space: a hotel room numbered 208, with whiskey, ice and a woman whose face he cannot see, who turns out to be connected to Kumiko. He gets there through a wall that feels like cold gelatin. He goes back to that room again and again, and his last return is the climax of the novel.
- Why it is specific to this work: Wells show up elsewhere in Murakami. Here the well is the working route of the plot, a place he sits in deliberately and later buys, which leads to one particular room. It is also echoed by Lieutenant Mamiya's well in Mongolia.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.279], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.285], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.451], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.663], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.191], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.194]
- External: https://jungpage.org/learn/articles/book-reviews/165-novelist-and-analyst-search-the-japanese-psyche — Mary Ann Holthaus, reviewing the Murakami and Hayao Kawai conversations, reads the well as the way into another world. Kawai describes the wall between the two worlds as thin. Also https://auriea.org/Murakami_interview1 (a mirror of Laura Miller's 1997 Salon interview), where Murakami says he has always been very drawn to wells.

### 3. History's violence bleeding into the present (Manchuria / Nomonhan) — level: theme
- What it is: Long inset stories take the reader back to Japan's war on the continent: Mr. Honda's endless talk about Nomonhan, Lieutenant Mamiya watching a man skinned alive and then being thrown into a desert well, the killing of the animals at the Hsin-ching zoo, and the bat execution of Chinese cadets. The narrator eventually traces a chain linking every character to prewar Manchuria. He cannot understand why he and Kumiko have been pulled into a history older than they are. His own violence with a bat repeats the same pattern.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is the Murakami novel in which a domestic mystery is deliberately tied to a named historical atrocity, the 1939 Nomonhan war, and in which the past comes into the present as inherited guilt and as a method of violence.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.65], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.66], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.465], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.599], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.571], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.388]
- External: https://www.historynewsnetwork.org/article/32899 — Emily Parker (Wall Street Journal, 2006) reports that Murakami researched Nomonhan and was struck by how irrational the Imperial Army's command system was. She notes that Japanese critics at first dismissed the historical sections as decoration. Also https://auriea.org/Murakami_interview1 — Murakami tells Salon he did not want to write the war scenes but did.

### 4. Noboru Wataya: the evil that empties people out — level: theme
- What it is: The antagonist is not a monster. He is a smooth economist on television who becomes a politician. What he does to people is take away or corrupt their inner selves: Creta Kano was violated by him, Kumiko is held captive by him, and a whole public listens to him. The narrator's quest becomes a confrontation with him, carried out in the dream room with a bat. In the waking world the man collapses, and Kumiko herself ends his life.
- Why it is specific to this work: Evil here belongs to one person and is defined as defiling someone's inner being, not as violence to the body. The hero's success depends on striking back at that person on another plane.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.52], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.279], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.361], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.672], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.691], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.695]
- External: https://proof.csmonitor.com/1997/0709/070997.feat.feat.1.html — Christian Science Monitor (1997). Murakami describes the politician figure as embodying chaotic violence, and the article frames the novel as being about taking responsibility. Also Kirkus (URL above), which describes the arc as a confrontation with the evil that hurt Okada.

### 5. The mark on the cheek — level: device
- What it is: After his first stay in the well, the narrator wakes with a bluish-black mark on his right cheek about the size of a baby's palm. A veterinarian in the wartime zoo story had the same mark, and he turns out to be Nutmeg's father. Because of the mark, Nutmeg finds him and puts him to work as a secret healer. At the climax it is cut in the dark room, and afterwards it is gone.
- Why it is specific to this work: The mark passes between generations and between real and unreal levels. It works as proof that he really went somewhere, as a bond with the past, and as a job, and it disappears exactly when the story is resolved.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.330], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.462], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.603], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.676], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.694]
- External: https://www.kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/the-wind-up-bird-chronicle/ — Kirkus notes that the mark appears after an out-of-body experience and leads to his alliance with a mother-and-son pair of psychics. Also http://www.infinityplus.co.uk/nonfiction/windup.htm — David Mathew says the mark comes from the hotel-room encounter and gives him healing powers.

### 6. The wind-up bird: a fate-spring only some can hear — level: device
- What it is: An unseen bird in the neighbourhood makes a sound like someone winding a spring. Kumiko named it, May Kasahara uses it as the narrator's nickname, and in the well he imagines that he himself has to wind the world's spring now that the bird has gone quiet. The same cry is heard by a boy, by a doomed young soldier at the zoo, and by the veterinarian during the executions. Cinnamon even saves his stories as files under the novel's title. The narrator works out that people who hear it are pulled toward ruin, like wound-up dolls.
- Why it is specific to this work: It is the title device. It is one sound that runs across the 1980s and the 1930s and connects the private household to the wartime chronicle, and it carries the book's question about free will and fate.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.14], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.75], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.296], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.411], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.472], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.603]
- External: http://www.infinityplus.co.uk/nonfiction/windup.htm — Mathew notes that the title comes from the bird's cries and that May calls Toru "Mr Wind-Up Bird". The copyright page credits Alfred Birnbaum with first coining the term "wind-up bird" in the short story "The Wind-Up Bird and Tuesday's Women" [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.5].

### 7. A listener surrounded by other people's documents — level: structure
- What it is: Most of the novel is not narrated by Toru. It is handed to him. Creta Kano and Lieutenant Mamiya tell long first-person confessions while he serves coffee. Letters arrive from Kumiko, Mamiya and May Kasahara. Tabloid exposés about the cursed house with the well appear with the names blanked out. There are computer chats with Kumiko, and Cinnamon's numbered story files are told in the third person. Toru's stance is to receive: he hedges, echoes the other person's words back, and lets other voices build the world, until he finally acts himself.
- Why it is specific to this work: No other Murakami novel is assembled from so many different kinds of document, and here the patchwork form is itself how the history in strand 3 gets into the present.
- In-text refs: [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.107], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.156], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.316], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.446], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.515], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.555], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.559], [The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle p.582]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind-Up_Bird_Chronicle — The article notes May's letters, Mamiya's story told both in person and in letters, and the long accounts from Creta, Nutmeg and Honda. The Paris Review introduction (URL above) calls the book a hybrid narrative and compares it to Tristram Shandy. That is the interviewer's view.

## Weak candidates
- The Thieving Magpie overture as a recurring cue. It is the first sound in the book, and later the waiter in the dream hotel whistles it as a guide through the corridors, and Book Three reprises it [p.10], [p.281], [p.637], [p.653]. It is distinctive, but it works more as a thread connecting scenes than as the engine. It also overlaps with Murakami's general habit of using music. External: none found.
- Water and Honda's prophecy. Honda tells Toru to be careful of water that is missing or that shows up where it shouldn't [p.63], and at the climax the well fills up [p.680]. It may be a real structural rhyme, but in the text it serves the well strand (2) and does not stand by itself. External: none found.
- May Kasahara as a counter-voice about death and decay. Her letters and her teasing frame Toru's descent and give the closing farewell [p.292-295], [p.515], [p.681], [p.701]. She is essential as a character, but her role may belong under strand 7 and not be a separate mechanism. External: none found.
- Passive narrator turning into active "commitment". This is implied by the CS Monitor framing of responsibility and by the Murakami and Kawai book, whose chapter list has a section titled "The Meaning of Commitment" (https://www.daimon.ch/Haruki-Murakami-Goes-to-Meet-Hayao-Kawai). I could not read Murakami's own words about this novel there, so it is folded into strands 1 and 4 and not claimed as a strand.

## Why 7 strands
This is a three-book novel with two time periods and an unusually layered form. It needs a premise (1), a mechanism for crossing worlds (2), two thematic engines (history in 3 and personal evil in 4), two signature devices that tie the eras together (the mark in 5 and the bird in 6), and a distinctive document-collage structure (7). Each strand runs from the beginning to the end and is cross-referenced inside the text (see p.571 and p.603-604), so dropping any one would leave a visible gap.
# DNA: Hear the Wind Sing (1979, https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/hear_the_wind_sing.htm)
Type: novella

## Engine in one paragraph
A narrator who is about to turn thirty tries to write a story and keeps telling us how hard writing is. What he produces is a scrapbook of an eighteen-day summer in 1970, spent home from college, drinking beer at J's Bar with the Rat and meeting a girl with four fingers on one hand. The scrapbook is made of short numbered pieces that never quite add up. Around it he builds a fake American writer, Derek Hartfield, who serves as his teacher and his alibi. Nothing much happens in the summer itself. The weight comes from what has already gone missing: a dead girlfriend, the Rat's drift, and the girl who will vanish. The book is about trying to write your way toward a loss you can't put into words.

## DNA strands (4)

### 1. A story about failing to write the story (level: stance)
- What it is: The book opens and closes with a narrator in his late twenties, looking back nearly a decade, saying honest writing is close to impossible and that all he can manage is a list of gains and losses. The summer of 1970 is presented as his imperfect attempt, and the epilogue drops back to the present: he is 29, married, in Tokyo.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is Murakami's first novel and its frame is literally a debut writer's statement of method, so the subject of the book is partly the act of starting to write.
- In-text refs: [Hear the Wind Sing p.2-3] (writing as hopeless and as a partial attempt at self-therapy), [Hear the Wind Sing p.5-6] (the notebook split into gained/lost, and his intended reader), [Hear the Wind Sing p.84] (epilogue: narrator at 29)
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/hear_the_wind_sing.htm — the review describes the eighteen-day summer of 1970 framed by sections set nearly ten years later. Also https://letterpressproject.co.uk/inspiring-older-readers/2015-08-14/hear-the-wind-sing-pinball — summarizes Murakami's preface: he drafted the opening in English and translated it back, which is where the short, plain sentences came from.

### 2. Derek Hartfield, the invented mentor (level: device)
- What it is: The narrator says he learned almost everything about writing from Derek Hartfield, an American pulp writer who never existed. He gives him a full biography, titles, quotations, a dramatic suicide, and an invented researcher, and the closing section is the narrator's pilgrimage to Hartfield's grave in Ohio, where he says the story began.
- Why it is specific to this work: Hartfield is a hoax that appears only in this book. He lets the young writer name his influences and keep them at an ironic distance at once.
- In-text refs: [Hear the Wind Sing p.3-4] (Hartfield introduced as his teacher, his death), [Hear the Wind Sing p.66-68] (Hartfield's views on the novel, the Mars wells story), [Hear the Wind Sing p.85-87] (Hartfield's biography, the grave visit, credit to a fictional researcher)
- External: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/235279/9780804170147/readers-guide — the publisher's guide calls Hartfield fictional and asks why the narrator invokes him. https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/hear_the_wind_sing.htm — the review also identifies him as a fictional pulp author.

### 3. A collage of short numbered pieces (level: structure)
- What it is: The book is split into forty short numbered sections. Some are a single line, such as the chapter that only gives the story's dates. Some are a quoted passage from another book, and some are radio broadcasts marked ON and OFF air. Bar scenes, backstory, song lyrics and fake documents sit side by side with no transitions.
- Why it is specific to this work: Later Murakami novels flow as continuous narratives. Here the fragment form is the method itself, matching the narrator's claim that he can only make lists.
- In-text refs: [Hear the Wind Sing p.6] (one-sentence chapter giving the dates), [Hear the Wind Sing p.46] (a chapter that is just a quoted passage plus one line), [Hear the Wind Sing p.29] (DJ broadcast with ON/OFF blocks), [Hear the Wind Sing p.80-82] (DJ reads a sick listener's letter)
- External: none found that specifically discusses the numbered-fragment form (the reviews I could open note a pastiche feel and bare prose but say nothing about chapter shape).

### 4. A summer haunted by absences (level: theme)
- What it is: On the surface it's an idle summer of beer and talk. Under that, every thread is about someone gone or about to go. The third girl he slept with hanged herself and nobody knows why. The Rat is quitting college and can't say what is wrong. The four-fingered girl has an abortion and later vanishes without a trace. Losses are reported flatly and then left unexplained.
- Why it is specific to this work: The book is built so that the dead girlfriend is the center of gravity even though she gets only a few short passages. That makes this summer a story of mourning that refuses to call itself one.
- In-text refs: [Hear the Wind Sing p.42] (the third girl found hanged), [Hear the Wind Sing p.54-55] (her single photo; no one knows why she died), [Hear the Wind Sing p.78] (the girl's operation), [Hear the Wind Sing p.84-85] (the girl vanished; the narrator can't cry; the cow-stomach grass and the record are what remain)
