- External: https://letterpressproject.co.uk/inspiring-older-readers/2015-08-14/hear-the-wind-sing-pinball — the review notes that nothing much happens and singles out regret over lost relationships as the mood.

## Weak candidates
- The Rat as the narrator's double: he is a rich boy who hates the rich and wants to write novels, and in the epilogue he is still writing them [Hear the Wind Sing p.63-64], [Hear the Wind Sing p.84]. This could be a strand, a second would-be writer mirroring the first. I left it out because no external source I opened makes the double reading, and the Rat may be better read as part of strand 4.
- The radio DJ as a second voice [p.29], [p.80-82]: this is vivid, but I treat it as one instance of the collage (strand 3) rather than a separate engine.

## Why 4 strands
It's a novella with a heavy frame. The frame (stance), the hoax mentor (device), the collage form (structure) and the buried loss (theme) each do separate work, and taking any one away would change the book. Other features, like the bar talk, the songs and the similes, run on top of these four.

---

# DNA: The Strange Library (2005 illustrated Japanese edition; earlier version 1983 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Strange_Library)
Type: novella (illustrated short book)

## Engine in one paragraph
A polite schoolboy goes to return two library books and is sent to a basement room. A sinister old man there locks him in a cell and tells him to memorize three volumes on Ottoman taxation, and the plan is to eat his knowledge-filled brain afterwards. A gentle man in a sheepskin and a voiceless girl, who may not exist in the sheep man's world, help him escape on the night of the new moon. He gets home, but his new shoes, his starling and finally his mother are gone. The engine is a fairy-tale trap fitted inside the most harmless public building there is, told by a boy too obedient to refuse.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The library that eats your brain (level: premise)
- What it is: The book turns the public library into a predator. Knowledge is something you are made to cram so that someone else can consume it, and the sheep man explains this with deadpan economics: libraries everywhere do this, more or less, because lending knowledge for free has to pay off somehow. The whole plot is the trap of the cell, the memorizing and the escape.
- Why it is specific to this work: The horror premise is attached to a library and to the act of learning itself, which sets it apart from Murakami's other underground spaces.
- In-text refs: [The Strange Library p.14] (books marked internal use only), [The Strange Library p.27] (ordered to memorize three books within a month), [The Strange Library p.30-34] (brains will be eaten; the economics of libraries)
- External: https://themillions.com/?p=71619 — the Millions essay reads the library as a brain full of knowledge we may not want, and notes the sheep man's claim that knowledge-filled brains taste good.

### 2. A boy who can't say no (level: stance)
- What it is: The first-person child narrator obeys out of upbringing. He waits after knocking because his mother taught him to, stays because he can't refuse clearly, and follows orders because he's the type who does. He asks himself why he agrees when he disagrees, but never changes. This compliance is what walks him into the cell.
- Why it is specific to this work: Here the passive narrator is a child, and his politeness is the mechanism of the plot rather than just a personality trait.
- In-text refs: [The Strange Library p.9] (waits at the door because of how he was raised), [The Strange Library p.16] (can't give a clear no), [The Strange Library p.21] (asks himself why he gives in), [The Strange Library p.42] (follows orders by nature)
- External: https://themillions.com/?p=71619 — the essay notes the narrator accepts absurd events without question and worries mainly about missing dinner and upsetting his mother.

### 3. Overlapping worlds, then what's left: shoes, starling, mother (level: device)
- What it is: The rescuer belongs to a different layer of reality. The sheep man says no girl exists, she says each person has his own world, and in the end she turns out to be the starling, swelling to destroy the dog. After the escape the narrator can't say how much really happened. All he is sure of is the concrete losses: the birthday shoes he left behind, the starling's empty cage, and then (in this edition) his mother's death, which leaves him alone in the dark.
- Why it is specific to this work: The shoes and starling are set up early, and the ending pays them off as the only things he can verify.
- In-text refs: [The Strange Library p.47] (the girl on separate worlds), [The Strange Library p.80] (starling speaks in the girl's voice), [The Strange Library p.84] (unsure what was real; lost shoes and starling), [The Strange Library p.86] (mother's death, alone at 2 a.m.)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Strange_Library — the plot summary confirms the escape, the sheep man's disappearance and the vow never to return, but it doesn't mention the mother's death, so that final page may differ between editions. External for the overlapping-worlds reading: none found.

## Weak candidates
- The Ottoman tax collector immersion: while reading, the boy becomes the tax collector Ibn Armut Hasir, and that dream-Istanbul mirrors his own situation (a wife who serves meals, a pet bird, the new moon) [The Strange Library p.43], [The Strange Library p.54-55]. This is a nice story-within-story, but it is too brief to count as a strand.
- The black dog from his childhood returning as the old man's beast [p.48], [p.70-72]: a trauma reappearing. It's important, but I treat it as part of strand 3's dream logic.

## Why 3 strands
It's a short illustrated tale. The premise (the predatory library), the compliant child voice, and the overlapping-worlds ending with its concrete losses account for almost everything. Other features, like the food and the doughnuts, support these rather than drive them.

---

# DNA: Birthday Girl (2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story))
Type: short story (written for the anthology Birthday Stories; later in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
On her twentieth birthday a waitress fills in for a sick manager and brings dinner to the restaurant's unseen owner. He offers her one wish, and she makes it. More than ten years later she tells the story to the narrator at a bar, but she never says what she wished for. The story is built around that hidden center. The wish is the one thing we never get, and everything else (the ritual, the frame, the closing question) circles it.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The wish we never hear (level: device)
- What it is: The story cuts away right at the moment she states her wish. The section break hides it, and the old man's mild surprise that someone so young chose it is all we get. In the frame she refuses to tell, says it has both come true and not, and that time is part of it, and then lists her ordinary married life, adding that nobody, whatever they wish for, turns into anyone other than who they already are.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole story is built so the reader's main question stays permanently unanswered, and the gap becomes the point.
- In-text refs: [Birthday Girl p.14-15] (the cut at the moment of wishing; the old man's surprise), [Birthday Girl p.17] (she won't tell; it has both come true and not; her life as a list), [Birthday Girl p.18] (nobody becomes anyone but who they are)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story) — says the text never reveals the wish, in keeping with the custom of keeping birthday wishes secret.

### 2. Bar frame that turns the question on the listener (level: structure)
- What it is: The past is told in close third person, but the narrative keeps cutting back to the present bar, where she tells the narrator it really happened and toys with a coaster. At the end she asks what he would have wished for, he can't think of anything, and she tells him that's because he already made his wish. The last paragraph replays the old man's offer in the present tense, somewhere in the dark.
- Why it is specific to this work: The frame isn't just packaging. Its last move turns the story's question onto the listener (and the reader).
- In-text refs: [Birthday Girl p.8] (the frame introduced: talking about twentieth birthdays), [Birthday Girl p.14] (cut back to the present: this really happened), [Birthday Girl p.16-18] (coaster, silence, the reversed question, the present-tense echo)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story) — describes the nested structure (a married woman telling a friend) and the friend's inability to name a wish.

### 3. Magic hidden inside a fixed routine (level: premise)
- What it is: The supernatural door is an unchanging restaurant ritual. Every night at eight the manager alone takes chicken up to room 604 and brings empty plates down an hour later. Chefs try variations and get no response. Only because the manager gets sick on her birthday does she step into the ritual, and afterwards everything goes straight back to normal: empty plates, a manager with an ordinary stomach ache.
- Why it is specific to this work: The premise is a chance substitution in a rigid routine on a once-in-a-lifetime day. The old man himself treats the coincidence as meaningful.
- In-text refs: [Birthday Girl p.8-9] (the eight o'clock delivery and the always-chicken), [Birthday Girl p.10] (manager falls ill; she must take dinner up on the dot), [Birthday Girl p.13] (the old man links her birthday and the delivery), [Birthday Girl p.16] (empty plates; manager back the next day)
- External: none found that discusses the routine as the story's mechanism (the Wikipedia summary only recounts the shift substitution and the delivery).

## Weak candidates
- Turning twenty as a threshold: the coming-of-age birthday gives the wish its weight. Wikipedia notes twenty was Japan's age of majority. But this is part of strand 3's premise rather than its own engine [Birthday Girl p.12-13].
- The old man's habit of adding a polite phrase about her wishes [Birthday Girl p.11] foreshadows the wish. It's a nice detail, but too small to be a strand.

## Why 3 strands
It's a compact story with one secret. The hidden wish, the frame that turns it on the listener, and the ritual that makes it possible are the three load-bearing parts. Nothing else is structural.

---

# DNA: Abandoning a Cat (2019, The New Yorker — https://pen-online.com/culture/abandoning-a-cat-a-tribute-to-a-father)
Type: memoir essay (nonfiction; published in The New Yorker under Personal History, translated by Philip Gabriel)

## Engine in one paragraph
Murakami sets out to write about his father, a priest's son who was drafted through a clerical error, served in China, wrote haiku, became a teacher, and was estranged from his son for some twenty years. He opens with a small, odd memory: they left a cat at the beach and it beat them home. From there he works through his father's war years as an investigator, checking his own memories against records and often finding them wrong. The essay ends on chance and smallness: he is an unremarkable son of an unremarkable father, and his life rests on accidents. This is nonfiction, so the strands describe how the essay is built, not invented fiction.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The son as investigator correcting his own memory (level: stance)
- What it is: The narrator keeps admitting that he doesn't know or can't recall: why they got rid of the cat, why his father prayed in front of a glass case. He then checks family stories against military and university records and openly corrects himself. His father's regiment was not the one he had believed, and the timing of a story about being released doesn't match the records. Open questions are left on a list instead of being answered.
- Why it is specific to this work: The research process and its corrections are shown on the page, which makes uncertainty about the father the essay's method.
- In-text refs: [Abandoning a Cat p.1] (unanswered questions about the cat and the shrine), [Abandoning a Cat p.3] (discovers his belief about the regiment was wrong), [Abandoning a Cat p.6] (the release story doesn't fit the records; three missing years)
- External: https://pen-online.com/culture/abandoning-a-cat-a-tribute-to-a-father — the reviewer notes the father stays an enigma reached only through anecdotes, and that writing is Murakami's way to revive and reconsider memory.

### 2. Inherited weight: war, survival and transference (level: theme)
- What it is: The essay's heaviest moment is the father telling his young son, once and in passing, about his unit executing a Chinese prisoner. Murakami frames this as a burden handed down from father to son, a kind of ritual transfer. The father's daily sutras for the dead of both sides, and his guilt at surviving while his former divisions were wiped out, carry the same theme.
- Why it is specific to this work: It is Murakami's direct account of his own family's war inheritance, a subject his fiction usually approaches only at a distance.
- In-text refs: [Abandoning a Cat p.1-2] (morning sutras for the war dead of both sides), [Abandoning a Cat p.4] (the execution story and its transfer to the son), [Abandoning a Cat p.6] (survival by chance; why the sutras now make sense)
- External: https://pen-online.com/culture/abandoning-a-cat-a-tribute-to-a-father — the reviewer says the father's life was shaped by the Sino-Japanese and Second World Wars, and that Murakami wonders whether he would exist without those events.

### 3. Cat parables as bookends, ending in the raindrop (level: structure)
- What it is: The essay is framed by two cat memories. At the start is the abandoned cat that got home first, with the father's look changing from surprise to admiration to relief. At the end is a white kitten that climbed a pine tree and couldn't get down, which teaches that climbing down is harder than climbing up. The closing image makes a person one replaceable raindrop that still carries its own history.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title comes from the first cat, and the two cat stories give a nonfiction family history the shape of a parable, with an opening mystery and a closing lesson about death.
- In-text refs: [Abandoning a Cat p.1] (the cat beats them home), [Abandoning a Cat p.3] (the father's changing expression recalled), [Abandoning a Cat p.7] (the beach ride relived; the kitten in the pine; the raindrop; the unremarkable son)
- External: https://pen-online.com/culture/abandoning-a-cat-a-tribute-to-a-father — identifies the beach-cat episode as the central anecdote and the source of the title, and cites his conclusion about being an ordinary son.

## Weak candidates
- The father's haiku as a coded diary: Murakami reads the war haiku as the one place his father could say what censors wouldn't allow [Abandoning a Cat p.4-5]. This could be a device strand, but it supports strand 2 more than it drives the essay.
- Father-son estrangement and late reconciliation [Abandoning a Cat p.5], [Abandoning a Cat p.7]: this is central to the content, but it reads as the outcome of strands 1 and 2 rather than a separate engine. External: https://www.bookbrowse.com/bb_briefs/detail/index.cfm/ezine_preview_number/26369/abandoning-a-cat (twenty-year estrangement, deathbed reconciliation).

## Why 3 strands
As a memoir essay, its engine is the investigating, self-correcting voice, the inherited war theme, and the cat-parable frame. The biographical facts hang on these three.

---

Works covered: 4 of 4 (Hear the Wind Sing, The Strange Library, Birthday Girl, Abandoning a Cat). Note: page numbers follow the JSON page index of each file. The Hear the Wind Sing file appears to be an unofficial translation (neither Birnbaum's nor Goossen's), so page numbers won't match the published editions.
# DNA file: After the Quake (story collection, 6 stories)

Collection facts used below (all from https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake): Japanese title *Kami no Kodomo-tachi wa Mina Odoru*, Shinchosha, February 2000; English edition (Knopf) 2002, translated by Jay Rubin. All six stories take place in February 1995, the month between the Kobe earthquake and the Tokyo gas attack, and all are told in third person. The edition's copyright page names the English magazine venues (GQ, Granta, Harper's, The New Yorker, Ploughshares) [After the Quake p.119]. Per-story Japanese first-publication dates were not found in any source I opened, so each story header carries the collection years.

Page refs are to /home/claude/spotlight/books/After_the_Quake.json (page N = pages[N-1]). Every ref below was opened and read during this analysis. Story start pages: UFO in Kushiro p.6, Landscape with Flatiron p.24, All God's Children Can Dance p.41, Thailand p.57, Super-Frog Saves Tokyo p.73, Honey Pie p.92.

---

# DNA: UFO in Kushiro (in collection: Japanese 2000 / English 2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake; the story ran in The New Yorker in 2001, per https://wnyc.org/story/bryan-washington-reads-haruki-murakami)
Type: short story (in After the Quake)

## Engine in one paragraph
A wife watches the Kobe coverage for five silent days, then leaves her husband Komura. Her note says living with him was like living with air: he has nothing inside to give. With nothing better to do, Komura agrees to carry a small, weightless sealed box to Kushiro in Hokkaido for a coworker. There two young women pass around stories about wives who vanish, and in a love hotel one of them suggests the box held whatever used to be inside him. The story runs on a sealed container that stands in for a hollow man. It moves him to the cold edge of the country and ends just as he realises the journey has only started.

## DNA strands (3)
### 1. The box that holds the missing inner self — level: device
- What it is: A light wooden box, about the size of an urn for ashes, is the object Komura carries north. It is never opened. In bed, Shimao jokingly links it to his wife's charge that he is empty, and for an instant Komura feels close to violence. The box gives a shape to the inner emptiness the note named, and it lets the story ask what having anything inside would even mean.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole plot is a delivery errand for this one sealed object, and the story's central idea is the match between the box's unknown contents and the man's.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.7] (note: he has nothing inside to give; like living with air), [After the Quake p.10] (box like an urn for ashes, weighs almost nothing), [After the Quake p.21] (salmon made only of skin; what would the inner thing even be), [After the Quake p.22] (Shimao's joke that the box holds what was inside him; his near-violence)
- External: https://apersonalanthology.com/2022/12/02/ufo-in-kushiro-by-haruki-murakami/ — reads the never-revealed box as deliberate withholding that sits beside the bigger mystery of the wife's departure; also https://www.lrb.co.uk/v25/n08/thomas-jones/man-without-a-fridge — reads the box and Komura's failure in bed as signs of his emptiness.

### 2. Vanishing set off by a screen — level: premise
- What it is: The quake reaches the story only as TV images. The wife's silent marathon in front of the coverage comes just before she leaves, and nobody explains the link. In Kushiro the women tell a matching story: a wife saw a UFO and walked out a week later. Their view is that such things are probably linked in ways nobody can see. At the end, quake images on the hotel TV keep coming back to Komura and stop him from having sex.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title's UFO is a second, unexplained trigger for a wife leaving, set beside the first. That pairing is what makes the TV-watching read as a mysterious cause rather than background.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.6] (five days staring at the TV, then gone), [After the Quake p.15] (UFO wife leaves; maybe it is all linked), [After the Quake p.20] (quake images replay in his head during the failed sex)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake — notes that the characters experience the quake from far away, mainly through TV and newspapers.

### 3. A journey to the cold edge that ends at the start — level: structure
- What it is: Komura flies to Hokkaido in midwinter for no reason of his own. Distance is the running question: on arrival it doesn't feel far, and in the last scene he feels he has come a very long way. Shimao's closing line, said while tracing a pattern on his chest, turns that around: he is only at the beginning. The story is built as a trip whose ending opens rather than closes.
- Why it is specific to this work: The question of how far he has come is planted early and then turned in the last lines, so the trip itself is the story's arc.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.12] (doesn't feel far; planes too fast for the mind), [After the Quake p.13] (you can never get away from yourself), [After the Quake p.22] (now feels he has come a very long way), [After the Quake p.23] (just at the beginning)
- External: https://cliffordgarstang.com/the-new-yorker-u-f-o-in-kushiro-by-haruki-murakami — reads the ending as a hopeful reset rather than a grim one.

## Weak candidates
- The unfinished bear story that turns into a bell-ringing sex story [After the Quake p.14, p.18-19]. It is the first and only time Komura laughs, but it works as a mood shift, not as the engine.

## Why 3 strands
The story rests on an object (the box), a trigger (the screen, doubled by the UFO) and an arc (the trip that ends at a beginning). Anything else is texture.

---

# DNA: Landscape with Flatiron (in collection: Japanese 2000 / English 2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake)
Type: short story (in After the Quake)

## Engine in one paragraph
Junko is a teenage runaway living in a seaside town in Ibaraki. A near-midnight phone call brings her down to the beach, where Miyake, a middle-aged painter from Kobe, builds bonfires out of driftwood with great care. Over one fire, with her boyfriend sent home, the two talk about death. Junko has always read Jack London's "To Build a Fire" as a story about a man who wants to die. Miyake keeps dreaming he will die locked in a refrigerator, and he has left a wife and children in Kobe. He also explains his newest painting, an iron that is not really an iron. When Junko says she is completely empty, they agree to die together once the fire burns out. She falls asleep against him. The story runs on a slow, controlled fire whose life span sets the length of the night and the length of the pact.

## DNA strands (3)
### 1. The bonfire as a controlled ritual — level: premise
- What it is: Miyake builds the fire like a sculptor and lights it slowly. The fire spreads gently and is said to warm hearts. Junko feels a nameless rush of feeling in it, and Miyake teaches her that a fire made freely shows the viewer what is inside them. The fire's life is the story's clock, and the death pact is timed to it.
- Why it is specific to this work: Fire is the story's only setting and timer, and the closing line about waking when the fire goes out depends on it.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.26] (careful construction, slow lighting), [After the Quake p.29] (gentle spread, the fire accepts and forgives), [After the Quake p.32-33] (a free fire shows what is inside the person looking), [After the Quake p.39-40] (wait till the fire burns out; you'll wake when it goes out)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v25/n08/thomas-jones/man-without-a-fridge — calls this the best story in the book and reads the bonfire as a controlled counterpart to the earthquake.

### 2. Living by the shape of one's death — level: theme
- What it is: Three things about death are lined up. Junko reads London's freezing traveller as secretly longing to die, and her teacher mocked her for it. Miyake has a recurring dream of suffocating in a refrigerator, which is also why he owns none. Miyake cites Jack London's own death as a premonition that came true in disguise. Miyake says some people live in a way guided by how they will die. The story moves these ideas into a quiet pact rather than any act.
- Why it is specific to this work: The refrigerator dream and the reading of "To Build a Fire" belong only to this story, and they drive its conversation and its ending.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.27] (London story; the man wanted death), [After the Quake p.31-32] (Miyake can't sleep with a refrigerator around), [After the Quake p.35-36] (refrigerator death dream, always the same), [After the Quake p.37] (London's premonition; living guided by one's death)
- External: https://thestrangesttimes.substack.com/p/book-club-after-the-quake-landscape — a reading group discusses Junko's reading of London as perceptive and links the refrigerator dream to Murakami's recurring confined spaces.

### 3. The iron that is not an iron — level: device
- What it is: The title painting is just an iron in a room, but Miyake says it stands for something else that can only be painted through a stand-in. A page earlier he said the same about premonitions. The story names its own method here: things stand for other things, and we are never told what (Kobe, the family, the man himself).
- Why it is specific to this work: It is the title object and the story's stated rule for reading Miyake, whose Kobe past stays unspoken because he flatly refuses to say more.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.35] (wife and kids in Kobe; refuses to say more), [After the Quake p.37] (premonitions can stand for something else), [After the Quake p.38] (Landscape with Flatiron: the iron is not an iron)
- External: https://www.supersummary.com/after-the-quake/summary/ — notes that the painting is not really about an iron (no further analysis visible).

## Weak candidates
- Keisuke and Pearl Jam against 50,000 years of bonfires [After the Quake p.25], the present against deep time. It is a nice framing but secondary.

## Why 3 strands
Fire (setting and clock), death (theme), and the stand-for-something painting (the code) account for the story. The rest is character backstory that feeds them.

---

# DNA: All God's Children Can Dance (in collection: Japanese 2000 / English 2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake)
Type: short story (in After the Quake)

## Engine in one paragraph
Yoshiya, 25 and badly hungover, was raised in a sect by his young, beautiful, believing mother, who told him his father is "Our Lord." He has decided that his real father is a doctor who lost an earlobe to a dog. While his mother hands out supplies in quake-hit Kobe, he spots a man missing that earlobe in the subway. He follows him by train and taxi into an empty industrial zone and through a gap in a fence to a moonlit baseball field, where the man vanishes. With the question of fatherhood dissolved, Yoshiya dances alone on the pitcher's mound. He feels the rhythm of the world, including the earthquakes buried under it, and ends by calling on God aloud. The story runs on chasing a possible father into nothing and finding, in place of an answer, a body that can dance.

## DNA strands (3)
### 1. Two fathers: God or the man with no earlobe — level: premise
- What it is: The mother's story of three pregnancies despite strict contraception, which the sect's guide Mr. Tabata reads as God's will, sits against Yoshiya's certainty that a condom failed. The missing right earlobe is the single concrete mark that could prove the human father. The story keeps the faith version and the biology version side by side.
- Why it is specific to this work: The divine-versus-biological father question, carried by one physical sign, is this story's alone.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.43-44] (his father is the sect's God; Tabata's promise), [After the Quake p.46] (the doctor's missing earlobe, bitten by a dog), [After the Quake p.47] (three pregnancies as revelation; Yoshiya sure it was the condom; the man on the train has the same missing earlobe)
- External: https://firstthings.com/oh-god/ — notes the mother's faith is drawn as a caricature, yet she volunteers in Kobe while her son drinks.

### 2. The tail that leads into the dark — level: structure
- What it is: A real-time chase (subway, cab, a road along a wall, a pitch-black alley, a bent fence) is cut with backstory about the mother and the sect. It ends with the man simply gone and meaning itself coming apart. Yoshiya decides that what he chased was the tail of the darkness inside him.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's form is a chase that stops being about its target, and the narration says so outright.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.42] (spots the man, falls in after him, then cuts to the mother), [After the Quake p.49-50] (taxi tail into the factory zone), [After the Quake p.51] (dark alley, fence gap, man gone), [After the Quake p.52] (meaning breaks down; chasing the tail of his own darkness)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake — summarises the story as following a stranger with a missing earlobe to a baseball field. (Note: the same summary wrongly says the man dances there; in the text it is Yoshiya [After the Quake p.53-54].)

### 3. The dance on the mound as communion — level: theme
- What it is: Alone on the pitcher's mound, Yoshiya dances. His college girlfriend once called him "Super-Frog" for the way he danced. He feels his rhythm join the tides, wind and stars, then the dark rumbling under the earth where earthquakes live. The title sentence comes back twice: as his answer to an unseen watcher, and in the memory of holding the dying Mr. Tabata's hand. The story ends with a two-word cry to God.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title phrase is the climax's actual content, and the dance stands in for the faith he gave up.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.53] (Super-Frog nickname; rhythm of the world), [After the Quake p.54] (someone watching; title line; earthquakes under the dance floor), [After the Quake p.55] (Tabata's deathbed confession; hearts are not stones; title line again), [After the Quake p.56] (final cry to God)
- External: https://firstthings.com/oh-god/ — reads the closing cry to God as sincere and, in the end, not despairing.

## Weak candidates
- The outfield fly bargain with God, answered instead with an oversized penis [After the Quake p.44, p.53]. It is a comic thread about testing God that feeds strand 1 but doesn't stand alone.

## Why 3 strands
The question (which father), the form (a chase that dissolves the question) and the answer (dance in place of faith) make up the whole story.

---

# DNA: Thailand (in collection: Japanese 2000 / English 2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake)
Type: short story (in After the Quake)

## Engine in one paragraph
Satsuki is a Japanese thyroid pathologist going through menopause, divorced after years in Detroit. After a conference in Bangkok she takes a week's rest, driven by Nimit, an older, formal chauffeur who brings his late Norwegian employer's jazz tapes. Privately she hopes the Kobe quake crushed a man who lives there, a man she has hated for thirty years and blames for the children she never had. Nimit drives her to an old village woman, who finds a hard white stone inside her body. The woman prescribes a dream in which a snake will swallow it, and says the man was not hurt. Nimit tells her to start preparing for death and to stop putting her feelings into words. She flies home waiting for the dream. The story runs on a hatred that has become a physical object, and a cure that has to be dreamed, not spoken.

## DNA strands (3)
### 1. The stone of hatred inside the body — level: premise
- What it is: Satsuki's long hatred of a man in Kobe makes her half believe that her wish caused the earthquake. The old woman turns that hatred into a literal white stone, about the size of a child's fist, written over in Japanese characters, which would survive even cremation. Satsuki accepts the image and says he turned her heart, and her body, to stone.
- Why it is specific to this work: The hatred-to-stone conversion, plus guilt for a natural disaster, is unique to this story and drives its turn.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.62] (she hopes he was crushed; "liquefaction" talk), [After the Quake p.63] (he lived in Kobe; the children she should have had), [After the Quake p.68] (the white stone with characters; he is unhurt), [After the Quake p.69-70] (she weeps; she wished for the quake; heart and body turned to stone)
- External: https://bigskylibrary.net/reviews/review-after-the-quake.html — calls it the best story in the book, about a bitter woman shown how to let go of a heart of stone; and https://www.lrb.co.uk/v25/n08/thomas-jones/man-without-a-fridge — notes she wished for an earthquake to kill him and feels responsible.

### 2. Surrender to a guide — level: stance
- What it is: The friend who arranged the trip tells her to stop asking questions and let Nimit decide everything, and she does. The story then goes where he takes it: a private cold pool, sandwiches from an unnamed cook, a village seer. Nimit's own life of thirty-three years spent as another man's shadow mirrors the surrender, and his line that living and dying are of equal value becomes her inner refrain.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's movement comes from a doctor giving up control to a servant-guide, and the narration tracks her choice to stop asking.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.63] (let Nimit make all the decisions), [After the Quake p.64-65] (private pool; she stops herself asking who made the food), [After the Quake p.66] (Nimit as his employer's shadow; Coleman Hawkins solo), [After the Quake p.69] (prepare to die; living and dying of equal value)
- External: https://www.metrotimes.com/arts/ripple-effect-2283221/ — summarises the story as a stressed doctor finding peace at a quiet retreat, looked after by a valet.

### 3. A prescribed dream in place of a confession — level: device
- What it is: The cure is a dream written in advance: a green snake pushes out of a wall, and she must hold its neck until she wakes. At the airport she starts to tell her never-told secret, and Nimit stops her, because putting it into words would turn it false and hard as stone. His farewell polar-bear story about creatures that never connect is the only story she gets. She ends in her plane seat asking only to sleep and wait for the dream.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story refuses speech as therapy and ends on a scheduled dream. Its climax is a confession left unsaid.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.68] (the snake dream instructions), [After the Quake p.70] (Nimit stops the confession; words turn into stone), [After the Quake p.71] (polar bears; the Lapland employer had a stone inside too), [After the Quake p.71-72] (on the plane: let me sleep, wait for the dream)
- External: none found (no source I opened discusses the snake dream or the refused confession specifically).

## Weak candidates
- Music as a key to her dead father: the JATP and Erroll Garner tracks he made her memorise for candy, with "I'll Remember April" coming back as the final image [After the Quake p.60-61, p.71]. It is vivid and frames the story, but it supports the father wound behind strand 1 rather than driving anything. Still, a reader would likely remember it.
- The rabbit dream at the hotel pool [After the Quake p.63]. It previews the snake dream.

## Why 3 strands
Problem (the stone), method (surrender to Nimit) and cure (the dream instead of words). The jazz is strong but serves the father backstory.

---

# DNA: Super-Frog Saves Tokyo (in collection: Japanese 2000 / English 2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake)
Type: short story (in After the Quake)

## Engine in one paragraph
Katagiri is a mild, unpromoted collections officer at a Tokyo bank. He comes home to find a six-foot frog making green tea. Frog explains, with great courtesy and many literary references, that a giant Worm under the bank will cause a quake far bigger than Kobe's in three days. He needs Katagiri behind him, cheering, while he fights. Frog proves himself by scaring a debtor into paying. Then Katagiri is shot, or isn't, and wakes in hospital. Frog tells him the battle was fought in imagination and ended in a draw, then dissolves into worms and bugs in front of him. The story runs on a comic, formal monster-movie premise that turns into a fight inside one ordinary man.

## DNA strands (3)
### 1. The polite giant frog and the unheroic sidekick — level: premise
- What it is: The impossible turns up in a flat first sentence and then behaves like a perfect gentleman: apologising for coming in, making tea, croaking to prove he is a real frog, and correcting Katagiri every time he adds "Mr." to his name. He chooses Katagiri exactly because Katagiri is overlooked, and Katagiri's list of his own failings is answered by Frog's plain faith in him.
- Why it is specific to this work: The talking frog, his courtly manners and the repeated name correction are the story's signature, and the hero's job is only to give courage.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.73] (frog waiting at home; asks to be called just Frog), [After the Quake p.75] (croaks to prove he is real; correction repeated), [After the Quake p.79-80] (why Katagiri: sixteen years of thankless work; cheer me on), [After the Quake p.82-83] (Katagiri's list of his own ordinariness; Frog's answer)
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake — notes it is the only story in the collection with clearly supernatural elements.

### 2. Worm: stored-up rage given a body — level: device
- What it is: The quake has a creature under it. Worm sleeps for decades, soaks up every tremor, and turns it into rage. The Kobe quake woke him. Frog doesn't call him evil, only too dangerous to ignore now. The fight is planned through the bank's boiler room, but it really takes place in the imagination, with light against darkness.
- Why it is specific to this work: Only here is the earthquake given a body and an enemy, located under the hero's own workplace.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.76-77] (quake under the bank; fight Worm), [After the Quake p.77-78] (Worm absorbs tremors, makes rage; Kobe woke him), [After the Quake p.87-88] (battle in imagination; Katagiri's light; a draw)
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v25/n08/thomas-jones/man-without-a-fridge — summarises Frog fighting a quake-causing worm woken by Kobe while Katagiri lies in a coma, and compares it to Godzilla.

### 3. Frog as un-Frog: the fight is inside — level: theme
- What it is: The rescue becomes a puzzle about what was real. The shooting never happened, Katagiri was found unconscious with no wound, and he shouted for Frog in his sleep. A wounded Frog says that what the eyes see isn't necessarily real and that his enemy is partly the self inside himself. Then he bursts into maggots and centipedes that invade Katagiri, until a nurse switches on the light. It ends with Katagiri's grief for Frog and a restful sleep.
- Why it is specific to this work: The final shift, in which the monster battle turns out to be a struggle inside the self and the helper falls apart into vermin, belongs to this story.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.84] (the shooting), [After the Quake p.86] (no wound, no quake), [After the Quake p.88-89] (pure Frog and un-Frog; the enemy is the me inside me), [After the Quake p.89-91] (Frog bursts into worms; nurse; dreamless sleep)
- External: https://www.murrayewing.co.uk/mewsings/tag/jay-rubin/ — reads Frog as Katagiri's own repressed strength and his wish to live fully.

## Weak candidates
- Frog's literary references (Nietzsche, Conrad, Anna Karenina and its locomotive, Hemingway, Dostoevsky's "White Nights") [After the Quake p.80, p.82, p.84, p.88]. They are a strong running gag, and the locomotive comes back in Katagiri's last words [After the Quake p.91], but they colour strand 1 rather than standing alone.
- The Big Bear Trading debtor [After the Quake p.80-81], one of the collection's bear echoes (see collection DNA).

## Why 3 strands
A premise (the polite frog and the ordinary helper), an embodiment (Worm as rage) and a turn (the fight is inside). The gags and references are style on top of these.

---

# DNA: Honey Pie (in collection: Japanese 2000 / English 2002, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake)
Type: short story (in After the Quake)

## Engine in one paragraph
Junpei is a 36-year-old short-story writer from the Kobe area who has loved Sayoko since college. He let their friend Takatsuki marry her, and after their divorce he stays on as "Jun" to their daughter Sala. Sala has had nightmares since the quake: the Earthquake Man wants to stuff her into a too-small box. Junpei calms her with made-up stories about Masakichi, a talented honey bear with no friends. At the zoo he adds Tonkichi, a salmon-catching bear who loses everything. The night he and Sayoko finally sleep together, Sala appears with a message that the box is ready for everyone. Junpei sits up till dawn, decides to propose, rewrites the bears' ending into a happy one, and vows to keep watch. The story runs on a bedtime tale that mirrors the adults' triangle. A passive man becomes a protector only when he finds the happy ending in the story he is telling.

## DNA strands (3)
### 1. The bear tale that mirrors and then repairs the triangle — level: structure
- What it is: The story opens inside the bedtime tale: Masakichi doesn't belong to the bear world or the human world. At the zoo Junpei adds Tonkichi, whose salmon vanish without warning, and Sayoko echoes the salmon image in bed to say what it took for him to understand. The tale's ending is first sad, then rewritten at dawn: the bears bake honey pies together and never part, using Sala's own earlier idea.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title comes from the rewritten ending, and the frame tale tracks the adult story beat by beat.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.92-93] (opening inside the tale; Masakichi between two worlds), [After the Quake p.94] (Sala proposes honey pies), [After the Quake p.110-112] (Tonkichi, salmon vanish, sent to the zoo), [After the Quake p.114] (he only understood once the salmon were gone), [After the Quake p.116] (new ending: honey pies, best friends forever)
- External: https://www.supersummary.com/after-the-quake/summary/ — summarises Junpei calming Sala's quake nightmares with bear stories (plot only).

### 2. The passive writer who finally decides — level: theme
- What it is: Everything in Junpei's love life has been decided by others. Takatsuki picked the friends, took Sayoko, and now urges Junpei to marry her, and Junpei notes he has always been passive. He is a natural short-story writer whose stories are about unrequited young love. The story ends with his first real decisions: to propose in the morning, and to write different stories about people who wait through the night for the light so they can hold the ones they love.
- Why it is specific to this work: It is the collection's only writer protagonist, and his change is both personal (a proposal) and about his craft (a new kind of story).
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.98] (Takatsuki moves first; Junpei says he doesn't mind), [After the Quake p.103] (born short story writer; novels defeat him), [After the Quake p.109] (his position always passive; then the quake), [After the Quake p.115-116] (he will propose; he wants to write different stories)
- External: https://avclub.com/haruki-murakami-after-the-quake-1798197817 — describes the shy, passive writer always second in a triangle with his two closest friends, calls the story the personal centrepiece of the collection, and notes vague parallels with Murakami himself.

### 3. The Earthquake Man's box and the vow to keep watch — level: device
- What it is: The quake gets into the family through a child's dream: a tall old man wants to stuff Sala into a box far too small for anyone. At the moment of the lovers' union she delivers his message that the box is waiting, lid open, for everyone. Junpei stares at the dead TV screen, sensing that something is waiting inside it, and ends sitting against the wall guarding the sleeping mother and child.
- Why it is specific to this work: The box threat cuts into the climax, and the closing vow is aimed at it directly.
- In-text refs: [After the Quake p.96] (the Earthquake Man and the little box), [After the Quake p.114-115] (Sala at the door; box ready for everybody), [After the Quake p.115] (dead TV; they are waiting), [After the Quake p.116] (keep watch; never let anyone put them in that box)
- External: none found (no source I opened analyses the Earthquake Man).

## Weak candidates
- Kobe as a severed root: Junpei sees the quake on Spanish TV, never calls his estranged parents, and feels he has no roots [After the Quake p.109-110]. It is real but works as the trigger for strand 2.
- Sayoko's bra trick and the bra left hanging on a chair at dawn [After the Quake p.112-113, p.115]. It is memorable, but it is the hinge into the climax, not a mechanism.

## Why 3 strands
Frame (the bear tale), arc (passivity to decision) and threat (the box). The title, the climax and the last line each come from one of these.

---

# Collection-wide DNA (shared by all six stories)

### A. The quake felt from far away, through screens — level: premise
- Every story is set in February 1995 and keeps the disaster at a distance: on TV, in newspapers, or in someone else's trip. Examples are the wife's TV vigil, the mother's relief trips, Nimit's talk of liquefaction, Katagiri's averted Tokyo quake, and the Kobe footage Junpei watches with Spanish narration.
- Refs: [After the Quake p.6], [After the Quake p.17], [After the Quake p.48], [After the Quake p.62], [After the Quake p.78], [After the Quake p.109]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake — the month between the Kobe quake and the gas attack; characters encounter it mainly through media; all in third person (a departure from Murakami's usual first person).

### B. Inner emptiness turned into an object — level: theme
- Each protagonist carries a hollowness or a hardness that the story makes physical: the husband compared to air and the box, Junko's confession that she is empty, a heart of stone in God and the stone in Satsuki's body, Worm's stored rage, and the Earthquake Man's box.
- Refs: [After the Quake p.7], [After the Quake p.38-39], [After the Quake p.49], [After the Quake p.68], [After the Quake p.78], [After the Quake p.96]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_the_Quake — quotes Rubin that the characters must "confront an emptiness they have borne inside them for years."

### C. Confinement in a box — level: device
- A tight container keeps coming back as the shape of fear or loss: Komura's urn-like box, Miyake's death in a refrigerator, and the Earthquake Man's too-small box waiting for everyone.
- Refs: [After the Quake p.10], [After the Quake p.35-36], [After the Quake p.96], [After the Quake p.115]
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v25/n08/thomas-jones/man-without-a-fridge — lists empty boxes among the book's recurring motifs and notes porous boundaries between stories.

### D. Endings on the edge of sleep, dream or dawn — level: structure
- None of the six resolves. Each stops at a threshold: Komura told he has only started, a nap until the fire dies, a cry to God on the mound, waiting for the dream, a dreamless sleep, and keeping watch until morning.
- Refs: [After the Quake p.23], [After the Quake p.40], [After the Quake p.56], [After the Quake p.71-72], [After the Quake p.91], [After the Quake p.116]
- External: https://avclub.com/haruki-murakami-after-the-quake-1798197817 — says the quake calls lives into question, prompting reflection that slowly turns into action (not resolution).

### E. Echoes between stories (bears and frogs) — level: device
- Images pass from story to story: a bear story in Kushiro, the Big Bear Trading debtor, the honey bear; Yoshiya nicknamed "Super-Frog" before the Frog story, and a skinny frog as Tonkichi's last meal. Kobe is also a buried private past for Miyake, Satsuki and Junpei.
- Refs: [After the Quake p.14], [After the Quake p.53], [After the Quake p.80], [After the Quake p.92], [After the Quake p.111]; Kobe pasts [After the Quake p.35], [After the Quake p.62], [After the Quake p.109]
- External: https://www.lrb.co.uk/v25/n08/thomas-jones/man-without-a-fridge — mentions the "Super-Frog" nickname crossing into the Frog story and notes uncanny echoes across the book.

## Why these collection strands
A (distance) and B (emptiness made physical) are what the collection is about; C, D and E are the formal ties that make six separate stories read as one cycle. Murakami's own remarks found online were about wanting to write after his hometown was hit, not about these mechanisms (https://www.thejakartapost.com/life/2018/10/08/murakami-says-writing-is-what-he-can-do-for-disaster-sufferers, which does not name the book), so the external support here comes from critics and the translator.

Stories covered: 6 of 6 (UFO in Kushiro, Landscape with Flatiron, All God's Children Can Dance, Thailand, Super-Frog Saves Tokyo, Honey Pie). None skipped.
# DNA: Men Without Women (Japanese edition 2014, English edition 2017; sources: https://books.bunshun.jp/ud/book/num/9784163900742 for the Japanese release of April 2014, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men_Without_Women_(Murakami_short_story_collection) for the English edition of May 2017; the 2014/2017 dates also appear on the copyright page [Men Without Women p.5])
Type: story collection (7 stories in the English edition, translated by Philip Gabriel and Ted Goossen)

Note on dates for individual stories: the only first-publication data I saw are the English magazine appearances listed on the copyright page [Men Without Women p.5]. They are given per story below as "English first appearance". I did not find a verified Japanese magazine date for any single story, so none is given. The Japanese edition contained six stories. "Samsa in Love" is not among them (bunshun.jp list above) and was added in the English edition.

---

## Collection-level DNA (shared by the whole book)

### Engine in one paragraph
In Murakami's Japanese foreword, the motif is spelled out as the title says: men who, for one reason or another, have been left by a woman or are about to be. He says he wanted to restate that one situation as several different stories (summarized at https://president.jp/articles/-/12932). So the collection works like variations on a theme. The woman is lost to another man, to death, to a vanishing or to a history the man can't see, and each story tests a different male response: performance, observation, starvation, listening, numbness, newborn desire, or lyrical mourning. The title comes from Hemingway's 1927 collection (https://www.riotmaterial.com/lonely-people-haruki-murakamis-men-without-women/).

### Shared strands (4)

#### A. The man left behind — level: premise
- What it is: Every story starts after, or just before, a man loses a woman. The causes vary: adultery he discovers, death, suicide, a third man, a confinement that may take all women away. The man is shown in the aftermath rather than in the romance.
- Why it is specific to this collection: here the motif is the stated organizing principle of the whole book, not one theme among many.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.16] (Kafuku's wife and her four affairs), [Men Without Women p.117] (Kino walks in on his wife and friend), [Men Without Women p.87] (Tokai's woman leaves for a third man), [Men Without Women p.162] (the call reporting M's suicide), [Men Without Women p.113] (Habara fears losing all women)
- External: https://president.jp/articles/-/12932 — quotes the foreword: the motif is men who have been, or are about to be, left by women, recast as several stories.

#### B. The woman's interior as a locked room (the male blind spot) — level: theme
- What it is: The men can't know what the women felt or why they acted. Each story names that gap in its own image: a blind spot, a lying organ, a dream of a moon made of ice, buttons done up wrong, the unknown reason for a suicide. Usually a second character, not the hero, offers the explanation that might be right.
- Why it is specific: the collection makes the not-knowing itself the subject, and each story gives it a distinct metaphor.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.30] (Takatsuki: no man sees fully into a woman), [Men Without Women p.89] (Tokai's theory of women's lying organ), [Men Without Women p.130] (Kino's wife: something wrong from the start), [Men Without Women p.169] (the narrator can only guess what M told her husband)
- External: https://www.csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2017/0508/Men-Without-Women-is-Murakami-at-his-whimsical-best — the reviewer sees the stories exploring men's inability to truly possess the women in their lives.

#### C. A wound passed through a listener — level: structure
- What it is: The central experience reaches us second-hand. A man confesses to a driver, a writer, a bartender or a lover, or a story is told after sex, or a narrator reconstructs a dead man from a secretary's account. The listener is a recurring structural slot.
- Why it is specific: across the book, almost no story presents its core event directly. The retelling and the reaction to it are the story.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.33] (Kafuku confesses his revenge plan to Misaki), [Men Without Women p.74] (Tokai confides in the writer Tanimura), [Men Without Women p.84] (Goto narrates Tokai's decline), [Men Without Women p.91] (Scheherazade's post-sex tales)
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/men_without_women.htm — notes that many of the tales are retold second-hand, which places the core experience at a further remove.

#### D. Ending on a vigil: waiting, prayer or a leftover object — level: structure
- What it is: The stories don't resolve. They close on a man waiting (for sleep, for dawn, for a trout that never comes, to learn to dress) or on a wish or prayer for someone absent. Often a planted object returns to carry the feeling: an unused squash racket, a willow tree, a half eraser.
- Why it is specific: the shared closing shape turns the theme's aftermath into the book's ending rhythm. Each loss ends as a vigil, not a reckoning.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.35] (Kafuku settles in to nap), [Men Without Women p.63] (the wish that Kitaru is at least healthy), [Men Without Women p.90] (the light racket), [Men Without Women p.113] (Habara waits as a lamprey), [Men Without Women p.142] (Kino waits for dawn), [Men Without Women p.173] (prayer for M)
- External: https://worldliteraturetoday.org/node/3380 — lists abrupt endings without easy resolution among the collection's common features.

### Weak candidates (collection)
- Songs as titles and frames: two titles are Beatles songs ("Drive My Car", "Yesterday"), and the title story ends on a lost playlist. The Kirkus review notes the Beatles supply two titles (https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/men-without-women/print). But music is a frame only in "Yesterday" and the title story, so it isn't book-wide DNA.
- A recurring writer-narrator named Tanimura narrates both "Yesterday" [Men Without Women p.58] and "An Independent Organ" [Men Without Women p.77]. This is a real link, but it only covers two stories. External: none found.

---

## Story 1. DNA: Drive My Car (English first appearance: Freeman's, October 2015, per [Men Without Women p.5]; also listed at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men_Without_Women_(Murakami_short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Ted Goossen [Men Without Women p.35].

### Engine in one paragraph
Kafuku is a widowed actor who loses his licence over a drink-driving incident and a glaucoma diagnosis. He hires Misaki, a silent young woman, to drive his yellow Saab. In the passenger seat he tells her the story he has never told anyone: after his wife died, he befriended one of her lovers as a performance, meaning to destroy him, then let it go. The car is the confessional, acting is the method, and the blind spot, literal and moral, is the subject. Misaki gives the answer he has avoided: perhaps his wife slept with the man precisely because she didn't love him.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. The passenger-seat confession — level: structure
- What it is: The story is built as a frame. In the present, Misaki drives in silence and Kafuku rehearses his lines. Inside that frame he tells her the story of Takatsuki. The car, with its roof up or down, its cassette tapes and its unlit cigarette, sets the pace of the confession, and the story ends inside it.
- Why it is specific to this work: the moving car and its silent female driver are what make the confession possible. A woman behind the wheel is the very thing Kafuku says makes him tense.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.7] (opening theory about female drivers), [Men Without Women p.14] (rehearsing lines with cassettes in the Saab), [Men Without Women p.21] (stuck in traffic, he begins the lover story), [Men Without Women p.35] (ending in the car)
- External: https://writingatlas.com/story/haruki-murakami-drive-my-car — tags the story as a story within a story, with Kafuku telling his feelings to Misaki from the passenger seat.

#### 2. Friendship as a role — level: premise
- What it is: Kafuku befriends his dead wife's lover, Takatsuki, as a stage role. He plans to find the man's weakness and ruin him, then finds that the performance and the real friendship can't be separated, and that his rage was never quite rage. The story's last thought goes back to the stage: you leave yourself for a while and come back slightly changed.
- Why it is specific: the revenge plot is carried out entirely with an actor's craft, and it ends with Misaki and Kafuku agreeing that everyone is acting.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.23] (a role must play out like a song reaching its final chord; both real and acting), [Men Without Women p.33] (the plan to punish him and the demon on the ceiling), [Men Without Women p.35] (we are all actors; the returning self is never the same)
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/men_without_women.htm — summarizes the story as Kafuku befriending one of the men his wife slept with and telling it all to the driver he hires (supports the premise, not the acting reading itself).

#### 3. The blind spot — level: device
- What it is: Glaucoma gives Kafuku a blind spot on the right side, which is why he needs a driver. In the bar, the same phrase becomes the name for what he missed in his wife. Takatsuki turns it into a general truth about men and advises looking into your own heart instead.
- Why it is specific: one medical detail sets the plot in motion and also names its theme. It is this story's own word for the collection's not-knowing.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.13] (glaucoma and the blind spot), [Men Without Women p.29] (his fatal blind spot about his wife), [Men Without Women p.30] (Takatsuki: all men share it; look within), [Men Without Women p.34] (Misaki's alternative explanation)
- External: https://writingatlas.com/story/haruki-murakami-drive-my-car — notes that a glaucoma diagnosis leaves him with a right-side blind spot, which is what forces him to hire a driver. Also https://newleftreview.org/sidecar/posts/blind-spots — reads the blind spot as both literal and as people's failure to understand each other and themselves. This piece is mostly about the film and gets some details of the story wrong, so it is secondary support only.

### Weak candidates
- Uncle Vanya as a mirror (Misaki compares herself to Sonya; the play's lines on enduring the years). It is resonant [Men Without Women p.32], but it takes up only a few lines in the story. The film makes it central, per the NLR piece above.

### Why 3 strands
A framed confession, a revenge carried out as a performance, and one image (the blind spot) that links them. Anything else is texture.

---

## Story 2. DNA: Yesterday (English first appearance: The New Yorker, June 2014, per [Men Without Women p.5])
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Philip Gabriel [Men Without Women p.63].

### Engine in one paragraph
Tanimura, a Kansai native who speaks standard Tokyo Japanese, looks back to age twenty. His friend Kitaru, a Tokyo boy who taught himself the Kansai dialect, sings nonsense Kansai lyrics to the Beatles song in the bath. Kitaru then asks Tanimura to date his girlfriend Erika, because their love feels too comfortable. Erika is secretly seeing someone else. Sixteen years later, Tanimura and Erika meet again and confirm what happened. The mangled song and Erika's dream of a moon made of ice are what carry the memory forward to now.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. Dating by proxy — level: premise
- What it is: Kitaru can't move his childhood love forward and asks his friend to go out with her instead. The odd three-way setup reveals what each person is hiding: Kitaru's paralysis, Erika's affair, and the narrator's own loneliness.
- Why it is specific to this work: the triangle is arranged on purpose by the boyfriend himself. It is a voluntary hand-off rather than a betrayal.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.45] (Kitaru proposes the date), [Men Without Women p.46] (the three meet), [Men Without Women p.53] (Erika confesses the other man), [Men Without Women p.60] (years later she confirms it)
- External: none found that discusses the proxy date as such. https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2014/06/02/haruki-murakami-yesterday/ discusses Kitaru's refusal to follow the expected path.

#### 2. The nonsense "Yesterday" as frame and trigger — level: device
- What it is: The story opens with Kitaru's absurd Kansai version of the song and closes with the narrator, driving, hearing the real song and recalling the fake one. In between, the dialect (learned, worn and given up) works as identity performed. At the end the song becomes the machine that revives memory and admits memory rewrites itself.
- Why it is specific: the title song is literally mis-sung, and that mis-singing becomes the story's container. Its opening and closing are bookends made of the same lyrics.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.36] (the opening lyric and its strangeness), [Men Without Women p.37] (Kitaru learned the dialect like a foreign language), [Men Without Women p.62] (the song on the car radio; memory reconstitutes itself), [Men Without Women p.63] (the lyric returns at the close)
- External: https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2014/06/02/haruki-murakami-yesterday/ — notes that Kitaru insists on his own words to the song, and that the story is told as a remembrance of student days. Also https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/men-without-women/print — remarks on the Tokyo-born character's adopted Kansai dialect.

#### 3. The moon made of ice — level: device
- What it is: Erika describes a recurring dream of herself and Kitaru on a ship, watching a thin moon of ice that melts at dawn. Years later the narrator asks about it. She no longer dreams it but remembers every detail. In the last section the narrator realizes he was watching that same moon alone all along: a borrowed dream becomes his own loneliness.
- Why it is specific: a dream passes from one character to another and changes its owner and meaning. It is the story's emotional hinge.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.54] (the dream told), [Men Without Women p.61] (asked again years later; dreams can be borrowed), [Men Without Women p.63] (the narrator alone at the porthole)
- External: https://www.riotmaterial.com/lonely-people-haruki-murakamis-men-without-women/ — singles out the narrator, isolated at twenty, and his image of a moon of ice seen through a porthole. https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2014/06/02/haruki-murakami-yesterday/ calls the ice moon the story's primary symbol.

### Weak candidates
- The sixteen-year jump to a single reunion [Men Without Women p.58] is a structural feature, but it serves strands 1 and 3 rather than standing alone.

### Why 3 strands
One premise (the arranged triangle) and two devices that carry it into memory: a song mis-sung and a dream handed on.

---

## Story 3. DNA: An Independent Organ (in the Japanese collection of 2014 per https://books.bunshun.jp/ud/book/num/9784163900742; no separate first-publication date verified)
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Philip Gabriel [Men Without Women p.90].

### Engine in one paragraph
The writer Tanimura puts together a portrait of Dr. Tokai, a fifty-two-year-old cosmetic surgeon. Tokai has lived a smooth, artificial life as every woman's convenient second lover until he falls hopelessly for one married woman. A book about a doctor sent to Auschwitz makes him ask who he would be with everything stripped away. When the woman leaves him for a third man, he starves himself to death. His assistant reports the end, and Tokai's own theory, that women have a separate organ for lying, is turned back on him: he loved with an organ of his own that worked beyond his will.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. The contrived life undone by love — level: premise
- What it is: The narrator opens with a theory about people who live artificial lives without knowing it, then sets Tokai in that slot: a bachelor with a carefully arranged system of affairs. Falling in love for the first time breaks the system completely, and he wastes away from lovesickness to below half his weight.
- Why it is specific to this work: the man who was immune to attachment dies of it, literally starving. The love story runs as a reductio.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.64] (the opening theory of artificial lives), [Men Without Women p.66] (always the convenient number-two lover), [Men Without Women p.78] (a serious case; boats tied by a rope), [Men Without Women p.87] (death at under half his weight)
- External: https://www.csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2017/0508/Men-Without-Women-is-Murakami-at-his-whimsical-best — summarizes a carefree bachelor surgeon falling for a married woman who disproves his theory.

#### 2. Identity with everything stripped away — level: theme
- What it is: The Auschwitz doctor, a respected Berliner reduced overnight to a number, gives Tokai the question that haunts him: what is he without his career and comforts? His emaciated body later echoes the camp survivors, and the narrator ends by wondering whether the racket Tokai left him is an answer to that question.
- Why it is specific: an extreme historical analogy is set inside a romantic story. Identity, not just heartbreak, is what is being tested.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.76] (the Auschwitz doctor), [Men Without Women p.77] (Tokai's inventory of useless skills), [Men Without Women p.87] (the survivors' image returns), [Men Without Women p.90] (the racket as a possible answer)
- External: https://www.theartsdesk.com/books/haruki-murakami-men-without-women-review-bit-too-abstract-and-post-modern — notes that the surgeon's self-questioning draws on Nazi persecution and leads not to revelation but to self-erasure.

#### 3. The independent organ, turned inside out — level: device
- What it is: Tokai's cynical theory is that women carry a separate organ that lies on its own, leaving their conscience clear. The narrator delays it to the very end, then flips it: Tokai fell in love through just such an organ, one beyond his will. That organ is what keeps lives from being a series of contrivances.
- Why it is specific: the title is a theory that refutes itself in the closing paragraphs, so the story's moral sits in a reversed aphorism.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.89] (the theory stated), [Men Without Women p.90] (applied to Tokai's love; the rope image repeated)
- External: https://www.csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2017/0508/Men-Without-Women-is-Murakami-at-his-whimsical-best — names the organ theory as the story's crux.

### Weak candidates
- Writer-narrator as portraitist from partial evidence: he admits that conjecture fills the gaps, uses Goto's testimony, and says he writes so as not to forget [Men Without Women p.64], [Men Without Women p.84], [Men Without Women p.88]. This is strongly present, and complete-review notes the second-hand sourcing (https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/men_without_women.htm). But it is close to collection strand C, so I kept it as a candidate rather than a fourth strand.

### Why 3 strands
A premise (the artificial life collapsing), the question it raises (identity stripped bare) and the device that closes it (the organ theory reversed). The narrator's stance is shared with the collection.

---

## Story 4. DNA: Scheherazade (English first appearance: The New Yorker, October 2014, per [Men Without Women p.5])
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Ted Goossen [Men Without Women p.113].

### Engine in one paragraph
Habara can't leave "the House" for a reason that is never given. A plain housewife-nurse, assigned to support him, visits twice a week with groceries and sex. Afterwards she tells him stories in instalments that always stop at a climax when the clock says it's time to go: she was a lamprey in a former life, and as a teenager she broke into a classmate's house and swapped small tokens. His survival isn't at stake as in the original tale. What is at stake is his only link to reality and to women.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. The Thousand and One Nights frame, made domestic — level: structure
- What it is: Each visit follows the same pattern: chores, then silent sex, then a story broken off at 4:30 at its most suspenseful point. Habara lives for the next instalment and records only cryptic keywords in his diary. The embedded tale, the teenage break-ins, runs on its own cliffhangers.
- Why it is specific to this work: the story's form copies its namesake. The serial cliffhanger is the plot, and the listener's dependence on it is the theme.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.91] (the naming and the diary), [Men Without Women p.93] (the 4:30 break-off), [Men Without Women p.104] (an instalment cut off at 4:32), [Men Without Women p.112] (the next part deferred)
- External: https://floodmagazine.com/1881/a-new-murakami-story-has-appeared-in-the-new-yorker — notes the story borrows the framing of One Thousand and One Nights, whose heroine survives through cliffhanger storytelling.

#### 2. The withheld confinement — level: premise
- What it is: Habara is shut in with no TV, internet or newspaper. A parenthesis says there is a good reason, but it is never given. Scheherazade's role is called support activities, and Habara can't tell duty from affection. The mystery is left unsolved on purpose, so all the meaning flows into the visits.
- Why it is specific: the frame situation, the reason the hero is imprisoned, is deliberately held back. That turns the storyteller into his only window.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.93] (no TV, for a reason; can't go outside), [Men Without Women p.97] (support activities; duty vs. pleasure blurred), [Men Without Women p.113] (fear of losing her and all women)
- External: https://www.csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2017/0508/Men-Without-Women-is-Murakami-at-his-whimsical-best — describes a man trapped indoors for reasons never revealed. https://www.theartsdesk.com/books/haruki-murakami-men-without-women-review-bit-too-abstract-and-post-modern calls Habara mysteriously imprisoned.

#### 3. The lamprey — level: device
- What it is: Scheherazade casually says she was a jawless lamprey, swaying among weeds and waiting to fasten onto a trout. The image of clinging, hidden desire matches her teenage obsession. In the final paragraph Habara imagines himself as a lamprey waiting for a trout that never comes, as night falls.
- Why it is specific: one odd creature, introduced as small talk, becomes the story's closing image and the shape of both characters' longing.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.94] (the lamprey claim), [Men Without Women p.95] (how lampreys feed; her memory of it), [Men Without Women p.113] (Habara as a lamprey at the end)
- External: https://www.theartsdesk.com/books/haruki-murakami-men-without-women-review-bit-too-abstract-and-post-modern — highlights her claim to have been a lamprey eel in a former life.

### Weak candidates
- The break-in tale, with its swapped tokens and the stolen shirt [Men Without Women p.103], [Men Without Women p.110], is vivid enough to be a strand, but it works inside strand 1 as the instalment content.

### Why 3 strands
A borrowed frame (instalment storytelling), a deliberately withheld premise (confinement) and one private image (the lamprey) that closes it.

---

## Story 5. DNA: Kino (English first appearance: The New Yorker, February/March 2015, per [Men Without Women p.5])
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Philip Gabriel [Men Without Women p.142].

### Engine in one paragraph
Kino catches his wife in bed with his best friend, quits his job without a scene, and opens a small jazz bar in his aunt's old coffee shop under a willow tree. A gray cat sleeps there and a quiet regular named Kamita reads at the counter. Kino never lets himself feel the hurt. The cat leaves, snakes gather, and Kamita tells him that not doing wrong isn't enough and sends him away. In a business hotel, someone knocks at his door and then at his eighth-floor window, and he finally admits he is deeply hurt.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. The unfelt hurt as an opening for darkness — level: theme
- What it is: Kino skips the pain. He shuts the door on the adultery, accepts the apology politely, and tells himself he was only somewhat hurt. The story argues that this emptiness, rather than any wrong he did, is the gap the dark finds. Healing begins only when he says he is hurt and weeps.
- Why it is specific to this work: the moral is about a sin of omission. Not doing the right thing, here not feeling, is what gets punished.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.117] (he lowers his head and leaves), [Men Without Women p.130] (the apology accepted; only somewhat hurt), [Men Without Women p.134] (Kamita: not doing wrong is not enough), [Men Without Women p.140] (he admits he stifled the pain), [Men Without Women p.142] (he is deeply hurt and weeps)
- External: https://www.theartsdesk.com/books/haruki-murakami-men-without-women-review-bit-too-abstract-and-post-modern — singles out the ending where Kino recognizes his hurt over his wife's affair. https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2015/02/16/haruki-murakami-kino/ — reader comments read the snakes as an image of repressed sorrow (reader opinion, not criticism).

#### 2. The bar as a guarded sanctuary — level: device
- What it is: The bar is built as a haven: old jazz records, the regular's ritual drink, a sleeping cat as a good-luck charm, a willow out front, and Kamita, who turns out to have been placed there by Kino's aunt. When the protection fails, the cat goes first and then snakes appear. The aunt says snakes are ambiguous guides that hide their hearts outside their bodies, and in his last hours Kino understands the willow and Kamita as his protectors.
- Why it is specific: the setting is the story's moral machinery. Its guardians and invaders track the state of Kino's heart.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.118] (building the bar), [Men Without Women p.131] (the cat leaves, the snakes come), [Men Without Women p.133] (snakes as ambiguous; the pet door nailed shut), [Men Without Women p.141] (Kamita and the willow)
- External: https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2015/02/16/haruki-murakami-kino/ — commenters read the bar as a picture of Kino's mind and Kamita as a guide figure (reader comments).

#### 3. A realist chronicle that tips into a siege — level: structure
- What it is: Most of the story is a calm, almost documentary record of a bar's life. Then it shifts: the cat leaves, Kamita gives his warning, Kino goes into exile with a strict rule to send postcards without any message, and he breaks the rule. The climax is a knocking at the door and then at a window eight floors up, which he must not look at.
- Why it is specific: the change of register is staged as a slow tightening, from customers to omens to rules to siege, and the broken postcard rule triggers the end.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.135] (the postcard instructions), [Men Without Women p.139] (he writes a message anyway; knocking at 2:15), [Men Without Women p.140] (knocking at the heart's door), [Men Without Women p.141] (the knocking at the window)
- External: https://worldliteraturetoday.org/node/3380 — says the story raises more questions than it answers and drifts into surreal self-questioning. https://www.theartsdesk.com/books/haruki-murakami-men-without-women-review-bit-too-abstract-and-post-modern praises its concrete detail and supernatural elements.

### Weak candidates
- The woman with burn scars, shown at closing time [Men Without Women p.126], is a strong scene and is echoed when Kino imagines his wife's back [Men Without Women p.131]. But it serves strand 1 (wounds seen and not felt) rather than standing alone.

### Why 3 strands
A theme (the unfelt hurt), a place-based device that makes it visible (the guarded bar and its snakes) and a structure that brings it to a crisis (realism tightening into a siege).

---

## Story 6. DNA: Samsa in Love (English first appearance: The New Yorker, October 2013, per [Men Without Women p.5]; not in the six-story Japanese edition of 2014, per https://books.bunshun.jp/ud/book/num/9784163900742)
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Ted Goossen [Men Without Women p.161].

### Engine in one paragraph
Kafka's story is reversed. A being wakes to find it has become the human Gregor Samsa, in a stripped room with a boarded window, in a house the family has abandoned. It learns to stand, eat and cover itself. A hunchbacked young locksmith comes through a city full of tanks and checkpoints to fix a broken lock. His first desire, and the wish to talk with her about the world, give him a reason to accept being human.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. The Metamorphosis reversed — level: premise
- What it is: The story opens as a mirror of Kafka's first line. Something non-human becomes Samsa, and the room where Kafka's Gregor was locked up is now empty, its lock gouged from inside. The creature seems once to have been the thing shut in there.
- Why it is specific to this work: the story only works as a sequel or inversion of one famous text.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.143] (he wakes having become Samsa; bare, boarded room), [Men Without Women p.155] (the lock gouged by some tool), [Men Without Women p.160] (whatever was locked in there must have been him)
- External: https://worldliteraturetoday.org/node/3380 — calls it a playful reversal of Kafka's The Metamorphosis. https://nagoya.repo.nii.ac.jp/records/2005132 (Hayashi Keisuke, 2023) — reports that Murakami frames it as a sequel to Kafka and reads Kafka's story as being about becoming an ordinary person.

#### 2. The human body as baffling equipment — level: device
