- What it is: Everything human is seen as if for the first time: two legs, defenseless skin, ridiculous genitals, the strange custom of clothes, words like brassiere and pray. His erection and his longing come before he has the vocabulary for them. By the end he no longer wishes to be a fish or a sunflower, because only as a human could he feel this.
- Why it is specific: the estrangement isn't a passing joke. It is the whole point of view, and love is what turns the handicap into a reason to live.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.145] (inventory of the body; why not a fish or a sunflower), [Men Without Women p.149] (people walk clothed on two legs), [Men Without Women p.155] (doesn't know the word brassiere), [Men Without Women p.161] (no longer wishes to be anything else)
- External: https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2013/10/21/haruki-murakami-samsa-in-love/ — notes his first stumbling moments learning to stand, and how the body offers no defense. The reviewer reads falling for her as what makes being human less ridiculous.

#### 3. History pressing in from outside, unnamed — level: device
- What it is: Through the locksmith's offhand remarks we learn of an upheaval: soldiers, tanks, checkpoints on the bridges, people rounded up in Prague. Samsa understands none of it. The occupation stays at the edge of the frame, and the woman's line about doing small jobs honestly to stay sane anchors the tender plot against it.
- Why it is specific: an unexplained historical crisis is seen through a newborn consciousness that has no words for it. The love scene happens in the middle of a city falling apart.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.153] (she crossed the city through the upheaval), [Men Without Women p.157] (soldiers, tanks, Prague), [Men Without Women p.160] (small dutiful work as sanity), [Men Without Women p.161] (it is all a mystery to him)
- External: https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2013/10/21/haruki-murakami-samsa-in-love/ — mentions the troubles in Prague outside the door, set against the quiet moment of connection. The reviewer does not name a date.

### Why 3 strands
A borrowed-and-reversed premise, the estranged point of view it produces, and the off-stage history that gives the small love its stakes.

---

## Story 7. DNA: Men Without Women (title story; written for the 2014 Japanese collection per https://books.bunshun.jp/ud/book/num/9784163900742)
Type: short story (in Men Without Women). Translated by Philip Gabriel [Men Without Women p.173].

### Engine in one paragraph
A call after one a.m. from an unnamed husband says that M, the narrator's former lover, has killed herself, and explains nothing more. The narrator, now married, has no facts to work with. So he writes a private mythology instead: he met M at fourteen over a half eraser in biology class (which he admits never happened), sailors keep stealing her away, ammonites and coelacanths guard his lost fourteen-year-old self, and a unicorn statue stands for lonely men. Out of that comes a definition of the condition of being men without women. It ends with M's beloved elevator music and a prayer.

### DNA strands (3)

#### 1. The unexplained late-night call — level: premise
- What it is: The whole piece starts from one bare message that leaves the narrator stuck between knowing and not knowing. He doesn't know why she died, how the husband found him, or what she told him. He lies to his wife and stays awake. Every later section is an attempt to fill that gap.
- Why it is specific to this work: the inciting event is nothing but information withheld. The story is the narrator's response to a question that has no answer.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.162] (the call; standing holding the phone), [Men Without Women p.163] (left suspended; lying to his wife), [Men Without Women p.169] (still asking why he was called)
- External: https://www.csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2017/0508/Men-Without-Women-is-Murakami-at-his-whimsical-best — summarizes the 1 a.m. call from the husband of an ex-girlfriend who killed herself, the third such ex.

#### 2. An invented mythology in place of facts — level: device
- What it is: Since he says he can't give real details, he writes the essence instead of the truth. He sets out a first meeting at fourteen and openly says it is false, and builds a world of crafty sailors who carry her off to ports, ammonites and coelacanths at the bottom of the sea, and a unicorn as the emblem of lonely men. These images come back over and over and are gathered up in the last paragraph.
- Why it is specific: the story openly swaps biography for a self-declared fantasy and keeps saying so. The sailors and the unicorn exist only here.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.164] (facts withheld; the eraser at fourteen, admitted as invented), [Men Without Women p.165] (sailors carry her away), [Men Without Women p.166] (writing about essence rather than truth), [Men Without Women p.169] (the unicorn as emblem)
- External: https://www.csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2017/0508/Men-Without-Women-is-Murakami-at-his-whimsical-best — notes he imagines a past they should have had, meeting at fourteen over a broken eraser. No external source discussing the sailors or the unicorn was found.

#### 3. Defining a condition by what is lost — level: stance
- What it is: The narrative turns into an essay that addresses the reader. It defines Men Without Women as a sudden, permanent, plural state: a wine stain on a carpet, a world where sounds and distances change. Then it catalogues what is lost, most concretely M's elevator music, which survives only as a reflex, now that his car has an iPod instead of a cassette deck.
- Why it is specific: this is the title story acting as a manifesto for the book. It names the condition the other six dramatize, and it measures loss through a playlist.
- In-text refs: [Men Without Women p.168] (you suddenly become one; the plural), [Men Without Women p.170] (the stain; the world sounds different), [Men Without Women p.172] (elevator music as space; lost along with her), [Men Without Women p.173] (the remnants and the prayer)
- External: https://www.riotmaterial.com/lonely-people-haruki-murakamis-men-without-women/ — describes becoming one of the Men Without Women as sudden and irreversible. https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/men_without_women.htm — sees the story as a somewhat forced effort to tie the collection together, which confirms its summarizing role even while criticizing it.

### Weak candidates
- The self-mocking hedge (he notices he overuses the word for probably) [Men Without Women p.170]. It's charming, but it is a voice tic rather than an engine.

### Why 3 strands
A withheld premise (the call), the private mythology written to fill it, and the essay voice that turns one loss into the book's defining condition.

---

Stories covered: 7 of 7 (Drive My Car, Yesterday, An Independent Organ, Scheherazade, Kino, Samsa in Love, Men Without Women). None could not be analyzed.
# DNA: First Person Singular (story collection; Japanese edition 2020, English translation by Philip Gabriel 2021; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))

Type: short story collection (8 stories). Each story is analyzed separately below, followed by DNA shared by the whole collection.

Page refs are to /home/claude/spotlight/books/First_Person_Singular.json (page N = pages[N-1]). Story page ranges, located by title pages and read in full: Cream p.9-20 · On a Stone Pillow p.23-32 · Charlie Parker Plays Bossa Nova p.34-42 · With the Beatles p.46-70 · Confessions of a Shinagawa Monkey p.73-89 · Carnaval p.91-107 · The Yakult Swallows Poetry Collection p.109-121 · First Person Singular p.123-131.

Magazine-publication dates for individual stories (Bungakukai; title story new to the book) come from the same Wikipedia page.

---

# DNA: Cream (Bungakukai, July 2018; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
An older narrator tells a younger friend about something that happened when he was eighteen and drifting between exams. A girl he barely knew invited him to a piano recital on a Kobe mountaintop, but when he got there the hall was chained shut and abandoned. In a tiny park nearby, in the middle of a panic attack, an old man appeared and gave him a riddle about an impossible circle, told him that working through hard things is what produces the best part of a life, and then disappeared. The story is about holding on to a question that was never answered. The event is never explained, and the old man's riddle becomes the tool the narrator returns to whenever something irrational happens to him.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The unsolved story told to a listener — level: stance
- What it is: The whole episode is set inside a present-day conversation. The narrator admits up front that he never reached a conclusion about it, and the younger friend comes back at the end to ask what actually happened and why. The narrator's answer is that intention and principle were not the point, which turns a mystery story into a story about how to live with not knowing.
- Why it is specific to this work: The listener (a surfer who thinks about waves) is there to voice the reader's demand for an explanation, and the narrator refuses it on the page. He answers with the image of ducking under a huge wave.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.9], [First Person Singular p.19], [First Person Singular p.20]
- External: https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2019/01/21/haruki-murakami-cream/ — Trevor Berrett centers his reading on this frame (why retell such an old episode now?) and cites Murakami's New Yorker Q&A, where Murakami says asking the right question can be better than getting the right answer.

### 2. The empty destination replaced by a stranger — level: premise
- What it is: An invitation with no clear motive draws the boy up the mountain with flowers, and the event turns out not to exist: a locked gate, a weed-grown parking lot, no people, no wind. In place of the expected meeting comes an unasked-for one, with an old man who sits down across from him while he is fighting for breath, speaks only in a riddle, and is gone when the boy opens his eyes. The bouquet he leaves on the bench is the only proof that any of it happened.
- Why it is specific to this work: The structure is a swap. The social appointment collapses and a metaphysical lesson arrives instead, from a person who appears and disappears like the recital itself.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.12], [First Person Singular p.15], [First Person Singular p.16], [First Person Singular p.18]
- External: https://csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2021/0406/First-Person-Singular-delves-into-lost-love-and-strange-happenings?icid=rss — Terry Hong summarizes the story as a bewildering event involving the old piano-duet partner and a mysterious old man in a park.

### 3. The impossible circle and the "cream" of life — level: device
- What it is: The old man's riddle is a circle with many centers and no outer edge. With it comes the idea that only what you earn through hard thinking becomes the essence of your life, and everything else is boring and worthless. The narrator never solves the circle, but he guesses it exists only in the mind, in love, compassion, ideals and faith. Both the circle and the "cream" idea come back word for word as the closing refrain.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's title comes from this device, and the device is what the narrator still uses, decades later, to get through disturbing and illogical events.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.16], [First Person Singular p.17], [First Person Singular p.20]
- External: https://mookseandgripes.com/reviews/2019/01/21/haruki-murakami-cream/ — In the comments on this review, one reader links the many-centered circle to Borges's "The Aleph", and others find the closing explanation of the circle too explicit. These are readers' comments, not scholarship.

## Weak candidates
- The panic attack (hyperventilation) as the doorway to the uncanny [p.15]. It is vivid, but it serves strand 2 rather than standing on its own.
- The loudspeaker car announcing death and judgment [p.13-14]. It foreshadows the old man's lesson, but it is a single passing scene.

## Why 3 strands
The story has three parts: a frame that refuses an answer, an event that empties out and is replaced, and a riddle that becomes a lifelong tool. Remove any one of them and the story stops working.

---

# DNA: On a Stone Pillow (Bungakukai, July 2018; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
A narrator who can recall neither the woman's name nor her face tells about one night with her when he was a college sophomore. She was an older waitress at the restaurant where he washed dishes. Before they slept together she warned him she might cry out another man's name, so she bit down on a towel. A week later her hand-bound book of tanka arrived in the mail, and those poems about love, death and severed heads are all of her that remains for him. The story asks what survives a person: not the face and not the name, but a few words, which outlast memory and may outlast her life.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A night with a stranger who belongs to someone else — level: premise
- What it is: The night is a chance encounter with no plan behind it. Its defining condition is that the woman is in love with a man who treats her like takeout, and she expects to call his name in bed. The narrator's job is practical: he finds the thickest towel. In daylight he barely recognizes her. He forgets the man's ordinary name, but it stays with him as an example of how much an ordinary name can mean to someone.
- Why it is specific to this work: The bitten towel and the other man's name are this story's own objects. The intimacy is real, but it is borrowed, and both people know they will never meet again.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.24], [First Person Singular p.26], [First Person Singular p.27], [First Person Singular p.28]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-2/ — The reviewer notes her unsettling warning about calling out another name, and how readily the narrator accepts it.

### 2. Her tanka set into the prose — level: device / structure
- What it is: The story places the woman's own poems (in translation) between its prose sections. Each tanka sits where it can comment on the narrator's memory: distance between lovers, the inescapable present, meeting again, a nameless ax. The book itself is a physical object he describes closely: string-bound, copy number 28, no price, no date, sent without a note.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title of the story is the title of her book, and the poems are the only way the woman speaks after that morning. Here the text inside the text is a woman's voice, not the narrator's.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.23], [First Person Singular p.29], [First Person Singular p.30], [First Person Singular p.32]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-2/ — The reviewer reads the poems closely (the stone pillow as a medium of longing, the number 28, the dust image) and concludes that a poem can outlive its author.

### 3. Words that survive as witnesses — level: theme
- What it is: The ending widens from the woman to time itself. Bodies decay, and names and memories are swept away. If we are lucky, a few words shelter through the storm and remain as shy, honest witnesses. But making such words requires laying your own neck on the cold stone pillow. The narrator suspects she may have taken her own life, hopes she has not, and admits he cannot say what remembering her is worth.
- Why it is specific to this work: The severed-head imagery of her poems turns into a statement about what writing costs. The stone pillow becomes the place where an artist offers up their own neck.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.31], [First Person Singular p.32]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-2/ — The reviewer emphasizes the narrator's sense that people age in a blink and that a few words may remain.

## Weak candidates
- The two moles beside her nose, described as a small constellation, which echoes the Jupiter poem [p.25, p.31]. It is a recurring image, but too minor to count as a strand.

## Why 3 strands
The story has three parts: a borrowed night, a book of poems that preserves her voice, and a meditation on what survives. They follow each other in that order and the story has nothing else.

---

# DNA: Charlie Parker Plays Bossa Nova (Bungakukai, July 2018; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
As a college student, the narrator published a straight-faced review of a record that could not exist: Charlie Parker, who died in 1955, playing bossa nova in 1963 with Jobim. The joke fooled the editor and some readers. Some fifteen years later the record turns up in a New York used-record shop, exactly as he had invented it. He doesn't buy it, and the next day the shop says it never had it. Much later, Bird appears to him in a dream, plays "Corcovado" for him alone on a battered sax, talks about dying at thirty-four, and thanks him for letting him play bossa nova. The engine is an invented text that keeps coming true, one step further each time.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A review of an impossible record — level: premise
- What it is: The story opens with long passages of the fake review itself, written in a jazz fan's breathless style and complete with a track list and a dream rhythm section. Then the narrator explains the hoax and how people reacted to it. The made-up thing carries enough detail that its author half believes in it.
- Why it is specific to this work: The thing the plot turns on is a piece of music criticism about music that does not exist, which no other story in the collection uses.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.34], [First Person Singular p.35], [First Person Singular p.36], [First Person Singular p.37]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-3/ — The reviewer notes that the editor took the fiction for real criticism and reads the whole story as a blurring of fiction and reality.

### 2. Three-step escalation from text to object to ghost — level: structure
- What it is: The story is built in three escalating sections divided by breaks: the invented review, then the record appearing as an object in New York and vanishing, then Bird himself appearing in a dream. Each step makes the fiction more real and more personal, and each ends in loss: the record is gone, the music can't be recalled, and only Bird's words survive, written down at 3:30 a.m.
- Why it is specific to this work: The structure follows a single invented thing as it crosses into reality one step at a time. It ends in a thank-you from the dead man the fiction brought back to life.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.37], [First Person Singular p.38], [First Person Singular p.39], [First Person Singular p.41], [First Person Singular p.42]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-3/ — The reviewer sees the vanished record as a sign that the fictional world is fragile, and links the dream visit to older Japanese tales of conversations with the dead.

### 3. The dare to believe, said twice — level: device
- What it is: The fake review challenges the reader to believe the impossible record exists and insists it really happened. The narrator repeats that same challenge word for word as the last lines of the story, about Bird's dream visit. A smaller callback works the same way: the record-shop owner's deadpan joke about an even more absurd album comes back from Bird's mouth in the dream.
- Why it is specific to this work: The repeated refrain changes the opening joke into a sincere claim, and the callback proves the dream knew about the waking world.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.34], [First Person Singular p.39], [First Person Singular p.42]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-3/ — The reviewer singles out the closing line as the story's final move.

## Weak candidates
- Bird's speech on death: the melody stuck in his head, Beethoven's First Concerto, death as both instant and endless [p.41]. It is powerful, but it sits inside strand 2.
- The smell of coffee in the dream as proof it is a dream [p.40, p.42]. A sensory device, but minor.

## Why 3 strands
The story has three parts: the invented object, the way it escalates into reality, and the refrain that seals it. Everything else is texture.

---

# DNA: With the Beatles (Bungakukai, August 2019; New Yorker, February 2020; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
An aging narrator starts from the sadness of seeing the girls of his generation grow old. He goes back to 1964: a nameless girl hurrying down a dim school hallway holding the LP With the Beatles, glimpsed once and never seen again. That glimpse became the measure of every later love. The middle of the story is his first real girlfriend, Sayoko. He shows up at her house on the wrong Sunday and spends the morning with her strange older brother, who suffers memory blackouts, and reads him the bleak last section of Akutagawa's "Spinning Gears." Eighteen years later he runs into the brother in Tokyo and learns that Sayoko killed herself without explanation. The story weighs the ideal image that never fades against the real person he never understood.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The girl with the LP as a lifelong gauge — level: device
- What it is: A ten-second sighting, with a pounding heart, sound dropping away, and a bell ringing faintly in his ears, becomes the instrument he uses to measure his longing for every later woman. He admits the memory might be idealized, and finds the album itself only so-so when he finally hears it in his thirties. The story ends by imagining her still sixteen, still hurrying down that hallway.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title object and the internal bell are unique to this story. The image opens and closes it, and it explains why Sayoko, whom he did love, never rang the bell.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.46], [First Person Singular p.47], [First Person Singular p.48], [First Person Singular p.68], [First Person Singular p.70]
- External: https://stanforddaily.com/2020/03/02/youth-death-and-murakamis-beatles/ — Roberta Gonzalez Marquez treats the hallway girl as the core image: an idealized youth the narrator longs to relive, set against Sayoko's ordinary, aging reality.

### 2. Not knowing the people closest to you — level: theme / premise
- What it is: The long middle scene is a misunderstanding. He comes a week early and finds the house empty except for the brother. The brother confesses that blocks of time vanish from his memory, compares it to a tape that skips a movement, and says he fears what he might do in those gaps. He asks the narrator to understand Sayoko for him. Years later the roles reverse: the brother is healthy and successful, and Sayoko, who seemed the least troubled of them, is dead by her own planned hand. Both men admit they never understood her.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's hinge is the brother, a minor and eccentric figure, together with the Akutagawa text read aloud (a suicide's last pages). Both foreshadow a death no one saw coming.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.59], [First Person Singular p.60], [First Person Singular p.61], [First Person Singular p.64], [First Person Singular p.67], [First Person Singular p.68]
- External: https://stanforddaily.com/2020/03/02/youth-death-and-murakamis-beatles/ — The review traces how the brother's sudden confession raises the tension, and how suicides (the teacher, Akutagawa, and one at the end) shape the story.

### 3. Pop history as wallpaper beside private time — level: structure
- What it is: The narration keeps breaking off into essay-like digressions: Beatles chart facts, the year's world news set aside in favor of getting a girlfriend, Percy Faith on the sofa, the later troubles of the film's stars (which he says are unrelated), and a homeroom teacher's suicide set beside a teenage make-out session. Pop music is called wallpaper, always there and barely heard, which raises the question of whether lives, too, are only decoration. A bracketed textbook-style question, borrowed from the school reader in the brother scene, mocks the reader's urge to interpret.
- Why it is specific to this work: The digressions are timed to particular years (1964, 1965, 1968). The comprehension question imitates the supplementary reader he was holding in the story.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.48], [First Person Singular p.49], [First Person Singular p.51], [First Person Singular p.53], [First Person Singular p.57], [First Person Singular p.70]
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/first_person_singular.htm — M.A. Orthofer notes the long digression on the film actors that the narrator admits is unrelated, and reads it as hinting at deeper connections anyway.

## Weak candidates
- The comprehension-question parody on its own [p.57, p.70]. It is a sharp formal trick, but it is a single instance, so it is folded into strand 3.
- Hearing "Hello, Goodbye" on the car radio on the day of the breakup [p.66-67]. An apt echo, but incidental.

## Why 3 strands
This is the longest story, but it runs on three things: an ideal image, a real person he failed to know, and a digressive, history-soaked way of telling. The brother episode belongs to the second.

---

# DNA: Confessions of a Shinagawa Monkey (Bungakukai, February 2020; New Yorker, June 2020; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
At a run-down hot-spring inn in Gunma, an old monkey who speaks politely scrubs the narrator's back. Over two large bottles of beer, he tells his life story: raised in Shinagawa by a physics professor who loved Bruckner, rejected by other monkeys, unable to desire female monkeys, and so driven to steal fragments of the names of women he loves, using their ID cards. He has stopped, and keeps the seven names as fuel for cold nights. The next morning the inn denies that it serves bottled beer. Five years later, a travel editor forgets her own name in front of the narrator, and her driver's license had been stolen near Shinagawa. The engine is a talking-animal fable about love as possession of a name, and about how love and loneliness can't be separated.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The name thief — level: premise
- What it is: The monkey's single odd power is taking a fragment of a beloved woman's name by focusing hard on an object with the name written on it, such as a license or name tag. The victim's name grows lighter, and some victims can no longer recall their own names. He calls it a pure, platonic act, and also admits it causes harm and comes from a compulsion he blames on dopamine.
- Why it is specific to this work: The theft of a name as a substitute for physical love is this story's own rule. Murakami returned to the creature as a sequel to his earlier story about the same monkey.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.80], [First Person Singular p.81], [First Person Singular p.82]
- External: https://www.wusf.org/2021-04-06/haruki-murakami-ive-had-all-sorts-of-strange-experiences-in-my-life — Murakami tells Petra Mayer (NPR) that he wrote this as a kind of sequel to the earlier monkey story, wondering what had happened to him afterwards.

### 2. Extreme love, extreme loneliness — level: theme
- What it is: The monkey belongs to neither world. Other monkeys mock him for talking funny, and humans can't be spoken to. His love is a perfect, secret possession that is also total isolation, two sides of one coin. His speech about love as the fuel that lets you go on living, even when it ends or is never returned, is the story's heart. The narrator later reduces it to a two-part formula and thinks of the monkey whenever he hears Bruckner.
- Why it is specific to this work: The comic premise (a monkey that loves only human women) is the exact means of staging that paradox, and the shared beer and snacks make it tender rather than grotesque.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.79], [First Person Singular p.82], [First Person Singular p.83], [First Person Singular p.89]
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/first_person_singular.htm — Orthofer reads the name-stealing as a metaphor for love and loneliness.

### 3. Testimony without proof — level: structure / stance
- What it is: The narrator is a working writer who took notes on the train but held back for five years. He had no proof (the inn denies the beer), no theme an editor would accept, and the risk of being told he was making things up again. What finally pushes him to write is a second witness who doesn't know she is one: the editor who forgets her own name after losing her license at Shinagawa. He protects both of them by telling her nothing, and ends feeling guilty.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story builds in the problem of writing it down. It asks how to report an impossible event when the only evidence is a stranger's symptom.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.84], [First Person Singular p.85], [First Person Singular p.86], [First Person Singular p.87], [First Person Singular p.88]
- External: https://massreview.org/2021/07/13/the-murakami-of-our-times/ — The Massachusetts Review essay describes this story as one that hinges on an act of testimony.

## Weak candidates
- The calm, polite small talk that greets the impossible (asking which part of Shinagawa, agreeing it's a nice area) [p.75-76]. Central to the tone, but a general Murakami habit; it is listed under the collection's shared DNA instead.
- The Bruckner bond, Seventh vs. Ninth Symphony [p.76, p.89]. A memory trigger, but secondary.

## Why 3 strands
A fable needs a rule, a meaning and a frame. Here they are the name theft, the love-loneliness paradox, and a writer's testimony that lacks proof.

---

# DNA: Carnaval (Bungakukai, December 2019; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
In his fifties, the narrator meets F*, a woman he calls the ugliest woman he has been close to. She is witty, rich and musically brilliant, and she is fully aware of her looks and uses them. They discover a shared desert-island choice, Schumann's Carnaval, and form a two-member club that listens to and ranks forty-two recordings over six months. She explains Carnaval as music of masks and the faces beneath them. Then she disappears, and he sees her on the TV news, arrested with her strikingly handsome younger husband for a fraud that wiped out pensioners' savings. A coda recalls a plain girl from a college date whose phone number he lost. The engine is the idea that every face is a mask, set out through a piece of music and then proved by a crime.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The blunt verdict and the essay on ugliness — level: stance
- What it is: The story opens with a harsh superlative. The narrator immediately qualifies it, then deliberately keeps the brutal word because it is truer to who she was. Next comes an essay-like stretch on beauty and ugliness: beautiful women are unhappy about tiny flaws, while F*'s ugliness is a whole that has to be accepted or rejected entire. He uses the image of a monkey each woman carries on her back, golden and identical for beauties, shaggy and individual for the others.
- Why it is specific to this work: No other story in the collection argues so openly about appearance, and here the argument is the point of entry. F* is introduced as a theory before she is a person.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.91], [First Person Singular p.92], [First Person Singular p.93], [First Person Singular p.94]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-6/ — The reviewer discusses the blunt superlative in the opening, argues the relationship still reads as respectful, and links it to Akutagawa's "Two Komachis". By contrast, https://dev.australianbookreview.com.au/976-abr-online/2021/may-2021-no-431/8599-cassandra-atherton-reviews-first-person-singular-by-haruki-murakami (Cassandra Atherton) calls it the hardest story to read because of how F* is described.

### 2. Carnaval as a theory of masks that comes true — level: device / theme
- What it is: The piano piece holds the story together. It is the desert-island test that bonds them (she cracks her knuckles ten times when excited), it is the subject of their club, and it is the key she offers: Schumann, a divided soul, wrote cheerful carnival masks with evil spirits beneath them, and everyone wears a mask. The narrator suspects she is describing herself, and admits he never desired her out of fear of what lay beneath. The fraud arrest makes the theory literal: her calm, expressionless face on TV, her husband's model-perfect smile like a mask. The story ends on his preferred recording, Rubinstein's, which he describes as playing in the gap between mask and face rather than tearing the mask off.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story is named after one piece of music and structured around its meaning. Even the closing line repeats F*'s comment that happiness is relative.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.96], [First Person Singular p.97], [First Person Singular p.100], [First Person Singular p.101], [First Person Singular p.102], [First Person Singular p.103], [First Person Singular p.105]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-6/ — The reviewer quotes F*'s idea that all of us wear masks, reads her as drawing out hidden darker selves, and suggests an evil spirit was hidden behind her mask.

### 3. The paired memory in the coda — level: structure
- What it is: After the F* story ends, a final section jumps back decades to one autumn date with a plain girl from a dorm. He bored her with a monologue about Art Pepper, then wanted to see her again because she was more than a dismissive label about her looks, and then lost her number forever. He calls both episodes minor side trips that changed nothing, yet says they return with the force of a night wind battering doors.
- Why it is specific to this work: The coda reverses the main story. With F* he was drawn into the mask, while with the college girl he never got past a careless label, and that turns the story back on the narrator's own way of looking at people.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.105], [First Person Singular p.106], [First Person Singular p.107]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-6/ — The reviewer reads the narrator's remark about a pair of minor incidents as the modesty of an older author looking back.

## Weak candidates
- The wife jokingly calling F* his girlfriend, without suspicion [p.99, p.102]. It shows how F*'s looks protect them, but it is secondary.

## Why 3 strands
The story moves through a blunt thesis about faces, then a piece of music that turns the thesis into masks and is proved by a crime, then a coda that turns the question back on the narrator.

---

# DNA: The Yakult Swallows Poetry Collection (Bungakukai, August 2019; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
A narrator who names himself Haruki Murakami tells, in an essay-like voice, how he became a lifelong fan of a losing baseball team at a half-empty Jingu Stadium in 1968. He sets this among memories of his Tigers-fan father, a ball that fell into his lap at nine, his mother's unexplained hoard of Tigers phone cards, and a dead outfielder. Between the prose sections are "poems" he says he wrote during losing games and self-published in 1982. It ends with a young vendor apologizing that he only has dark beer, which the narrator says is how writing novels feels. The engine is a self-portrait built from losing: learning to accept defeat gracefully, and valuing time spent rather than results.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The "I" named Haruki Murakami, with fact and invention mixed — level: stance
- What it is: This is the only story in which the narrator gives himself the author's name and real career milestones (his first novel and its prize in the year the team won, a later novel). It reads as memoir, but it contains at least one invention presented as fact: the self-published poetry book with numbered, signed copies now worth a fortune.
- Why it is specific to this work: Murakami confirms in an interview that the story is close to what happened to him, and that the poetry book is pure invention. That makes this story the collection's sharpest test of what the first-person "I" can be trusted to report.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.111], [First Person Singular p.112], [First Person Singular p.114]
- External: https://www.wusf.org/2021-04-06/haruki-murakami-ive-had-all-sorts-of-strange-experiences-in-my-life — Murakami tells NPR the story is largely what happened to him, calls it closer to an essay, and says publishing the poetry book is pure invention.

### 2. Prose memoir interleaved with comic poems and footnotes — level: structure
- What it is: The story is a collage. Prose sections alternate with titled free-verse "poems" about a dropped fly ball, a bird's shadow, outfielders' backsides, and a tiny visitors' section at Koshien. Small dots separate the sections, and real footnotes on outfielders follow at the end. The narrator jokes that poets will want to string him up for calling them poems.
- Why it is specific to this work: No other story in the collection mixes forms this way. The verse carries the humor, while the prose carries the father, the mother and the deaths.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.113], [First Person Singular p.114], [First Person Singular p.116], [First Person Singular p.117], [First Person Singular p.118], [First Person Singular p.119], [First Person Singular p.121]
- External: https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/first_person_singular.htm — Orthofer calls it the most autobiographical piece and focuses on the childhood ball whose fate the narrator cannot recall.

### 3. Getting used to losing — level: theme
- What it is: A decade of defeats teaches the narrator that life holds more losses than wins, and that wisdom comes from accepting defeat gracefully. Around this, the story quietly arranges family loss: a cold relationship with a father who sulked when the Tigers lost, a late reconciliation, the father's death, and a mother whose memory is failing. The closing image is a vendor apologizing for selling only dark beer, which the narrator offers as his own apology to readers as a novelist. He ends hoping to win while bracing to lose.
- Why it is specific to this work: The theme comes from a particular team in a particular stadium, and the dark-beer apology is this story's own way of describing the writer's position.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.112], [First Person Singular p.115], [First Person Singular p.120], [First Person Singular p.121]
- External: https://dev.australianbookreview.com.au/976-abr-online/2021/may-2021-no-431/8599-cassandra-atherton-reviews-first-person-singular-by-haruki-murakami — Atherton describes the story as devotion to a perennially losing team and to old ballparks, close to spiritual reverence.

## Weak candidates
- The father's death presented at a slant, through a night of drinking at the funeral with no feeling [p.115]. Important, but it sits inside strand 3.
- The mother's phone cards, which he uses up at rare public phones [p.117-118]. A lovely detail, but small.

## Why 3 strands
The story is a named self, a mixed form, and a philosophy of losing. It has almost no plot, so it has no premise strand.

---

# DNA: First Person Singular (title story; new to the 2020 book, not previously published; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Person_Singular_(short_story_collection))
Type: short story (in First Person Singular)

## Engine in one paragraph
Alone at home one spring evening, a man who rarely wears suits puts one on for his private ritual of strolling in disguise, and feels an odd guilt, as if he had padded a résumé. In an unfamiliar basement bar, reading a dull mystery over a vodka gimlet, he stares at the back-bar mirror until the man in the suit stops looking like him. A well-dressed woman of about fifty sits beside him, mocks his pose, says the suit doesn't suit him, and accuses him on a friend's behalf of something horrible he did three years ago by some shore. He doesn't remember it, and he flees. Upstairs the world has changed: no spring, no moon, snakes on the trees, ash on the sidewalk, faceless people breathing sulfur. Her accusation is the last line. The engine is the "I" as a costume, and fear of the other self who may have done what you can't remember.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The suit as a costume of self — level: premise
- What it is: The story begins with a harmless secret ritual: trying on unworn suits and walking around for an hour. This time it brings a feeling of misrepresentation, as if a respectable, tax-paying man were pretending to be someone. Everything that follows (the stranger's judgment that he looks like he is in borrowed clothes, her critique of how his Italian tie clashes with his British suit) attacks that disguise.
- Why it is specific to this work: The guilt arrives before any accusation does. The clothes are what start the story.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.123], [First Person Singular p.124], [First Person Singular p.125], [First Person Singular p.129]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-8/ — The reviewer connects the protagonist's unease in the suit to a guilty conscience about misrepresentation, with a public self that doesn't match.

### 2. The mirror and the "first person singular" — level: device / theme
- What it is: In the bar mirror his reflection becomes a stranger. He thinks about the forks in his life, where more often the choice chose him, and arrives at the phrase that names the book: he is simply one first person singular, here only because of those turns. Afterward he is afraid that another self, one that isn't really him, did something terrible at that shore.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story gives the collection its title and turns the narrating "I" that all eight stories rely on into a question: who is in the mirror?
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.126], [First Person Singular p.130]
- External: https://dev.australianbookreview.com.au/976-abr-online/2021/may-2021-no-431/8599-cassandra-atherton-reviews-first-person-singular-by-haruki-murakami — Atherton reads the mirror scene as the narrator questioning whether something beyond him controls his actions, and quotes the line about the choice choosing him.

### 3. Accusation without memory, and a world that turns — level: structure
- What it is: The confrontation is built from the stranger repeating his words back as questions until she makes her specific yet symbolic charge (a friend, three years ago, the shore). He refuses to ask what she means. The moment he climbs out, the ordinary spring street becomes an underworld, and her line of judgment repeats as the final sentence, as though spoken out there.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is the only story in the collection that ends with the surreal taking over completely, and it ends on someone else's verdict, not the narrator's reflection.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.127], [First Person Singular p.128], [First Person Singular p.129], [First Person Singular p.131]
- External: https://murakami.club/2021/05/05/fps-8/ — The reviewer reads the story as asking whether the harm of one's past could turn out to be one's own, notes the closing accusation, and compares the encounter to The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle.

## Weak candidates
- The deadpan correction about the drink's exact name during the confrontation [p.128]. Characteristic, but it is texture, not engine.

## Why 3 strands
A costume, a mirror and a verdict. The story is short and its three moves are clearly marked.

---

# Shared DNA of the whole collection

### A. An aging "I" looking back decades at one encounter, with memory openly unreliable — level: stance
- Every story is narrated by an older man recalling a single brief meeting from long ago (at 18, 19, in college, at 14-16, five years before, in his fifties). He usually admits right away how little he knows: no name, no face, no conclusion, possibly a false memory. Murakami called this his own form of first-person writing, distinct from the confessional I-novel, and ended the book by turning the "I" itself into a question.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.9], [First Person Singular p.23], [First Person Singular p.46], [First Person Singular p.91], [First Person Singular p.126]
- External: https://www.wusf.org/2021-04-06/haruki-murakami-ive-had-all-sorts-of-strange-experiences-in-my-life — Murakami says he reshapes and fictionalizes his experiences rather than relating them as they were, and rejects the I-novel idea. https://complete-review.com/reviews/murakamih/first_person_singular.htm — Orthofer notes the narrator's distance in time and his preference for hazy recollection.

### B. A named artwork or text as the hinge of each story — level: device
- Each story turns on a specific named piece of music or writing, often reproduced on the page: the Mozart four-hands duet (Cream), the tanka book (Stone Pillow), the imaginary Parker LP and its review (Charlie Parker), the Beatles LP and "Spinning Gears" (With the Beatles), Bruckner (Shinagawa Monkey), Schumann's Carnaval (Carnaval), the poems (Yakult Swallows), and a mystery novel that won't hold his attention (title story). Several stories place an invented text inside themselves.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.10], [First Person Singular p.30], [First Person Singular p.36], [First Person Singular p.59], [First Person Singular p.96], [First Person Singular p.114]
- External: https://csmonitor.com/Books/Book-Reviews/2021/0406/First-Person-Singular-delves-into-lost-love-and-strange-happenings?icid=rss — Hong names music and baseball as the recurring passions across the stories.

### C. Inexplicable events left unexplained, with a trace kept anyway — level: theme
- The stories refuse explanations: the locked recital hall, the record that appears and vanishes, the inn that denies the beer, F*'s unknown motives, the accusation about the shore. The narrator holds on to a small leftover instead, such as a bouquet, a poetry book, notes taken at 3:30 a.m., or a train notebook. Murakami says the strangeness itself is what gives such experiences meaning, and that fiction isn't there to analyze them.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.19], [First Person Singular p.32], [First Person Singular p.42], [First Person Singular p.84], [First Person Singular p.130]
- External: https://www.wusf.org/2021-04-06/haruki-murakami-ive-had-all-sorts-of-strange-experiences-in-my-life — Murakami says he has had all sorts of strange experiences and that their very strangeness gives them meaning.

### D. Women glimpsed briefly and lost, and how little he understood them — level: theme
- Five stories center on a woman the narrator met briefly or knew only partly: the recital girl, Chiho, the hallway girl and Sayoko, F* (and the college date), and the stranger in the bar who speaks for a woman he can't remember. Two of these women may have died by suicide, and one turns out to be a criminal. Late in each story he admits he never really knew them. This is also the collection's most criticized feature.
- In-text refs: [First Person Singular p.31], [First Person Singular p.68], [First Person Singular p.105], [First Person Singular p.129]
- External: https://dev.australianbookreview.com.au/976-abr-online/2021/may-2021-no-431/8599-cassandra-atherton-reviews-first-person-singular-by-haruki-murakami — Atherton criticizes women in the book as sexualized or existing to give the male narrator meaning, citing Stone Pillow, With the Beatles and Carnaval.

## Weak candidates (collection level)
- Responding to the impossible with polite small talk, as with the monkey [p.75-76] and the old man [p.16]. It is real, but it is a general Murakami habit rather than specific to this book.
- Closing on a present-tense image or a repeated line (Cream p.20, Parker p.42, Beatles p.70, Yakult p.121, title story p.131). This shows up across the book, but it is a technique, not an engine.

Stories covered: 8 of 8 (Cream; On a Stone Pillow; Charlie Parker Plays Bossa Nova; With the Beatles; Confessions of a Shinagawa Monkey; Carnaval; The Yakult Swallows Poetry Collection; First Person Singular). None missing.
# DNA: The Elephant Vanishes — Part A (stories 1–9 of 17)

Collection: The Elephant Vanishes, English edition 1993, Knopf (source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes). Edition used: Vintage International, page refs from /home/claude/spotlight/books/The_Elephant_Vanishes.json.

The table of contents [The Elephant Vanishes p.4] lists 17 stories. 17 is odd, so the first half with the extra story is stories 1–9.

**Stories covered in this file (TOC order, start page):**
1. The Wind-Up Bird and Tuesday's Women (p.7–33)
2. The Second Bakery Attack (p.35–46)
3. The Kangaroo Communiqué (p.48–60)
4. On Seeing the 100% Perfect Girl One Beautiful April Morning (p.62–65)
5. Sleep (p.67–97)
6. The Fall of the Roman Empire, the 1881 Indian Uprising, Hitler's Invasion of Poland, and the Realm of Raging Winds (p.99–104)
7. Lederhosen (p.106–113; its title page is missing from the ebook text, and the story opens on p.106)
8. Barn Burning (p.115–130)
9. The Little Green Monster (p.132–135)

Not covered here (Part B): Family Affair, A Window, TV People, A Slow Boat to China, The Dancing Dwarf, The Last Lawn of the Afternoon, The Silence, The Elephant Vanishes.

Method: I read all nine stories in full in the page text (pp.7–135), cross-checked them against /home/claude/murakami/notes/Elephant__part1.md, and looked for web sources on each story. All Japanese publication years come from the Wikipedia table at the URL above.

---

# DNA: The Wind-Up Bird and Tuesday's Women (1986, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
A thirty-year-old man who quit his law-office job for no clear reason spends one Tuesday at home. He cooks, irons and shops, and he is supposed to look for the missing family cat. Three women break into the day: an anonymous caller who knows him and wants ten minutes of intimacy, a sixteen-year-old girl sunbathing beside a dead-end alley who talks about a lump of death inside the body, and his wife, who suspects him and at night accuses him of killing the cat through indifference. Nothing gets solved. The cat stays lost, the caller is never named, and the phone rings unanswered. The story is about a man whose life has drifted off course without his noticing, and about the women who can see the blind spot he cannot.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Three women interrupt one passive day — level: premise
- What it is: The plot is one ordinary day of housework, and each scene is driven by a woman who calls him or speaks to him: the stranger on the phone, his wife twice on the phone and then in person, and the girl in the yard. He mostly reacts, deflects or hangs up. Each woman knows something about him that he will not look at: his age, his blind spot, his coldness.
- Why it is specific to this work: The structure of the day (the "Tuesday" of the title, set when the wife asks what day it is) is a sequence of female interruptions to a man who has stepped out of working life, and this exact setup was later reused to open a novel.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.7], [The Elephant Vanishes p.10], [The Elephant Vanishes p.15], [The Elephant Vanishes p.21], [The Elephant Vanishes p.32]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind-Up_Bird_Chronicle — the story is described as a slightly different version of the novel's first chapter.

### 2. The lost cat and the sealed passage — level: device
- What it is: The errand is to find the missing cat, which the wife named after her brother-in-law. The search leads into a back alley that has been closed off at both ends over the years, so it goes nowhere, past an abandoned house with a stone bird statue. The search for something small and domestic leads into a hidden, dead-end space behind the neighborhood, where the girl and the narrator's sleep take over.
- Why it is specific to this work: The cat, the sealed alley and the vacant house with the stone bird are the physical setup of this story (and the seed of the later novel). It is not just a generic Murakami cat.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.11], [The Elephant Vanishes p.17], [The Elephant Vanishes p.18], [The Elephant Vanishes p.20], [The Elephant Vanishes p.22]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — Walley summarizes the story as a day spent looking for the wife's lost cat among strange calls, an odd teenage girl and the wife's accusations.

### 3. The wind-up bird: a world that needs winding, a man who has run down — level: theme
- What it is: The unseen bird's call is imagined as winding up the neighborhood's small world every day. The narrator pictures himself as having drifted far out through the solar system, away from the person he was meant to be. By evening he sees the house as a wind-up toy world where only he grows old. The image comes back at the end, when he turns the cat's absence into a small poem about the bird failing to wind it.
- Why it is specific to this work: The bird is the governing image here: it measures the narrator's own running-down and gives the story (and later the novel) its title.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.12], [The Elephant Vanishes p.13], [The Elephant Vanishes p.31], [The Elephant Vanishes p.33]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — reads the wind-up bird as a metaphor for keeping the world going, which the narrator can no longer do, and his passivity as the problem his wife sees.

## Weak candidates
- Counting and timing (stopwatch during the sex call, rings counted until he gives up), [p.14], [p.17], [p.33]. It is strong in this story but it is a collection-wide habit (notes technique 6), so I folded it into strand 1 rather than counting it as its own strand.

## Why 3 strands
The premise (women interrupting a drifting man's day), the setting device (cat plus sealed passage) and the governing image (the wind-up bird) are each necessary. Remove any one and the story becomes something else.

---

# DNA: The Second Bakery Attack (1985, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
Two weeks into their marriage, a couple wake at 2 a.m. with a hunger nothing in the near-empty fridge can fix. The husband remembers that ten years earlier he and a friend tried to rob a bakery, but the baker turned the robbery into a deal: listen to Wagner and take the bread. That deal felt like a curse that never lifted. The wife decides the curse is now on her too, and that the only cure is to commit the attack again, properly. Equipped with a shotgun and ski masks that she already owns, she leads a hold-up of an all-night McDonald's for thirty Big Macs. By dawn the hunger is gone.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. An unfinished act that must be repeated to lift a curse — level: premise
- What it is: The first attack failed as a crime because it became an exchange. It stayed unresolved and quietly broke the friendship. The wife's reasoning is that a curse is only lifted by finishing what was left unfinished, so the present night repeats the past act, with a McDonald's standing in for the bakery because no bakery is open.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story is built as a sequel to an earlier act (the title counts it as the second), and its logic is ritual repetition to settle a debt from youth, delivered as a newlywed's demand.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.37], [The Elephant Vanishes p.39], [The Elephant Vanishes p.40], [The Elephant Vanishes p.41], [The Elephant Vanishes p.42]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — Walley reads the husband's job as having "sold out" and the marriage as a small space of resistance, which fits the idea of reopening a youthful refusal.

### 2. The undersea volcano seen from a small boat — level: device
- What it is: The husband turns his hunger into a numbered mental picture: he floats in a boat over very clear water, looking down at the peak of a submerged volcano whose distance he cannot judge. He checks the picture again at each turn of the night. The water grows clearer and the boat seems to hang in air, and after the hold-up, at dawn, the volcano is gone and he lies down waiting for the tide.
- Why it is specific to this work: This one private image does all the emotional reporting and gives the story its ending. It is an image made for this story, not a recurring Murakami object.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.36], [The Elephant Vanishes p.37], [The Elephant Vanishes p.40], [The Elephant Vanishes p.46]
- External: https://shortstorymagictricks.com/2017/08/30/the-second-bakery-attack-by-haruki-murakami/ — notes the underwater volcano and the narrator's link between hunger and fear of heights as key moves.

### 3. The wife as unexpected commander; the heist as a polite procedure — level: theme
- What it is: The new wife turns out to be a stranger with hidden equipment and practiced skills. She tapes over the license plate, gives the orders, pays for the Cokes and ties up the staff gently. The husband only holds the gun. The absurd crime is carried out with calm courtesy, and the couple's first real shared act is this.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story uses the hold-up to show how little the narrator knows about the person he married. Marriage as unknown territory is staged through her competence.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.35], [The Elephant Vanishes p.41], [The Elephant Vanishes p.42], [The Elephant Vanishes p.45]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — calls it Murakami's first serious look at married life.

## Why 3 strands
The premise (repeat the act to end the curse), the inner gauge (the volcano) and the marriage theme (the wife in command) are distinct and each is needed. The Wagner deal belongs inside strand 1 rather than standing alone.

---

# DNA: The Kangaroo Communiqué (1981, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
A department-store complaints clerk has to reject a woman's request to exchange a record (she bought Mahler instead of Brahms). After watching the kangaroos at the zoo, he decides to answer her personally instead. The reply starts as a letter and then turns into a rambling cassette recording that tells her her complaint is rejected, confesses that her flat, emotionless letter moved and aroused him, and admits that his one wish is to exist in two places at once. The whole story is this one-way message to a woman who never answers.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. The story is an official reply that turns into a taped confession — level: structure
- What it is: The text is the clerk's message itself. It opens as office correspondence (complaint categories, the formal verdict), then switches to a cassette with bracketed sound cues for whistling, knocking and rattling, and he speaks to the recorder's level meters as his only audience. The business register slowly dissolves into intimacy, and he even gives the message a name for her diary.
- Why it is specific to this work: Its form (a complaint reply spoken onto tape to a stranger) is unique in the collection and is the story's whole shape.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.49], [The Elephant Vanishes p.50], [The Elephant Vanishes p.51], [The Elephant Vanishes p.52], [The Elephant Vanishes p.53]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes — lists the story (Japanese 1981; first English version in Zyzzyva, 1988). https://shortstorymagictricks.com/2021/02/18/the-kangaroo-communique-by-haruki-murakami/ — reads it as a letter whose narrator repeatedly fails to write a proper reply, scattershot and self-aware.

### 2. Imperfection, forgiveness, and the wish to be two — level: theme
- What it is: At the kangaroo enclosure the clerk comes up with a principle that an imperfect message is better than none and that imperfection lets people forgive one another in a chain. He also confesses that what he hates is being only one separate person, and that he wants to exist in two places, or two selves, at once. The kangaroos, which exist only to carry on the species, are the trigger.
- Why it is specific to this work: The kangaroo-inspired philosophy of imperfection and the two-places wish are this story's own argument, and the message form is justified by it.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.48], [The Elephant Vanishes p.50], [The Elephant Vanishes p.51], [The Elephant Vanishes p.58], [The Elephant Vanishes p.59]
- External: https://shortstorymagictricks.com/2021/02/18/the-kangaroo-communique-by-haruki-murakami/ — names the coined principle of imperfection, tied to mutual forgiveness, as the story's key idea.

## Weak candidates
- Reading a stranger entirely from her letter (comma-per-period counts, a letter with a story but no self in it) [p.54], [p.55]. This is central to why he writes, but it could be read as part of strand 2 (being moved by an imperfect, impersonal text). Kept as a candidate.

## Why 2 strands
It is short and built on one form plus one idea. A third strand would just split the confession into pieces.

---

# DNA: On Seeing the 100% Perfect Girl One Beautiful April Morning (1981, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
In a Harajuku side street, a thirty-two-year-old man passes a woman he knows is perfect for him, though she is not beautiful and he cannot recall her features. He does not speak, and she disappears into the crowd. Afterwards he works out what he should have said: a fairy tale about two teenagers who found their perfect match, doubted it, agreed to part and test fate, lost their memories to influenza, and years later passed each other on that same street without a word. The story is about a missed chance turned into a myth that explains it.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. A perfect fit that cannot be acted on — level: premise
- What it is: The narrator's certainty is total and has nothing to do with looks, type or memory of her face. Every opening line he rehearses sounds ridiculous, so the moment passes. "Perfect" is a feeling of fit, set against the ordinary, partial love people later settle for.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole piece rests on one wordless passing and on a measured idea of fit (100 percent against later loves of 75 or 85), which is the story's own scale.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.62], [The Elephant Vanishes p.63], [The Elephant Vanishes p.65]
- External: iwriter.qq.com reproduction of Sam Anderson, "The Fierce Imagination of Haruki Murakami," NYT Magazine, Oct 2011: https://iwriter.qq.com/read/1022866267/5 — Murakami says 1Q84 is basically this story about separated lovers searching for each other: "I just made it long." (Original: https://www.nytimes.com/2011/10/23/magazine/the-fierce-imagination-of-haruki-murakami.html, blocked for fetching. The murakami.club page https://murakami.club/2017/09/13/100-perfect-girl/ quotes the same interview.)

### 2. The speech he should have given is a fairy tale that mirrors the frame — level: structure
- What it is: The second half is an embedded tale set off as a fable. It replays the frame scene exactly (April, Harajuku, one walking east and one west, her letter) as the end of a long fated story, so the missed encounter becomes the tragic final scene of two lost lovers. The narrator closes by saying that this is what he should have told her.
- Why it is specific to this work: Pairing a present-day anecdote with an invented fairy tale that retells it as destiny is the story's form, and it has no counterpart in the other stories in this half.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.63], [The Elephant Vanishes p.64], [The Elephant Vanishes p.65]
- External: https://murakami.club/2017/09/13/100-perfect-girl/ — reads the story as a prequel-like seed of 1Q84's separated lovers, based on Murakami's NYT remarks.

## Why 2 strands
It is a five-page piece: one situation and one formal trick. More would be padding.

---

# DNA: Sleep (1989, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
A dentist's wife, thirty, narrates her seventeenth day without any sleep. It began after a nightmare and a paralysed vision of a gaunt old man pouring endless water on her feet. Instead of exhaustion she gets clarity and energy. While her husband and son sleep, she spends the nights reading Anna Karenina again and again, drinking brandy and eating forbidden chocolate. By day she runs the household on autopilot and swims harder, and nobody notices. She decides sleep is only maintenance for the habitual grooves that wear a person down, and refuses it. Then she thinks of death as endless wakeful darkness, drives to the harbor at 3 a.m., and is trapped in her car while two shadowy men rock it.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Total sleeplessness as a secret expansion of the self — level: premise
- What it is: The impossible condition (no sleep, no fatigue) is handled with calm realism, and it gives her a private night life. She takes back her old self, the passionate reader, through Tolstoy, chocolate and brandy, while her family notices nothing. The trigger is a paralysis vision she cannot explain.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's engine is a woman who gains a hidden third of every day and uses it to recover the person marriage erased.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.67], [The Elephant Vanishes p.74], [The Elephant Vanishes p.78], [The Elephant Vanishes p.81], [The Elephant Vanishes p.89]
- External: https://japaneselit.net/tag/sleep/ — summarizes the story as a housewife who, after a sleep-paralysis episode, loses the need to sleep and stays up reading Anna Karenina, with her thoughts moving from daily life to literature and death.

### 2. The domestic machine and the refusal of repair — level: theme
- What it is: Her routine is presented as a simple machine she can run with her mind detached: buttons, levers, the same small farewell exchange every morning. From a library book she takes the idea that sleep exists to correct the one-sided wear of habit, and she refuses to be fixed up that way. The same insight turns on her family's sleeping faces, where she sees a smug sameness she now resents in both husband and son.
- Why it is specific to this work: The argument about sleep as maintenance for a worn-down domestic self is unique to this story and is what turns the premise into a rebellion.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.70], [The Elephant Vanishes p.85], [The Elephant Vanishes p.88], [The Elephant Vanishes p.92], [The Elephant Vanishes p.93]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=320 — Walley describes her days of cooking, cleaning and marriage as mechanical, and sleeplessness as making her freer and more content before the turn.

### 3. Female first-person liberation that slides into horror — level: stance
- What it is: A woman narrates in past tense, controlled and detailed, until her thoughts reach death as wakeful darkness. Then the telling shifts into present tense for the night drive and ends mid-crisis, with the car that won't start, the rocking shadows and her tears, with no resolution.
- Why it is specific to this work: The female voice, the tense shift and the abrupt horror ending reverse the story's own fantasy of freedom.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.94], [The Elephant Vanishes p.95], [The Elephant Vanishes p.96], [The Elephant Vanishes p.97]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=320 — notes the deliberate woman's point of view, reads the story as a fantasy of freedom that becomes literary horror, and cites Rubin calling it a new Murakami and a turning point.

## Weak candidates
- Anna Karenina as a mirror (a married woman's awakening, Anna's fate as a shadow) [p.77], [p.89]. The novel is clearly chosen on purpose, but the text never draws the parallel, so I keep it here rather than as a strand.

## Why 3 strands
The impossible premise, the argument that gives it meaning, and the female voice that turns it into horror are three separate engines. The story is long enough to carry all three.

---

# DNA: The Fall of the Roman Empire, the 1881 Indian Uprising, Hitler's Invasion of Poland, and the Realm of Raging Winds (1986, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
On a Sunday afternoon a man writing up his weekly diary notices a sudden gale with no forecast behind it. He takes in the laundry, gets a phone call that is all wind noise, catches himself writing a world-war event into his diary by mistake (it was only in a film he saw), and listens to music he thinks suits high winds. At 4:05 the wind stops as suddenly as it started, his girlfriend arrives to make oyster hot pot, and he notes the day's events for next week's diary as four grand historical headings.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. Grand history as code names for trivial events — level: structure
- What it is: The story is divided into four numbered sections, each headed by a part of the absurdly grand title. Each heading labels something tiny: the wind starting, a phone call drowned in wind, a slip in the diary, the record choice. In the last scene the headings become his literal diary shorthand, so the title turns out to be his own private note.
- Why it is specific to this work: The mismatch between epic labels and a domestic Sunday, used as the story's actual section structure and paid off in the ending, belongs to this story alone.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.99], [The Elephant Vanishes p.100], [The Elephant Vanishes p.101], [The Elephant Vanishes p.102], [The Elephant Vanishes p.104]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — summarizes the story as a diary-keeper whose trivia and history blur, ending with phrases from the title, and reads it as a demonstration of private symbolic systems.

### 2. The meticulous diarist versus the unexplained wind — level: stance
- What it is: The narrator records exact minutes (when the laundry went out, when the wind began, each call, when it stopped), keeps a fixed fact-to-comment ratio in his diary, and boasts of more than two decades without a missed day. The wind is never explained, and his girlfriend shrugs it off as one of the many things nobody understands. His response is to file it into his system.
- Why it is specific to this work: The comedy and meaning come from a narrator whose answer to an inexplicable event is to put it in his records, and the story ends on his creed of a system for everything.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.99], [The Elephant Vanishes p.100], [The Elephant Vanishes p.102], [The Elephant Vanishes p.103], [The Elephant Vanishes p.104]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — calls it a satire of journal-keeping.

## Why 2 strands
It is a slight comic piece: one formal joke and the character who makes the joke possible.

---

# DNA: Lederhosen (1985, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
On a rainy Sunday, while the narrator's wife is out, her sporty friend tells him how her parents divorced over a pair of shorts. On her first solo trip abroad, her mother went to a German shop to buy lederhosen for the father. The shop would not sell without fitting the actual wearer, so she found a stranger with her husband's build to stand in. Watching him prance happily in the lederhosen, she realized she hated her husband, and she never came home. The daughter could only forgive her mother once she heard about the lederhosen, and she insists they are the whole point.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. A story passed through three tellers — level: structure
- What it is: The narrator hears it from the daughter, who heard it years later from the mother, at a funeral. The frame keeps cutting back to the narrator's living room (the movie ending, cold coffee, the rain, the wife still out) and ends with his own private thought about the lederhosen the father never received.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story is built entirely as relayed testimony, and the core event is never witnessed directly by anyone in the frame. That distance is how it works.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.106], [The Elephant Vanishes p.107], [The Elephant Vanishes p.109], [The Elephant Vanishes p.112], [The Elephant Vanishes p.113]
- External: https://krm.fyi/murakami/blog/unstable-narrative — Maynard analyzes the nested narration (main narrator, the friend, the mother reached only secondhand) and reads the narrator's last question as the author pointing at this structure.

### 2. The proxy fitting: seeing a husband in a stranger — level: device
- What it is: The shop's rule (no sale without the wearer) forces a substitute body. Seeing her husband's exact shape on a stranger in silly German shorts lets the mother see her marriage from outside, and certainty arrives in half an hour with nothing happening. The object then becomes what lets the daughter forgive.
- Why it is specific to this work: A comic shop policy that produces a stand-in husband, and a garment that both causes the break and makes it forgivable, is the story's own mechanism.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.110], [The Elephant Vanishes p.111], [The Elephant Vanishes p.112], [The Elephant Vanishes p.113]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986 — reads the daughter's insistence that the lederhosen are the point as private symbolism, and notes it as an early story centered on a woman's point of view.

## Why 2 strands
A secondhand frame plus one strange object. Everything else (the mother's joy in traveling alone, the German shopkeepers' accents) serves those two.

---

# DNA: Barn Burning (1983, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
The narrator, a married novelist of thirty-one, has a platonic friendship with a twenty-year-old pantomime student who comes back from North Africa with a rich, polite, unreadable boyfriend. One Sunday, high, the boyfriend tells the narrator calmly that he burns other people's useless barns about every two months, and that he has chosen the next one, very near here. The narrator maps every barn in his area, keeps five candidates, and runs past them every morning. None burns. Months later the boyfriend says he did burn it, so close it was missed, and mentions in passing that the girl has disappeared. She never turns up. The narrator keeps running past intact barns.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The announced crime that never shows, and the woman who vanishes instead — level: premise
- What it is: A confession is made at the start, but the act is never seen. The barn the narrator watches for stays standing, while the only thing that really disappears is the girl, and the boyfriend brings this up only as an aside. The story never connects the two out loud, and the reader has to sit with the substitution.
- Why it is specific to this work: The engine is the gap between a promised act and an absence that may be the act. That gap is this story's signature and the reason it has been adapted.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.121], [The Elephant Vanishes p.125], [The Elephant Vanishes p.128], [The Elephant Vanishes p.129], [The Elephant Vanishes p.130]
- External: https://bookoblivion.com/2019/12/01/barn-burning/ — reads her disappearance as the real burning, since the barn never burns on the page. https://pen-online.com/culture/filmmaker-lee-chang-dong-adapts-a-short-story-by-murakami/ — Lee Chang-dong, who adapted it as the film Burning, says the story is mysterious although nothing really happens.

### 2. The narrator's map and running circuit — level: device
- What it is: The narrator responds with a survey. He buys a detailed map, marks sixteen barns, eliminates down to five, calculates the shortest loop, and runs it every morning for a month, timing himself. The diligence is comic and also a form of watching without acting, and he admits the idea of striking a match himself crossed his mind. The last scene keeps him on the same route a year later.
- Why it is specific to this work: The cartographic, timed jogging surveillance is this narrator's private ritual, and it gives the story its final image.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.126], [The Elephant Vanishes p.127], [The Elephant Vanishes p.128], [The Elephant Vanishes p.130]
- External: https://bookoblivion.com/2019/12/01/barn-burning/ — notes that the narrator keeps running past the barns and reads the barns as a figure for the girl and for the narrator's complicity.

### 3. Presence of the absent: pantomime oranges and simultaneity — level: theme
- What it is: The girl's skill is peeling oranges that aren't there, and she explains it as forgetting that there is no orange rather than believing that there is one. The boyfriend speaks of being in two places at once and of a moral balance that lets him burn barns without judging. Both point to a world where what is absent can be as real as what is present, and the narrator feels his own doubled awareness while high.
- Why it is specific to this work: The paired ideas (her mime, his simultaneity) prepare the reader to accept a disappearance as an event, and they are particular to these characters.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.115], [The Elephant Vanishes p.116], [The Elephant Vanishes p.123], [The Elephant Vanishes p.124]
