- External: https://hiroshima.repo.nii.ac.jp/records/2033504 — Yamane-Tano (2009) compares the 1983 text with Murakami's 1990 rewrite and argues the original is marked by ambiguity and a sense of simultaneous existence, which the revision largely removes.

## Weak candidates
- The Faulkner title. The title borrows Faulkner's 1938 story, and the Japanese original reportedly had the narrator reading Faulkner at the airport (changed in translation to magazines) per https://bookoblivion.com/2019/12/01/barn-burning/. The English text I read shows only the magazines [p.117], so I can't confirm this in-text. Not a strand.

## Why 3 strands
The missing event, the narrator's ritual response to it, and the ideas that make absence feel real are three separate supports. Remove one and the story either explains itself or loses its mystery.

---

# DNA: The Little Green Monster (1981, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes)

## Engine in one paragraph
A housewife alone all day in an isolated house watches the oak she planted as a child. A scaly green creature digs up from under the tree, unlocks her only door with its nose, and tells her (in halting, repeating speech) that it has climbed from the depths to propose because it loves her. It can read her thoughts. When she realizes its feelings show on its scales, she attacks it with her mind alone, imagining one torture after another, and it shrinks, weeps and fades until only its eyes are left. Then it is gone.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. A love-struck monster from under the childhood tree, in a house with one door — level: premise
- What it is: The intrusion comes from her own garden, from beneath a tree that is her oldest friend, into a solitary house with a single exit. The creature's open mind-reading means she has no private thoughts, and its love is an unwanted claim on her.
- Why it is specific to this work: The source (her own tree), the visitor (a monster proposing marriage) and the trap (mind-reading in a one-door house) set up this story alone.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.132], [The Elephant Vanishes p.133], [The Elephant Vanishes p.134]
- External: https://www.periodicos.ufam.edu.br/index.php/HonNoMushi/article/view/8622 — Gomes (Hon No Mushi, 2020) studies the story's grotesque, its doubles and the close bond between the monster and the central character.

### 2. Reversal: the woman destroys it with thought alone — level: theme
- What it is: Once she sees it is soft and vulnerable, her fear turns to cruelty. She kills it purely by imagining tortures in detail, which it feels. It dies trying to give her some last message, which she refuses. The real violence comes from the human, and it is done entirely inside the mind.
- Why it is specific to this work: Killing by thought, and the swap of who is monstrous, is the story's turn and ending.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.134], [The Elephant Vanishes p.135]
- External: https://www.periodicos.ufam.edu.br/index.php/HonNoMushi/article/view/8622 — argues that the character gains a power that subverts the usual logic of monster and fear, so the monster may not be what it first seemed.

## Why 2 strands
A four-page fable with one setup and one reversal. Splitting further would just restate them.

---

## Summary of strand counts
| # | Story | Strands |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The Wind-Up Bird and Tuesday's Women | 3 |
| 2 | The Second Bakery Attack | 3 |
| 3 | The Kangaroo Communiqué | 2 |
| 4 | On Seeing the 100% Perfect Girl One Beautiful April Morning | 2 |
| 5 | Sleep | 3 |
| 6 | The Fall of the Roman Empire... and the Realm of Raging Winds | 2 |
| 7 | Lederhosen | 2 |
| 8 | Barn Burning | 3 |
| 9 | The Little Green Monster | 2 |

## Source caveats
- Most external support comes from secondary or fan sources: Glynne Walley's University of Oregon J-lit blog, Short Story Magic Tricks, murakami.club and Book Oblivion. Scholarly sources are the Hiroshima University paper (Barn Burning) and the UFAM journal article (Little Green Monster, abstract only).
- I found no direct statements by Murakami about stories 1–3 and 5–9. The only direct Murakami statement used is the 2011 NYT Magazine remark on the 100% Perfect Girl, read via a reproduction because nytimes.com was blocked.
- I could not access Jay Rubin's "Haruki Murakami and the Music of Words". The only Rubin view cited (Sleep as a turning point) is secondhand via Walley.
- One analysis site (writingatlas, Little Green Monster) wrongly calls the story third person, so I did not use it. The text is first person [p.132].

Stories covered: 9 of 9 assigned (the first half of the 17 in the table of contents). None skipped. The remaining 8 stories belong to Part B.
# The Elephant Vanishes, second half: DNA analysis (part B)

Collection: The Elephant Vanishes (Knopf, English edition 1993; 17 stories; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)

How the split was made: the table of contents [The Elephant Vanishes p.4] lists 17 stories. The count is odd, so the first half takes stories 1-9 and this file covers the other 8, stories 10-17, in table-of-contents order:

10. Family Affair (p.137-162)
11. A Window (p.164-169)
12. TV People (p.171-188)
13. A Slow Boat to China (p.190-208)
14. The Dancing Dwarf (p.210-230)
15. The Last Lawn of the Afternoon (p.232-251)
16. The Silence (p.253-265)
17. The Elephant Vanishes (p.267-284)

Method: I read all eight stories in full from the JSON text, page by page. I checked them against the notes in /home/claude/murakami/notes/Elephant__part1.md (which covers p.2-256) and the Elephant Vanishes sections of /home/claude/murakami/notes/G3_ddd2_elephant2.md (which covers p.257-291). Then I looked for outside sources on the web. Every page ref below was opened and checked. The book is never quoted; everything is in my own words.

Sources for the year lines: Japanese publication years come from the table at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes. Months come from Glynne Walley's University of Oregon story notes (pages: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982 , .../stories-1982-1984/ , .../stories-1985-1986/ , .../stories-1989-1991/).

---

# DNA: Family Affair (1985, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley dates it Nov-Dec 1985: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986/)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Jay Rubin

## Engine in one paragraph
A brother of 27 and his younger sister have shared a Tokyo apartment for five years, living easily side by side. She gets engaged to a stiff, earnest computer engineer, and the brother can't stand him. Every argument in the story is really about the fiancé, and behind him about the brother's drifting, joking, womanizing life. In the end the home has quietly changed hands. Late at night the siblings make a tired, tender truce, and neither of them knows where they are going.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Fighting over the fiancé by proxy — level: premise
- What it is: The siblings never argue about the fiancé directly. They fight through other things: badly cooked spaghetti, steak versus croquettes, which records to play, what clothes to wear. The narrator points this out on the first page. Each small domestic clash measures how far the sister has moved toward a "proper" life and away from their old partnership.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole plot is built out of these displaced household skirmishes. No other story in the collection runs on a three-way family standoff like this.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.137], [The Elephant Vanishes p.150], [The Elephant Vanishes p.151], [The Elephant Vanishes p.154]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986/ — Walley reads it as an unusually conventional, family-centered story that ends with siblings who understand each other but are sadder and wiser. He compares it to Carver.

### 2. The broken stereo and the soldering iron — level: device
- What it is: The stereo has gone silent, so the narrator watches a war film with no sound, and the evening feels as dead as the set. The fiancé fixes it with a soldering iron he goes out to buy, and he tells them every home should own one. After a bad night, the narrator reflects that the soldering iron has made the apartment stop feeling like his. At the end he uses the repaired stereo to play his own late-night record very quietly for his sister.
- Why it is specific to this work: The repair is the one concrete sign of the fiancé's competence moving into the brother's territory. The same machine then carries the reconciliation.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.141], [The Elephant Vanishes p.153], [The Elephant Vanishes p.158], [The Elephant Vanishes p.159], [The Elephant Vanishes p.160]
- External: none found

### 3. The joker held up against "real life" — level: stance / theme
- What it is: The first-person brother answers every serious moment with a joke: the swordfish line, the squirrels and bears at the wedding, the made-up war years. His sister charges him with arrogance and says that living with him is not a real, grown-up life. He gives his shallow look-on-the-bright-side advice to the fiancé and then, word for word, to his sister. In between come drunk, joyless sex and vomiting in the street, where for a moment he sees himself clearly.
- Why it is specific to this work: The narrator's voice is put on trial inside the story, and the reader is invited to side partly against him.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.147], [The Elephant Vanishes p.154], [The Elephant Vanishes p.156], [The Elephant Vanishes p.157], [The Elephant Vanishes p.158], [The Elephant Vanishes p.161]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986/ — Walley notes a dissolute narrator whom the reader is encouraged to dislike. He wonders whether Murakami meant to undercut his usual first-person persona.

## Weak candidates
- The name Noboru Watanabe: the fiancé has the same name as the elephant keeper in the title story. English Wikipedia notes that the name recurs across works without the characters being connected (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind-Up_Bird_Chronicle). It is a cross-story habit rather than an engine of this story.

## Why 3 strands
This is a realist domestic comedy. Its engine is the displaced quarrel, one object carries the shift in the household, and the narrator's voice is itself what is being examined. Nothing else carries structural weight.

---

# DNA: A Window (Japanese 1982 under a different title, per Walley: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982 ; Wikipedia lists it as Mado, 1991, presumably the revised version: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Jay Rubin

## Engine in one paragraph
At 22, the narrator is paid to write graded, encouraging replies to lonely correspondence-course members. The company's rules forbid any personal contact. When he leaves the job, a 32-year-old housewife invites him to lunch and cooks him the plain hamburger steak her letter once made him crave. They spend a quiet afternoon together and nothing happens. Ten years later he rides past her neighborhood on the train, unable to find her window, still asking himself whether he should have slept with her.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Intimacy for hire: the Pen Society — level: premise
- What it is: The story opens with one of the narrator's critique letters, with the full polite format and a score. Only then does it explain the job. Members pay to write letters and get them corrected by someone of the opposite sex. The narrator slowly realizes they are lonely and using the corrections as company. Their closeness is real, but it is boxed in by rules, grades and fees.
- Why it is specific to this work: The paid, rule-bound exchange of letters is the frame inside which every feeling in the story happens.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.164], [The Elephant Vanishes p.165], [The Elephant Vanishes p.166], [The Elephant Vanishes p.168]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982 — Walley describes the letter-critique service as in effect a lonely-hearts club.

### 2. The perfectly ordinary hamburger steak — level: device
- What it is: Her letter about making hamburger steak sends him to a restaurant that only serves themed versions. He wants a plain one made by her. She invites him by promising exactly that, and the lunch is a perfect plain hamburger. The dish stands for the plain domestic intimacy he can taste but can't have.
- Why it is specific to this work: The hamburger runs from the first letter to the last paragraph and is what the narrator remembers her by.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.164], [The Elephant Vanishes p.167], [The Elephant Vanishes p.169]
- External: none found

### 3. The window seen from the train and the question left open — level: structure / stance
- What it is: The young man's visit is told from ten years later. Her apartment overlooks a train line, and now he looks up from that train at all the windows, unable to tell which was hers. The story ends by naming its own central question, whether he should have slept with her, and admitting he has no answer.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title, the closing image and the openly stated unanswered question make the story a retrospective puzzle about a road not taken.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.167], [The Elephant Vanishes p.168], [The Elephant Vanishes p.169]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982 — Walley summarizes the ending as the narrator wondering whether he should have slept with her, and calls the story funny and melancholy.

## Weak candidates
- The Burt Bacharach record: Walley says the original Japanese title asked whether you like Burt Bacharach, so the record once carried the title. In the English text it plays during lunch and in the closing image, but it serves as mood rather than engine [The Elephant Vanishes p.168], [The Elephant Vanishes p.169].

## Why 3 strands
The story is short, but it has a distinct premise (the paid letters), one object that carries the longing (the hamburger) and a distinctive ending frame (the window from the train and the open question). The ending frame gives the story its title.

---

# DNA: TV People (1989, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley dates it June 1989: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Alfred Birnbaum

## Engine in one paragraph
One Sunday evening, three silent, evenly shrunken figures walk into the narrator's apartment, set up a TV that shows no picture, and leave without acknowledging him. His wife doesn't mention it. His coworkers step aside for TV People carrying sets through a meeting and act as if nothing happened. Ignored on every side, the narrator begins to doubt his own existence. In the end a TV Person steps out of the screen and tells him his marriage is over. The narrator is left unable to speak and feels himself shrinking.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Intruders everyone agrees not to see — level: premise
- What it is: The TV People are reduced in uniform proportion, as if photocopied smaller. They do their job without a word and treat the narrator as if he isn't there. His wife and his colleagues clearly see them but act otherwise, so he alone is left wondering what is real. Being ignored this completely makes him feel he is fading away.
- Why it is specific to this work: The horror comes from collective non-acknowledgment, not from any attack: the intruders do nothing hostile.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.172], [The Elephant Vanishes p.174], [The Elephant Vanishes p.176], [The Elephant Vanishes p.177], [The Elephant Vanishes p.181]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley reads TV as a loaded symbol of image-saturated media. He sees the narrator as the last holdout and the wife's failure to notice as part of the horror.

### 2. Written-out noise: the clock and the Sunday headache — level: device
- What it is: Sounds are spelled out as strange capital-letter strings. The narrator's Sunday-evening headache comes with dragging, scraping noises. The wedding-gift mantel clock, which the TV People move to the floor, keeps up its own ticking string. The clock's sound returns at every pause and in the last paragraph, measuring time and unease.
- Why it is specific to this work: No other story in the collection builds its atmosphere from invented sound-words used as a refrain.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.171], [The Elephant Vanishes p.173], [The Elephant Vanishes p.177], [The Elephant Vanishes p.188]
- External: none found (the earlier reading notes record this as a technique, Elephant__part1.md technique 25)

### 3. The airplane that doesn't look like one, and the wife who has gone — level: theme
- What it is: The TV Person who climbs out of the screen shows a factory where two TV People are building an "airplane" that looks like a giant juicer. He insists that painting it will make it one, and the longer the narrator watches, the more he accepts it. In the same flat voice, the TV Person announces that things have gone too far and the wife is gone. The narrator realizes the damage may have happened without his noticing. He feels he must speak or turn to stone, and at the end the words slip away.
- Why it is specific to this work: The marriage breaks down through a TV broadcast whose claims become true once the narrator accepts them, and the story ends on his loss of speech.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.185], [The Elephant Vanishes p.186], [The Elephant Vanishes p.187], [The Elephant Vanishes p.188]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley calls it a darker turn for Murakami that comes close to a descent into madness.

## Weak candidates
- Present tense throughout. It gives the story its immediacy (noted in Elephant__part1.md technique 24) [The Elephant Vanishes p.171], but several stories in the collection use present tense, so it doesn't belong to this story alone.

## Why 3 strands
The story needs its premise of unseen intruders, its sound-word texture, and its turn into a broken marriage and lost speech. Without any one of these it would be a different story.

---

# DNA: A Slow Boat to China (1980, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley: April 1980, Murakami's first short story: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Alfred Birnbaum

## Engine in one paragraph
A man of about thirty digs through his unreliable memory for the three Chinese people he has met: a teacher proctoring an exam at a Chinese school, a shy college coworker he hurts by accident, and a former classmate now selling encyclopedias only to Chinese households. Each meeting ends with something he can't put right or can't say. Out of this he builds a private, imagined China that stands for a self he has lost and a place where he doesn't belong. He ends waiting at the harbor for a boat that may never come.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Numbered memory dig: three Chinese, one at a time — level: structure
- What it is: The story is divided into five numbered sections. It opens with the narrator as an archaeologist of his own past who gives up on exact dates, then tells each Chinese encounter in turn, then closes with a reflection. A head injury from a childhood baseball game, and the odd sentence he spoke on waking, frame the beginning and come back in the last section.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story is built as a list of encounters with a single kind of stranger, and that list makes up its form.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.190], [The Elephant Vanishes p.191], [The Elephant Vanishes p.192], [The Elephant Vanishes p.202], [The Elephant Vanishes p.207]
- External: https://kansai-u.repo.nii.ac.jp/records/15975 — Guan Bingbing and Yang Bingjing (2020) argue that the story's Chinese characters should be read through its structure and narrative method, not taken as stand-ins for real China.

### 2. The wrong-way train and the thrown-away number — level: premise / device
- What it is: After a happy night of dancing, the narrator absent-mindedly puts the girl on a Yamanote Line train going the wrong way around. Waiting at her station, he finds she has taken it as a deliberate slight and concluded she doesn't belong here. He confesses that fear makes him self-centered. Then he throws out the matchbook with her number on it and never sees her again. Her remark about not belonging becomes the story's refrain.
- Why it is specific to this work: A careless loop-line mistake turns small self-absorption into lasting harm, and the line it produces becomes the theme.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.199], [The Elephant Vanishes p.200], [The Elephant Vanishes p.201], [The Elephant Vanishes p.207]
- External: https://www.jstor.org/stable/40152571 — Celeste Loughman's 1997 World Literature Today article on Murakami's short fiction takes its title from the girl's remark (title seen in the citation list at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes_(short_story); I did not read the article itself).

### 3. My own China, and the slow boat — level: theme
- What it is: The narrator says the China he has read about all his life is really his private China, a part of himself cut off by the word. Riding around Tokyo he imagines the city crumbling and his China falling over it like ash. He doesn't belong in Tokyo either, and he waits on the harbor steps for the slow boat to China.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title image turns a real country into a figure for estrangement and longing, and the story is honest that it is his imagining.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.196], [The Elephant Vanishes p.207], [The Elephant Vanishes p.208]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982 — Walley reads the three Chinese as individuals rather than representatives, and China itself as an unknowable, imagined land. https://www.mentalfloss.com/literature/haruki-murakami-novels-and-stories-named-for-songs — the title comes from Frank Loesser's 1948 song, and the piece suggests the story touches on Japanese national shame toward China.

## Weak candidates
- The proctor's speech broken up by silences, with the instruction to keep the desks clean and be proud [The Elephant Vanishes p.194], [The Elephant Vanishes p.195]. It is memorable, but it is one episode within strand 1, not a separate engine.

## Why 3 strands
This was his first short story, and it already rests on three supports: a numbered memory catalog, one damaging mistake that produces the key line, and the "my China" figure that gives the title its meaning.

---

# DNA: The Dancing Dwarf (1984, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley dates it January 1984: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Jay Rubin

## Engine in one paragraph
A worker at a factory that manufactures elephants dreams of a dwarf, a brilliant dancer exiled to the forest. An old man tells him that before the revolution the dwarf danced for the king and is still officially unmentionable. In a second dream the dwarf offers a deal. He will dance inside the worker's body so the worker can win a beautiful coworker, but if the worker makes any sound before he has had her, the dwarf keeps his body. The worker passes a horrifying test in silence. The dwarf's prophecy comes true anyway: by the end the worker is a fugitive, and the dwarf comes every night to ask for his body.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A deal made in a dream, with silence as the price — level: premise
- What it is: The dwarf can enter a body only by agreement. His one condition is total silence from the moment the worker enters the dance hall until the conquest is complete, and breaking it means losing the body for good. The climax tests the vow: the girl's face rots into maggots in his arms. He realizes it is an illusion meant to make him scream, kisses it, and wins. The dwarf warns that he can win many times but lose only once.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story runs on fairy-tale contract logic of possession, a forbidden act and an ordeal, which is unlike the other stories here.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.223], [The Elephant Vanishes p.224], [The Elephant Vanishes p.225], [The Elephant Vanishes p.228], [The Elephant Vanishes p.229]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley calls it a surreal fairy tale with a fairy-tale final test, unlike anything earlier in Murakami's work.

### 2. The elephant factory — level: device
- What it is: Elephants are made in color-coded buildings by workers who rotate through the parts (ears, heads, trunks, legs) every month. Each new elephant is assembled from one real elephant cut into parts and stretched over five, so it is only one-fifth genuine. A state monopoly tests and stamps each one. The flat factory-manual account gives the dream its everyday, working-class world: guards, gossip, a dance hall, an old tavern.
- Why it is specific to this work: The manufactured-elephant economy appears nowhere else in the collection and sets the tone for everything around it.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.212], [The Elephant Vanishes p.213], [The Elephant Vanishes p.219], [The Elephant Vanishes p.220]
- External: https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/踊る小人 — the page links the factory setting to the elephant factory in Murakami's collection 象工場のハッピーエンド (Elephant Factory Happy End). It cites his afterword, where he says he has long thought seriously about how one would actually make real elephants.

### 3. Dance as dangerous power, after a revolution — level: theme
- What it is: The dwarf's dancing can draw out feelings people didn't know they had and steer an audience's emotions. An old worker tells this as forbidden history over drinks: rumor says the dwarf's power at court brought on the revolution, and the authorities still hunt him. When the worker dances with the dwarf inside him, he feels the stars and tides and is happier than ever before. That joy is exactly what makes him a wanted man.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story treats art and pleasure as politically dangerous possession, set in an invented state after a revolution.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.211], [The Elephant Vanishes p.217], [The Elephant Vanishes p.219], [The Elephant Vanishes p.227], [The Elephant Vanishes p.230]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley notes the story's revolution setting and the possession of the narrator's body to win a woman.

## Weak candidates
- Records filed in the wrong sleeves in the first dream [The Elephant Vanishes p.210]. It is a vivid opening image of the dwarf as pure motion, but it doesn't recur as an engine.

## Why 3 strands
A fairy-tale contract drives the plot, an invented industrial world holds it, and a theme of forbidden, possessing art gives it stakes. The story needs all three.

---

# DNA: The Last Lawn of the Afternoon (1982, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley dates it August 1982: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/)
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Alfred Birnbaum

## Engine in one paragraph
A writer in his thirties looks back fourteen or fifteen years to the summer he was 18 or 19, when his girlfriend broke up with him by letter. His lawn-mowing savings lost their purpose and he quit the job. On his last job, a big, heavy-drinking widow in the hills watches him mow her lawn with his usual care. Then she leads him up to her daughter's closed, dusty bedroom and asks him to say what kind of girl lives there, just from her things. He drives off worn out, holding on to the one thing he is sure of: he knows how to mow a lawn properly.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Memory told as fiction, from fifteen years on — level: stance
- What it is: The narrator opens by working out how long ago that summer was and correcting himself. He then says outright that memory is a kind of fiction and fiction a kind of memory, a loose pile that won't stay in order however you try to shape it. The whole story is told from that distance, and it closes in the present, noting that he has never mowed a lawn since.
- Why it is specific to this work: The opening essay on memory and fiction sets up the story as a writer's piece of recovered memory. No other story in this half says this so directly.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.232], [The Elephant Vanishes p.233], [The Elephant Vanishes p.251]
- External: https://cir.nii.ac.jp/crid/1050001202801294976 — Ōtani Satoshi's 2018 study of this story (Bulletin of Nishogakusha University) focuses on its narration in the context of the magazine Takarajima. Only the title and metadata were visible to me, not the argument.

### 2. Mowing done right as the one sure thing — level: theme
- What it is: After the breakup letter, the money he has been saving has no use. He quits, but he still mows with great care: he roughs the lawn in with the machine, finishes by hand, and always picks the most distant jobs for the drive. Doing the job right is a matter of pride. At the end, exhausted in a parking lot, he decides that a well-mowed lawn is all he really wanted.
- Why it is specific to this work: A summer job is turned into the narrator's private ethic, set against a love he can't understand.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.234], [The Elephant Vanishes p.235], [The Elephant Vanishes p.236], [The Elephant Vanishes p.237], [The Elephant Vanishes p.242], [The Elephant Vanishes p.251]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley describes it as a realistic, nostalgic story built around a college-era lawn-mowing job and the girlfriend's breakup.

### 3. The absent daughter's room — level: premise / device
- What it is: The widow takes him into the hot, shuttered room of a daughter who is never seen. The calendar is stuck on June and dust lies on the desk. Prompted by her, he opens the wardrobe and drawers and gives a cautious description of the girl. When he tries to picture her, he can only see his ex-girlfriend, and he finds he can't remember her clothes or her face. The story never explains why the girl is gone.
- Why it is specific to this work: Being asked to read a stranger from her belongings, in a room arranged around her absence, is the story's central, haunting scene.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.245], [The Elephant Vanishes p.246], [The Elephant Vanishes p.247], [The Elephant Vanishes p.248], [The Elephant Vanishes p.249]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley singles out the scene where an alcoholic housewife shows him her teenage daughter's untouched bedroom. He praises its balance of unease and sympathy and notes the story has nothing surreal in it.

## Weak candidates
- The widow likened to the camphor tree, and the hard drinking [The Elephant Vanishes p.241], [The Elephant Vanishes p.243]. These are strong character texture but part of strand 3, not separate.

## Why 3 strands
The memory-as-fiction frame shapes how the story is told. The mowing ethic is what the narrator holds on to. The daughter's room is the event the story exists to record.

---

# DNA: The Silence (1991, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley dates it January 1991: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ ; first published as a new piece in volume 5 of the collected works, per https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/沈黙_(村上春樹))
Type: short story (in The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Alfred Birnbaum

## Engine in one paragraph
Waiting out a snow delay at the airport, the narrator idly asks Ozawa, a quiet business contact who has boxed since junior high, whether he has ever hit anyone. Ozawa tells the story of the one punch he threw, at Aoki, a popular and manipulative classmate. Years later Aoki used a classmate's suicide to suggest that Ozawa was a bully. The whole school then shut Ozawa out in silence for half a year. He survived by seeing how shallow Aoki was. What still frightens him is the crowd that believes without question, and he ends the story by suggesting a beer.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Airport frame around a confession — level: structure
- What it is: A passing question in a too-warm airport restaurant opens a long first-person account in quotation marks. The listener-narrator barely speaks. Short cuts back to the frame (a jet climbing into the clouds, the heavy sky, the cups of coffee) break up the account, and it ends with an offer of a beer.
- Why it is specific to this work: Most of the story is one man's monologue told to a near-stranger in a waiting room, and the frame's pauses mirror the silence of the title.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.253], [The Elephant Vanishes p.258], [The Elephant Vanishes p.264], [The Elephant Vanishes p.265]
- External: https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/沈黙_(村上春樹) — describes the story as framed by an airport conversation between the narrator and the former boxer.

### 2. One punch, turned into a frame-up — level: premise
- What it is: In eighth grade Ozawa punched Aoki in a rage after Aoki spread a rumor that he had cheated, and he regretted it at once. Years later a classmate kills himself after being bullied. Aoki lets the school connect Ozawa's boxing and that old punch to the dead boy without ever lying outright. The police question Ozawa, and the school's silent treatment follows.
- Why it is specific to this work: A single real wrong becomes the material for a false accusation, which makes guilt and innocence tangled rather than simple.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.257], [The Elephant Vanishes p.260], [The Elephant Vanishes p.261]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley reads it as a sober meditation on evil centered on mistaken guilt and bullying.

### 3. Depth against surface; the silent crowd as the real monster — level: theme
- What it is: Ozawa describes boxing as going down into a private depth, a kind of loneliness that isn't sad. On a crowded train he sees that Aoki is all surface, and that frees him. His lasting fear is not people like Aoki but the faceless group that believes them without a doubt. In his nightmares their silence seeps into everything like ice water.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title's silence is both the punishment and the moral subject, and the story ends by naming the crowd, not the manipulator, as the true horror.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.254], [The Elephant Vanishes p.255], [The Elephant Vanishes p.262], [The Elephant Vanishes p.263], [The Elephant Vanishes p.264]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley notes its distrust of shallow elites and finds the moral somewhat obvious. https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/沈黙_(村上春樹) — in 1993 it was issued as a group-reading text for high-school students by Japan's national school library association.

## Weak candidates
- None.

## Why 3 strands
The frame, the frame-up and the crowd-silence theme are each essential. Remove any one and the story becomes a plain bullying anecdote.

---

# DNA: The Elephant Vanishes (1985, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes ; Walley dates it August 1985: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1985-1986/ ; English version in The New Yorker, 18 Nov 1991: https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1991/11/18/the-elephant-vanishes)
Type: short story (title story of The Elephant Vanishes), translated by Jay Rubin

## Engine in one paragraph
A thirty-one-year-old PR man who sells kitchen appliances follows, through his clipping scrapbook, the case of the town's old elephant. The elephant has disappeared together with its keeper, and its leg shackle was found still locked. Months later, on a promising first date, he admits he may have been the last to see them. From a hidden spot on a hill above the elephant house, he saw that the difference in size between elephant and keeper seemed to have shrunk. The story is too complete to talk past, and the date goes nowhere. He goes on selling well, but he says something in him has lost its balance.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Not escaped but vanished: a locked-room impossibility — level: premise
- What it is: The narrator reads the newspaper section by section until the elephant story appears. Then he takes the reporter's evasive language apart point by point: the shackle locked with no key, a fence too high to cross, no footprints. He concludes that the elephant didn't escape but vanished, a fact the police, mayor and press won't accept. He clips everything into a scrapbook. Public interest fades, and he keeps visiting the chained, empty enclosure.
- Why it is specific to this work: It treats a vanished elephant the way a detective treats a locked room, with civic and procedural seriousness, and the title comes from this premise.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.267], [The Elephant Vanishes p.272], [The Elephant Vanishes p.273], [The Elephant Vanishes p.274], [The Elephant Vanishes p.275]
- External: https://asian.fiu.edu/jsr/mori-masaki-elephant-mori-formatted1.pdf — Masaki Mori argues the disappearance suits every party concerned. He reads it as a symptom of a system that discards whatever has no use, and notes the community forgets it quickly.

### 2. The changed balance between elephant and keeper — level: device
- What it is: Through an air vent, the narrator used to watch the private affection between the old elephant and its keeper. On the last evening he saw that their sizes had moved closer together: the elephant smaller, or the keeper larger. He felt a different, colder time running inside the elephant house. Afterward he says his own sense of balance is off, and he can no longer tell doing something from not doing it.
- Why it is specific to this work: The shrinking-size image is what actually happens in the story, and it carries over into the narrator's own inner imbalance.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.280], [The Elephant Vanishes p.281], [The Elephant Vanishes p.282], [The Elephant Vanishes p.283]
- External: https://asian.fiu.edu/jsr/mori-masaki-elephant-mori-formatted1.pdf — Mori treats the elephant's shrinking as a metaphor for the narrator's own shrinking humanity. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elephant_Vanishes_(short_story) — summarizes the theme as the narrator feeling that things have lost their balance once keeper and elephant seem to become the same size.

### 3. A pragmatic world and its unsellable things — level: theme
- What it is: At a product launch, the narrator promotes a coordinated kitchen line built on "unity", using the company's preferred English loanword for "kitchen". He admits that what a kitchen really needs can't be sold, and that "pragmatic" is a word he plays with to make work easier. The old elephant is the opposite: useless, and tolerated only until its death frees up land for the developer. At the end he sells more than ever, and the company's sales language has become his description of the whole world.
- Why it is specific to this work: The sales jargon, introduced as a joke, ends up as the narrator's account of the world, and that sets the story's tone.
- In-text refs: [The Elephant Vanishes p.268], [The Elephant Vanishes p.269], [The Elephant Vanishes p.276], [The Elephant Vanishes p.277], [The Elephant Vanishes p.283]
- External: https://asian.fiu.edu/jsr/mori-masaki-elephant-mori-formatted1.pdf — Mori's core reading, "The Creature Disappears for Our Convenience", is that the story critiques efficiency and postindustrial expediency through the PR man's job.

## Weak candidates
- A story split into two halves: civic satire and news analysis in the first, the date in the hotel bar in the second. Mori argues the mismatch is deliberate and reflects how divided the narrator is. It is real, but it follows from strands 1 and 3, so I don't list it separately [The Elephant Vanishes p.275], [The Elephant Vanishes p.278].
- Telling a story that is too complete at the wrong moment. The date dies because the elephant story leaves no opening for conversation [The Elephant Vanishes p.278], [The Elephant Vanishes p.282]. This is an important beat, but it is the means by which strand 2 reaches the present, not a separate engine.

## Why 3 strands
The impossible disappearance, the shrinking balance and the pragmatic-world theme are the three things readers and critics (Mori) recognize as the story. The two-part structure and the failed date grow out of these.

---

Stories covered: 8 of 8 assigned (stories 10-17 of 17 in the table of contents). None were left unanalyzed.
# DNA: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman — Part A (first half of the stories)

Collection: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman (English edition 2006; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman). Translators: Philip Gabriel and Jay Rubin.

Table of contents (book p.3-4) lists 24 stories. 24 is even, so the first half is stories 1-12. Stories covered in this file:
1. Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman (book p.9-24)
2. Birthday Girl (p.25-38)
3. New York Mining Disaster (p.39-50)
4. Airplane: Or, How He Talked to Himself as If Reciting Poetry (p.51-59)
5. The Mirror (p.60-64)
6. A Folklore for My Generation: A Pre-History of Late-Stage Capitalism (p.65-84)
7. Hunting Knife (p.85-99)
8. A Perfect Day for Kangaroos (p.100-105)
9. Dabchick (p.106-113)
10. Man-Eating Cats (p.114-129)
11. A "Poor Aunt" Story (p.130-148)
12. Nausea 1979 (p.149-159)

Method: every story was read in full from the JSON text (pages 9-159), cross-checked against /home/claude/murakami/notes/Blind_Willow__part1.md. The author's "Introduction to the English Edition" (p.5-8) is used only as a source of Murakami's own comments, paraphrased and cited as [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.N].

Recurring external sources:
- Wikipedia story table (years written/published): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman
- Glynne Walley's story-by-story reading notes (University of Oregon J-lit blog), one page per period:
  - 1980-82: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/
  - 1982-84: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/
  - 1989-91: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/
  - 1995-96: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326
- Kirkus review of the collection (Sept. 1, 2006): https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman
