# DNA: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman (first version 1983, rewritten 1995 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A stalled twenty-five-year-old takes his partly deaf fourteen-year-old cousin to a new hospital. Sitting in its cafeteria, he slides back to another hospital visit eight years earlier. A friend's girlfriend drew and told an invented fable there: pollen-dusted flies crawl into a sleeping woman's ear, and a young man climbs a hill to save her. The friend died not long after. The fable's ear and the cousin's ear merge, and the narrator realizes, too late, what he and his friend failed to notice that summer. The story runs on that fold between two hospitals and on a quiet reckoning with harm nobody saw happening.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Two hospitals folded into one — level: structure
- What it is: The present-day trip (bus, cafeteria, bench, bus back) is the outer track. A matching setting (a hospital cafeteria with the sea beyond the window) opens a memory of the earlier visit. The memory stalls and resumes, and each return to the present is marked by a clock time.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole story is built from exactly two hospital visits that echo each other detail by detail, and the narrator even gets a false sense of having seen this cafeteria before.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.15], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.16], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.17], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.19]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326 — Walley reads it as a gentle bonding trip that blends into the memory of the earlier hospital visit. He asks whether the girlfriend died and whether the narrator's carelessness played a part.

### 2. The blind-willow fable inside the story — level: device
- What it is: The girlfriend invents a tree whose pollen-covered flies enter a woman's ear, put her to sleep and eat her from inside. A young man climbs toward her, and the girl insists he is not her boyfriend. Later, looking into his cousin's ear, the narrator pictures those flies nesting in the dark hole.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title, the ear motif and the rescue that comes too late all come from this one embedded tale, so the cousin's deafness and the old memory share a single image.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.18], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.19], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.22], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.23]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326 — Walley says this story best explains Murakami's interest in ears, which it treats as dark openings into the head, like wells.

### 3. Unseen harm and belated guilt — level: theme
- What it is: The cousin quotes a half-remembered western line: if you can see the enemy, they are not really there. The narrator recalls the box of chocolates that melted on the beach while he and his friend lazed, and he names it their careless self-absorption. He freezes on the bench, caught in a place where visible things do not exist and invisible ones do, before he steps onto the real bus and tells his cousin he is all right.
- Why it is specific to this work: The guilt attaches to one tiny, concrete failure of attention (the melted chocolates). The story's last word on it is a sentence about the visible and the invisible.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.21], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.22], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.23], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.24]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus says the narrator's relationship with his deaf cousin brings him a sharper sense of other people's suffering and of his own alienation.

## Weak candidates
- The bus full of identical, sunburned elderly hikers on a route with no hiking trail (p.12, p.14). The narrator raises it, finds no explanation and drops it. It adds to the mood, but it is not the engine.
- The narrator's stalled life (job, girlfriend, grandmother all lost that spring; p.13). Murakami uses this setup often, so it is not specific enough.
- Murakami's comment: the story was heavily revised before translation, so the English version differs a lot from the Japanese original [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7-8]. That is context, not a strand.

## Why 3 strands
The story stands on its frame (two hospitals), its embedded fable and the guilt that connects them. Take any one away and the story collapses.

---

# DNA: Birthday Girl (2002 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story))
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
On her twentieth birthday a waitress fills in for a manager who suddenly falls ill. She carries the nightly chicken dinner up to room 604, where the restaurant's owner lives and no one ever sees him. The neat old man offers her one wish as a birthday present. She makes it, and the reader never learns what it was. More than ten years later she tells this at a bar to the narrator. Now married with children, she says only that the wish both did and did not come true. Then she turns the question on him.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. One wish, granted by an unseen owner — level: premise
- What it is: The restaurant's ritual is fixed: a chicken dinner goes up to room 604 at eight every night, and only the manager has ever seen the owner. A chance substitution sends the birthday girl up instead. The courtly old man treats the coincidence as meaningful and grants one irrevocable wish, sealed with a single clap.
- Why it is specific to this work: The magic is tied to two exact things: the twentieth birthday (the coming-of-age year in Japan when the story was written) and the old man's mysterious standing dinner order.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.26], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.27], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.28], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.32], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.34]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story) — Murakami wrote it for his anthology of birthday stories. The age of majority in Japan was twenty when it was written. Murakami confirms the anthology origin himself [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7].

### 2. The withheld wish — level: device
- What it is: A section break cuts exactly where she states the wish. The reader sees only the old man's mild surprise that a girl her age would ask for that rather than beauty or money. In the frame she will say only that it came true yes and no, that time plays a part, and that people can never become anything other than themselves.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole story is shaped around one blank: the wish. Its contents can only be guessed from the reactions around it.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.33], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.34], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.36], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.37]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story) — The article notes the wish is never revealed, which follows the custom of keeping birthday wishes secret. Also https://www.josai.ac.jp/josai_lab/628/ — a contextual analysis says the hints point to an unusual, non-material wish that takes a long time to come true.

### 3. Bar-side frame that turns on the listener — level: structure
- What it is: The waitress tells the story years later to the narrator, while fiddling with a paper coaster. The past events alternate with brief returns to the bar. At the end she asks what he would have wished for. He cannot think of anything, and she says that is because he already made his wish. A closing coda replays the old man's raised finger, now in the present tense.
- Why it is specific to this work: The frame does more than deliver the story. It hands the wish question to the listener (and the reader), and the coda keeps the old man frozen in time.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.26], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.35], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.37], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.38]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthday_Girl_(short_story) — The article calls it a story-within-a-story and notes that the teller turns the question back on her friend.

## Weak candidates
- The rain and the restaurant's fixed routines (memorized daily specials, the cashier who never changes) give atmosphere (p.27). They support the premise but are not a separate strand.

## Why 3 strands
Premise, blank and frame each do separate work. The wish exists to be withheld, and the frame exists to pass that blank on to the listener.

---

# DNA: New York Mining Disaster (written 1980/1981, per https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 3/81)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
At twenty-eight the narrator lives through a year of funerals. Five friends die, and each time he borrows the same black suit from a friend who visits the zoo during typhoons. The story is a loose collage: the typhoon-zoo ritual, the deaths reported flatly one after another, an evening of switching a TV off and on, and a New Year's Eve stranger who says she once killed a man who looked just like him. Then it cuts, with no link, to coal miners trapped in the dark, waiting for rescuers to dig through. Murakami counts it among the first three short stories he wrote [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.5].

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The Year of Funerals and the borrowed suit — level: premise
- What it is: Friends in their late twenties die in a run: a suicide in a bathtub, an oil-field accident, a heart attack during a nap, car crashes. The narrator borrows the zoo friend's never-used funeral suit for every service, because buying his own would feel like accepting that people die.
- Why it is specific to this work: The suit goes back and forth between the two men and makes the death count physical. That arrangement drives the story's scenes.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.40], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.41], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.42], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.43]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/ — Walley calls it moody and impressionistic, with reflections on death that stay unresolved but may still move the reader.

### 2. Collage of fragments with an unrelated coda — level: structure
- What it is: The sections don't follow from one another: the typhoon zoo, the funerals, the TV, the New Year's Eve woman. The story ends with a short scene that nothing introduces: miners who have put out their lamps to save air, listening for pickaxes, while reality slips away from them.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title points only at that last scene, so the reader has to link the miners to the living friends who are waiting through a year of deaths.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.39], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.45], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.47], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.50]
- External: https://shortstorymagictricks.com/2018/12/31/new-york-mining-disaster-by-haruki-murakami/ — The post notes that the sections seem unrelated but connect by the end, and that the title comes from a Bee Gees song.

### 3. Deaths you can't see or smell — level: theme
- What it is: The zoo friend switches the TV off for five minutes and points out that the newscaster never noticed. Cutting the connection makes one side stop existing. He adds that some ways of dying leave no funeral and no smell. The stranger's claim that she killed the narrator's double, legally and morally innocent, carries the same idea of invisible death.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story moves from literal funerals to deaths that leave nothing to mourn, and the trapped miners make that point without a word of explanation.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.44], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.46], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.47], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.49]
- External: none found (no source discusses this specific idea; Walley only notes the story's broader reflections on death).

## Weak candidates
- The friend's private ways of keeping chaos away (typhoon visits to the zoo, cleaning at 3 a.m.; p.39, p.44). Walley mentions the cleaning, but it describes a character more than it drives the story.
- The New Year's Eve woman as a doppelgänger figure (p.47-49). She could stand as a strand of her own, but she seems to serve the invisible-death theme, so she is folded into strand 3.

## Why 3 strands
The story is a premise (a year of funerals), a form (collage plus coda) and one idea they share. There is no plot beyond these.

---

# DNA: Airplane: Or, How He Talked to Himself as If Reciting Poetry ([1987] 1989 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A twenty-year-old is having an affair with a married woman seven years older. Her husband is away and owns a large opera collection no one plays. She cries, they make love quietly, and a commuter train passes whenever she glances at the clock. One day she tells him he talks to himself without knowing it, and she writes down from memory a broken little poem he said about an airplane. Both of them sit thinking about an airplane his heart might be building deep in a forest. The last line moves into the voice of the man he later became, looking back.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Speech he doesn't know he makes — level: premise
- What it is: The lover tells him he talks to himself and sounds as if he were reciting poetry. As a child she was punished until she stopped talking to herself, and she says hearts are like deep wells. She writes his words out as a fragmentary poem about an airplane. He remembers none of it, and she says some far-off part of him was thinking about airplanes.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story turns on one transcribed document, his unconscious "poem," which the title names. Their shared puzzlement over it is the closest the two lovers get to each other.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.55], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.56], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.57], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.58]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley reads the unexplained airplane as gentle rather than menacing, and as a nod to the subconscious as the source of art.

### 2. Close third person that ends in first-person retrospect — level: stance
- What it is: Almost all of the story is third person but stays inside the young man's confusion: is he in love, and why is she with him. The final line switches into a first-person recollection of that period, so the whole story turns out to be a memory.
- Why it is specific to this work: The switch in the last line changes how the reader sees the narration, which is rare among these first-person stories.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.51], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.53], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.54], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.59]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley calls the third-person narration very experimental for Murakami, though it stays as close to the man's thoughts as first person would.

### 3. Clock, train and silent amplifier as the affair's meter — level: device
- What it is: Each time she checks the time (watching for her daughter's return from kindergarten), a train goes by, as reliably as a conditioned reflex. The husband's opera albums and a huge tube amplifier no one knows how to turn on stand by in silence. Time is told by trains, and the absent husband is present only as music nobody hears.
- Why it is specific to this work: The train-on-every-glance rule belongs to this story alone and is repeated at its key moments.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.52], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.54], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.56], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.58]
- External: none found

## Weak candidates
- The image of their lovemaking as a small room hung with colored strings he doesn't dare pull (p.53-54). It is vivid but used once.

## Why 3 strands
One premise (the unconscious poem), one stance (third person that ends in a first-person memory) and one rhythm device (trains and the clock). These three make the story what it is.

---

# DNA: The Mirror (written 1981/82, per https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 2/83)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
The host of a ghost-story evening, a man who has never seen a ghost, finally tells the one experience that terrified him. In his late-sixties drifter years he worked as a night watchman at a school. On his 3 a.m. round, with a typhoon approaching, he saw himself in a full-length mirror by the entrance. The reflection was another self that hated him and began to take control. He smashed it with his kendo sword. In the morning there was no mirror and never had been. He ends by telling the guests he owns no mirrors.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. Oral ghost-story turn addressed to "you" — level: stance
- What it is: The story opens by sorting the guests' stories into two kinds (ghosts and premonitions) and sets the host apart from both. It explains a watchman's duties to listeners assumed never to have done the job, and it assumes they have guessed the ending. It closes with a question to them and a dry fact about his house.
- Why it is specific to this work: The voice is a performance at a gathering. The final coda, shaving without a mirror, only works as something said to a live audience.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.60], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.61], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.64]
- External: none found (the Yale Review reprint, July 2006, https://yalereview.org/article/haruki-murakami-the-mirror, prints the text with no commentary).

### 2. A double that hates you, in a mirror that never existed — level: premise
- What it is: The reflection looks exactly like him but is another self, filled with cold hatred. Its hand starts to lead his. Nothing remains in the morning except his cigarette butt and sword, and he concludes that the most frightening thing is one's own self.
- Why it is specific to this work: The horror is a self that is not a ghost, and the proof is an object that never existed.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.63], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.64]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus reads the doppelgänger as a sign that the watchman has failed or turned against his essential self. Also https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley calls it a light ghost story about confronting the self and the subconscious.

## Weak candidates
- The broken pool gate banging all night like a head nodding and shaking at random (p.62, p.64) works as a sound refrain for the narrator's split will. It is memorable, but it supports the premise rather than standing alone.
- Murakami places this among the short shorts he wrote in 1981-82 [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.5]. That is context.

## Why 2 strands
It is a very short piece: one told experience (the nonexistent mirror) in one distinctive telling voice.

---

# DNA: A Folklore for My Generation: A Pre-History of Late-Stage Capitalism (1989 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A novelist narrator born in 1949 frames a generational essay about the sixties, virginity and ideals. Then he retells, as folklore, the story a perfect former classmate told him over wine by a fireplace in Lucca. That classmate's equally perfect high-school girlfriend kept refusing sex, saying that loving him and sleeping with him were separate matters. She promised to sleep with him once she had married someone else. Years later she calls to keep the promise. They meet, touch as they did in high school, and stop there. That night he pays a prostitute. A half-remembered fairy-tale ending, a king and his retainers bursting into laughter, becomes his response to the whole thing, while the narrator says he cannot laugh.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Writer-narrator as folklorist of his generation — level: stance
- What it is: The story begins as an essay on what was special about the sixties. The narrator then sets out his method: the friend was a poor storyteller, so he has rearranged the pieces, filled in some details and changed facts to protect people. Getting the tone right, he says, is what makes a story true. The retelling is framed as one generation's shared tale.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title and the method paragraph present a private romance as folklore for a whole generation. The narrator's closing judgment speaks for everyone of that age.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.65], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.66], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.69], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.83]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley sets the grand title against a small story, sees it as backward-looking like Norwegian Wood and emblematic of the author's generation, and asks whether it is about lost idealism or compromise.

### 2. The promise to sleep with him after marrying someone else — level: premise
- What it is: The model couple share everything except sex. She keeps saying that loving him and sleeping with him are separate matters. Her strange compromise is a promise to sleep with him only after she is someone else's wife. When she finally keeps it, they find that the possibility has gone, sealed and frozen, and they stop at touching.
- Why it is specific to this work: The plot hangs on one odd, binding promise that is kept and then quietly emptied when the moment comes.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.72], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.74], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.77], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.81], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.82]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley summarizes it as a small story of a classmate's failed romance.

### 3. The fairy-tale ending as refrain: the sad is also comic — level: device
- What it is: Instead of describing what happened in her apartment, the friend recalls the only line he remembers from a childhood fairy tale: when it was all over, the king and his men burst out laughing. He repeats it at the end as a reflex and says very sad things always contain something comic. The narrator closes by saying he could not laugh then and cannot now.
- Why it is specific to this work: A borrowed line takes the place of the climax, and the frame ends on the gap between the teller's laugh and the listener's refusal to laugh.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.82], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.83]
- External: none found

## Weak candidates
- The Lucca restaurant frame (fireplace, logs, espresso; p.70, p.77, p.79-80, p.83). It shapes the telling but is covered under strand 1.

## Why 3 strands
A framing voice, an odd promise and a refrain that replaces the climax. The story needs all three to work.

---

# DNA: Hunting Knife (1984 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 12/84)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
On a beach resort near an American base, a Japanese couple at the end of their twenties spend idle days between two anchored rafts while military helicopters fly over. Next door are a silent American mother and her son in a wheelchair. On the last night, unable to sleep, the narrator finds the son alone in the moonlight. The son explains how his family divides its labor: the healthy ones make money, and the sick ones produce emptiness. He shows a hunting knife no one knows he carries, asks the narrator to cut things with it, and describes a dream of a knife lodged where his memories are.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Measured vacation stillness that breaks on the last night — level: structure
- What it is: The opening measures everything: two rafts, fifty strokes, then thirty; helicopters crossing between them; the mother and son arriving at the same hour each afternoon. The days run together until the last one, when the pattern breaks. The pair don't appear, a stranger is on the raft, and at 1:20 a.m. comes the moonlit meeting.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story builds a fixed, symmetrical routine (twin rafts, the paired nods) precisely so that its last night can break it.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.85], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.86], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.87], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.88], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.92]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus describes the narrator's odd encounters at a tourist hotel, which build to the conversation with the young man in the wheelchair.

### 2. Excess and lack: a family's division of labor — level: theme
- What it is: The son says his family splits into the healthy, who make tile and money, and the sick, who are moved from place to place like rain. He says his useless legs are the pivot the family turns on, and that lack attracts more lack while excess attracts more excess. His job, he says, is to produce the emptiness.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story names its own idea in the son's speech. The narrator later sees the knife as something that links emptiness and excess.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.95], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.98]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus reads his possession of the knife as a silent rebellion against his fate.

### 3. The secret knife and the cutting at night — level: device
- What it is: A catalogue-bought folding knife that nobody knows he carries. He asks the departing stranger to cut things in his place: bark, a kickboard, coconuts, the bar menu, finally the empty air. Then he tells the dream of a knife stuck in the soft part of his head that no one can pull out, which outlasts everything like an old bone on a beach.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title object turns the son's talk about emptiness into physical action and then into the closing dream image.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.96], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.97], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.98], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.99]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley calls the story almost haunting but says he never fully connected with it. Murakami says it was heavily revised before translation [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7-8].

## Weak candidates
- The heavy ex-stewardess on the raft (p.88-91), who later floats in the narrator's mind like mist he cannot cut (p.98). She is an important echo of excess but a secondary figure.
- The missing watch and the stubble as proof that time is moving (p.94, p.96). This is a Murakami habit across stories, not specific here.

## Why 3 strands
A routine that breaks, an idea stated outright and an object that acts it out. Those three hold the story up.

---

# DNA: A Perfect Day for Kangaroos (1981 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 10/81)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A couple waits a whole month for the right morning to see a baby kangaroo announced in the paper. When they finally go, it is no longer a baby, and the girlfriend is disappointed. Then it climbs back into its mother's pouch. The narrator watches a kangaroo family (a dried-up father, a mother and baby fused into one, and an unexplained extra female) while fielding his girlfriend's questions. They leave to get a beer. Murakami groups it with the short shorts he wrote in 1981-82 [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.5].

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. The moment that nearly passes — level: premise
- What it is: Rain, wind, a toothache and errands put off the outing for a month, met each time with a shrug at how life goes. When they arrive, the baby has grown, and the girlfriend fears she will never get another chance. The pouch scene gives back what seemed lost, briefly.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole short short is the gap between the newspaper photo and the real animal a month later, and the near-miss resolves in one small image.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.100], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.101], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.102], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.104]
- External: none found

### 2. A kangaroo family as a mirror for the couple — level: theme
- What it is: The father stares into the feed trough like a composer whose talent has dried up, still hunting for lost notes as they leave. Mother and baby become one unit at rest in time. A second, identical female has no clear role. The girlfriend's questions about protection and the pouch hint at concerns of her own.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's quiet tension sits entirely in how this one enclosure is arranged and what the couple read into it.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.102], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.103], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.104], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.105]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/ — Walley finds gendered depths in it (an ineffectual father, mother and child in perfect harmony). He wonders whether the couple is avoiding marriage or children, and whether the girlfriend fears being the extra female.

## Weak candidates
- The narrator's mildly self-mocking asides (he has never won an argument with a girl; p.101). This is a common Murakami register, not a strand.

## Why 2 strands
A short short with one situation and one tableau. More strands would be padding.

---

# DNA: Dabchick (1981 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 9/81)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A broke, divorced man follows postcard directions down an endless underground corridor to start a well-paid new job. He finds a door, a doorman in a bathrobe, and a demand for a password no one told him about. From a few grudging hints he insists the answer must be "dabchick" and wins the argument by sheer logic. The point of view then jumps to the boss, who is a palm-sized dabchick brooding about death and dentists. Told the newcomer knows the password, he remarks that he is fifteen minutes late.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. A Kafkaesque entry ordeal for an ordinary job — level: premise
- What it is: A bare, featureless corridor, a door that isn't where the instructions say, an unknown password, and a gatekeeper who has never met his superior. The narrator presses on because he is desperate for a steady salary.
- Why it is specific to this work: The bureaucratic labyrinth exists only to get a man into an office job, which sets up the comic payoff.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.106], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.107], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.108], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.109]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/ — Walley reads it as a Kafka-style story with a happy, trick ending and a lesson about persistence.

### 2. The wrong word argued into being — level: device
- What it is: The hints (connected with water, fits in the hand, can't be eaten, starts with D, eight letters) lead to "dabchick." The argument then turns on whether any dog would eat a palm-sized one. The doorman gives in. The jump to the dabchick boss shows that the word the narrator insisted on is really in charge.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title creature appears only because the narrator argues it into existence, and the final cut confirms him.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.110], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.111], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.112], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.113]
- External: none found beyond Walley's trick-ending note above. Murakami lists it among the 1981-82 short shorts [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.5].

## Weak candidates
- None.

## Why 2 strands
It is a joke with one setup and one payoff.

---

# DNA: Man-Eating Cats (1991 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 7/91)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
Their affair is exposed, and their families leave them. A Japanese designer and his lover Izumi, ten years younger, take all their savings and flee to a small Greek island facing Turkey. Every day he translates the English paper aloud to her at a harbor café. One item reports an old woman eaten by her trapped cats. Izumi asks what became of the cats, recalls a nun's lesson about a cat on a desert island, and suggests his son may remember him the way he remembers a childhood cat that climbed a pine tree and was never seen again. One full-moon night Izumi is gone. He follows bouzouki music up a hill to a celebration that may never have taken place. He feels his real self has been eaten by the cats.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Flight to the edge of Europe after an affair — level: premise
- What it is: The affair rested on near-total mutual understanding rather than romantic love. After a disastrous exposure the two pool their money over breakfast at a Denny's and leave for an island with nothing to do. There he feels no organic tie to the scenery, and his son survives in memory only as a smell of soap.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story is set at a literal border (the edge of Europe facing Asia Minor) and in a time paid for by a fixed sum of money. It is a life with no way back, as Izumi tells him.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.117], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.118], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.120], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.121], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.123]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley describes the couple fleeing their former lives and asks whether the story celebrates leaving everything or treats it as horror. Murakami says the story was reworked into part of Sputnik Sweetheart [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.8].

### 2. The news item about the cats, read aloud — level: device
- What it is: A newspaper story about cats eating their dead owner, which he translates aloud for Izumi, who loves being read to. For a moment it merges in his mind with bees licking jam. It sets off the nun's survival lesson and Izumi's question about the cats' fate. At the end it comes back as a vision of three cats devouring his real self.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title image starts as a found news item and ends as the narrator's verdict on himself.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.114], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.115], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.116], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.127], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.129]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley places the story in the collection's horror-tinged mode and notes that its brevity makes it haunting.

### 3. Vanishing under the full moon, and the self turned to plaster — level: theme
- What it is: The childhood cat that ran up the pine tree on a cloudless full-moon night and never came down returns as Izumi's disappearance on an identical night. Twice, on the plane over Egypt and on the moonlit hill, he feels his body become a plaster stand-in with no real self inside. The music that drew him uphill leaves no trace.
- Why it is specific to this work: Two disappearances on full-moon nights, decades apart, are placed side by side. The loss of a lover becomes the loss of the self.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.121], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.122], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.124], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.126], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.127], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.128]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley notes that the story foreshadows Murakami's next three novels.

## Weak candidates
- The impaled independence hero's weathered statue in the harbor square (p.117). It is a strong image of a body destroyed and forgotten, but it appears only once.
- The narrator's lost jazz-piano record collection as something to hold onto (p.128). It belongs to the dissolving-self scene rather than standing alone.

## Why 3 strands
Situation (flight), image (the cats) and the fear underneath (vanishing) are all doing work. The story uses all three to reach its ending.

---

# DNA: A "Poor Aunt" Story (1980 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 12/80)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
One bright July Sunday by a pond with two bronze unicorns, the phrase "poor aunt" lodges in a would-be writer's heart for no reason. His companion, who has a real poor aunt, tells him that writing about something means taking responsibility for saving it. By August a poor aunt is stuck to his back. Each viewer sees a different figure there: a dead dog, a scarred teacher, someone's mother. Friends drift away, and a TV show tries to make sense of him. In late autumn, after he watches a little girl punished on a train, she is gone. He calls his companion just to check that she is alive. Murakami names this among his first three short stories [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.5].

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A phrase that becomes a creature on his back — level: premise
- What it is: The words "poor aunt" arrive unbidden and leave a person-shaped emptiness. A few weeks later a poor aunt is physically on the narrator's back, weightless and harmless. Each observer sees in her the forgotten, pitiable figure from their own life.
- Why it is specific to this work: The supernatural element is a word made into a thing, and it takes a different face for every viewer.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.130], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.131], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.135], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.136], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.137]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/ — Walley reads the poor aunt as a conceptual sign whose meaning stays open to each reader rather than a fixed allegory.

### 2. Writing as responsibility and salvation — level: theme
- What it is: The companion who has a real poor aunt says she cannot write about her, and that he, who has none, may not be able to save one. On the morning show he explains the aunt as pure words, an electrode stimulating the mind. Months later he admits he still cannot write it. She tells him the poor aunt simply exists, without reasons, and that she sees only him on his back.
- Why it is specific to this work: This is a story about whether the story can be written. The writer's failure to write it, and his lack of any right to it, make up the plot.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.131], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.132], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.139], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.141], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.142]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/ — Walley reads it as a story about the writer's art and empathy, with real emotional truth in its picture of poor aunts.

### 3. Four numbered movements of essay, fantasy and one plain scene — level: structure
- What it is: Four numbered sections mix scene and essay. There is a field guide to poor aunts at weddings, a town of lost names, the aunt's perfection frozen in a glacier until the year 11,980, and a closing vision of poor aunts in vinegar bottles. The release comes in an ordinary realist scene: a girl on a train slaps her brother to rescue her hat and is disowned by her mother. The narrator, helpless, looks at his own hands.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story wanders off into lyrical fantasy and comes back through one small real moment of a person shut out. That moment is a "poor aunt" in the making, and after it she leaves.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.133], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.134], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.143], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.145], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.146], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.148]
- External: none found

## Weak candidates
- The bronze unicorns and the cola cans in the pond, imagined as a sunken city (p.130, p.132). This is a recurring setting, part of the frame.
- Murakami says this story was heavily revised before translation [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7]. That is context.

## Why 3 strands
A fantastic premise, a reflection on its own writing and a wandering form. As one of his first stories, it carries more pieces than the short shorts.

---

# DNA: Nausea 1979 (1984 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 10/84)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A young illustrator who trades jazz LPs with "Mr. Murakami" makes a habit of sleeping with his friends' wives and girlfriends. Over whiskey he describes forty days in 1979: he vomited every day without feeling ill, and every day an unknown man phoned, said his name and hung up. Both stopped on the same date. The final call added only a question: did he know who was calling? He kept a diary, so he can give every date, meal, weight and time. The narrator offers theories, refuses to pick one, and warns that it could happen again.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Forty days of vomiting and a voice that says his name — level: premise
- What it is: A healthy man throws up meal after meal with no nausea. Calls that say only his name find him wherever he is alone, even in a hotel and in friends' homes. Doctors and police dismiss him. He decides to treat it as a contest of wills and has new clothes made for his thinner body.
- Why it is specific to this work: The pairing of a body symptom with an anonymous call is unique to this story, and the two seem linked though no link is ever shown.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.150], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.151], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.152], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.154], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.155]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley connects the title to Sartre's novel Nausea.

### 2. The diary as evidence, told to "Mr. Murakami" — level: stance
- What it is: The illustrator's unbroken diary supplies exact dates with the weather, the menus of vomited meals, his weight at three points and the minute of the last call. The listener is a writer called Mr. Murakami, who asks short questions and stays neutral, while the frame insists this is a true anecdote. At the very end the narrator notes that he himself keeps no diary.
- Why it is specific to this work: The inexplicable is reported like a lab notebook, and the author appears by name as the person hearing it.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.149], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.156], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.157], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.159]
- External: https://shortstorymagictricks.com/2018/10/25/nausea-1979-by-haruki-murakami/ — The post notes that the story is almost entirely one conversation, broken by a narrator named Murakami who adds information and quiet judgment.

### 3. Guilt as a possible cause, never confirmed — level: theme
- What it is: The illustrator suggests that hidden guilt might have caused it all, and the narrator points out that this was his idea, not the narrator's. The narrator offers other explanations (a hired detective, coincidence) and says the real question is what you learn from it. Things that start without reason can stop without reason, and the reverse is also true. The illustrator answers that it may come for Mr. Murakami next, since he is probably not innocent either.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story ends by passing the possible punishment from the teller to the listener, and keeps the guilt hypothesis open.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.157], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.158], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.159]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley frames the story around whether anyone can live entirely free of guilt.

## Weak candidates
- Trading jazz LPs as the friendship ritual and setting of the telling (p.150, p.157). It is specific in its details (Erroll Garner, Seagram's VO, timed to the last call) but supports strand 2 rather than standing alone.

## Why 3 strands
A paired mystery, a documentary way of telling it and an open moral question. The last scene needs all three to land.

---

Stories covered: 12 of 24 (stories 1-12 in table-of-contents order: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman; Birthday Girl; New York Mining Disaster; Airplane; The Mirror; A Folklore for My Generation; Hunting Knife; A Perfect Day for Kangaroos; Dabchick; Man-Eating Cats; A "Poor Aunt" Story; Nausea 1979). Every story in this half was analyzed. Stories 13-24 (The Seventh Man through A Shinagawa Monkey) belong to Part B and are out of scope here.
# DNA: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman — Part B (second half of the stories)

Collection: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman (English edition 2006; source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman). Translators: Philip Gabriel and Jay Rubin (the translator is named at the end of each story).

The table of contents (book p.3-4) lists 24 stories, so the second half is stories 13-24. Stories covered in this file:
13. The Seventh Man (book p.160-172)
14. The Year of Spaghetti (p.173-178)
15. Tony Takitani (p.179-194)
16. The Rise and Fall of Sharpie Cakes (p.195-199)
17. The Ice Man (p.200-208)
18. Crabs (p.209-214)
19. Firefly (p.215-235)
20. Chance Traveler (p.236-253)
21. Hanalei Bay (p.254-274)
22. Where I'm Likely to Find It (p.275-295)
23. The Kidney-Shaped Stone That Moves Every Day (p.296-315)
24. A Shinagawa Monkey (p.316-343)

A final section, "Collection-wide DNA", covers what both halves share. It draws on /home/claude/murakami/dna/Blind_Willow_A.md.

Method: every story in this half was read in full from the JSON text (pages 160-343) and cross-checked against /home/claude/murakami/notes/Blind_Willow__part1.md (p.3-240) and the Blind Willow sections of /home/claude/murakami/notes/G5_blindwillow2_firstperson.md (p.241-349). The author's "Introduction to the English Edition" (p.5-8) is used only for Murakami's own comments, paraphrased and cited as [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.N].

Recurring external sources:
- Wikipedia story table (years written/published, notes): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman
- Glynne Walley's story-by-story reading notes (University of Oregon J-lit blog):

  - 1982-84: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/
  - 1989-91: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/
  - 1995-96: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326
  - 2002-03: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2002-2003
  - 2005: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/
- Kirkus review of the collection: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman
