# DNA: The Seventh Man (1996 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 2/1996)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
On a windy night, a nameless man in his fifties is the last of seven people in a circle to tell a story. When he was ten, a typhoon's eye passed over his seaside town, and he walked down to the beach with his frail best friend K. A first giant wave took K while the narrator ran for the seawall instead of pulling him back. In the crest of a second wave he saw K smiling hugely, arm stretched toward him. He spent forty years in the mountains, avoiding all water, unmarried and haunted by the same nightmare. Then K's old watercolors make him reread that grin, and he walks back into the surf. The story runs on one act of cowardice and the long fear that grew from it, and it ends with a stated lesson about facing fear.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The seventh teller in a circle — level: structure
- What it is: The story opens and closes in a room where a small group has taken turns telling stories, with wind beating the house. The man is a stranger to all of them and touches his collar when he searches for words. His long first-person account is enclosed by two short third-person scenes, and at the end the wind has dropped and he states the moral to the room.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title is the frame. The listeners stay silent and nameless, so the confession works as testimony in front of witnesses, and the calm outside mirrors his own release.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.160], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.171]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326 — Walley describes the story as a man speaking at a group session, and says it states its lesson unusually directly.

### 2. The two waves and the grin in the crest — level: premise
- What it is: The quiet of the storm's eye lures the boys onto a strangely emptied beach. The narrator alone hears the warning rumble and runs, and the first wave takes K and his dog. The second wave freezes above him, and inside it K grins from ear to ear, reaching for his hand, before the narrator blacks out. The fever, the body that is never found and the recurring dream of being dragged under all grow from that one image.
- Why it is specific to this work: The engine is a single supernatural snapshot, the friend in the wave's tongue. The narrator insists it was no hallucination, and it becomes the fixed picture he must later reinterpret.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.162], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.164], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.165], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.166], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.167]
- External: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7] — Murakami says the idea came to him while he was surfing and watching the waves. Also https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326 — Walley summarizes the friend swept away while the narrator ran instead of trying to save him.

### 3. Forty years of avoidance, undone by K's watercolors — level: theme
- What it is: He moves inland, avoids every pool, river, boat and plane, and never shares a bed for fear of screaming in his sleep. After his father dies, a box of K's seascapes reaches him. Studying them daily, he decides the grin may have been light and angle, or a farewell, and that the hatred was his own terror reflected back. He returns to the rebuilt town, wades in fully dressed, collapses in the surf and finds he is no longer afraid. He closes by saying the worst thing is not fear itself but turning your back on it.
- Why it is specific to this work: The cure comes from the victim's own art. The same beach and the same kind of wave reverse their meaning, and the story makes its moral explicit, which is rare for Murakami.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.168], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.169], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.170], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.171]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/?p=326 — Walley reads the ending as the narrator realizing that K was probably not pulling him under, and the lesson as not giving in to fear. He calls the story Murakami's first printed response to the disasters of 1995.

## Weak candidates
- The storm's "eye" as a staring presence the boy imagines in the sky, which he looks for again at the end (p.162, p.171). It is a good bookend, but it serves strand 2.
- The narrator's survivor's guilt is sharpened because nobody blames him (p.167). This is part of strand 3.

## Why 3 strands
A framed confession, one terrible image and the long route back to the sea. Take away any one of them and the story loses its shape.

---

# DNA: The Year of Spaghetti (1981 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 5/81)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
The narrator declares 1971 his Year of Spaghetti. He cooks and eats it alone every day in a pot big enough to bathe a German shepherd, and he always half-expects a knock at the door that never comes. One December afternoon the indistinct ex-girlfriend of a friend calls. She is in trouble and owed money, and she wants the friend's whereabouts, which the narrator knows. He lies, says he is cooking spaghetti, and mimes the whole process with an imaginary pot until she gives up. Years later he wonders whether he was partly to blame. Five pages of comic ritual turn out to be about deliberate non-involvement.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A year named after one solitary dish — level: premise
- What it is: The narrator buys a giant pot, a timer, spices and every brand of pasta, and lets the smell of garlic and oil soak into his books, records and old letters. He believes spaghetti is best eaten alone and organizes his weeks around it. The ritual is his way of shutting the world out.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole story is pinned to one food and one calendar year. The closing joke says that what Italy really exported in 1971 was loneliness.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.173], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.174], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.178]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1980-1982/ — Walley says the spaghetti stands for loneliness, calls the story obvious but refreshingly direct, and reads it as a young man cultivating his alienation.

### 2. The imaginary pot — level: device
- What it is: To refuse the caller, he claims to be cooking. He then fills an imaginary pot, salts imaginary water and sets an imaginary timer while she talks, using the delicacy of cooking as his excuse. His voice goes strange when he lies, and the phone feels like a pillar of ice. She hangs up with a weary joke about his spaghetti.
- Why it is specific to this work: The ritual from strand 1 becomes a literal wall. The lie absorbs him so completely that it stops feeling like a lie, which is the story's sharpest image of self-isolation.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.175], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.176], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.177]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus describes a lonely bachelor whose obsessive routine lets him avoid getting caught up in other people's messes.

### 3. Mock-heroic elegy — level: stance
- What it is: The narrator tells it with comic grandeur. He sets a list of spaghetti dishes out like verse, mourns all the spaghetti of 1971 as if it were the dead, compares tossing pasta into the pot to a jilted girl burning love letters, and imagines visitors (an old girlfriend, his younger self, film stars) hovering outside the door like scraps of memory. The guilt at the end is delivered in the same half-joking key.
- Why it is specific to this work: The inflated, elegiac tone is what makes a slight episode feel like a lament for a lost year. Without it, the story would be only an anecdote.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.173], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.174], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.175], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.178]
- External: none found

## Weak candidates
- The pool of winter sunlight that shifts an inch or two during the call (p.175, p.177). It is a nice clock device but minor.

## Why 3 strands
The premise is the ritual, the device turns the ritual into a weapon and the tone makes it an elegy. The story is too short to hold more.

---

# DNA: Tony Takitani (1990 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 6/1990)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
Shozaburo Takitani, a carefree jazz trombonist, plays through the war in Shanghai, survives a Chinese prison and loses his wife three days after his son is born. An American major names the boy Tony. Tony grows up alone and draws machines with cold precision. In middle age he falls suddenly in love with a young woman who wears clothes beautifully, and their marriage ends his solitude. But she cannot stop buying clothes, and she dies in a crash on her way back from returning a coat. Tony hires a stranger of her size to wear the dresses, then cancels the plan. He sells the clothes and later his dead father's records, and he is left completely alone. A whole double biography is told like a legend, and it moves from solitude to love and back to solitude.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A life summarized like a legend — level: stance
- What it is: A distant third-person voice compresses two lifetimes (war, prison, births, deaths, careers) into a few pages of summary. Almost nothing is staged as a scene. The few lines of speech appear without quotation marks, folded into the narration. Deaths arrive in one flat sentence.
- Why it is specific to this work: The detached, chronicle-like telling matches the emotional distance of both Takitanis, so the form enacts the subject.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.179], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.180], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.182], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.188], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.189]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley, agreeing with Rubin that it is a masterpiece, notes the third-person narration without dialogue that packs a novel's worth of events into a legend-like summary.

### 2. Inherited solitude, father and son — level: theme
- What it is: The foreign name marks the boy as an outsider, and being alone becomes his premise for living. His father lives the same habitual solitude in a cheerful key. Neither opens his heart to the other, and the father's same old songs sound subtly wrong to the grown son at a nightclub. Marriage briefly makes Tony fear loneliness for the first time. Once the dresses and then his father's records are gone, he is truly alone.
- Why it is specific to this work: Solitude is passed down and named in the title. The story's last action, removing the last objects, completes it.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.182], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.183], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.186], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.187], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.194]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley reads Tony's name as a sentence to solitude and the story as a father-son study of opposites. He sees the late losses as a return to the state Tony knows best. Also https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Takitani — Murakami built the story from a thrift-shop T-shirt printed with the name.

### 3. The size-7 wardrobe and the woman hired to wear it — level: device
- What it is: The wife's compulsive buying fills a whole room with dresses and shoes. After her death Tony advertises for an assistant of her exact size and asks her to wear the clothes as a uniform. The plain young woman weeps among the dresses without knowing why. To Tony the clothes now look like withered shadows of his wife, and he cancels the job. Later he can recall only the shadow of a shadow, plus the stranger's tears.
- Why it is specific to this work: The clothes are the wife's whole presence: the cause of her death, the stand-in afterward and the way grief fades. No other story uses a wardrobe this way.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.187], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.188], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.189], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.190], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.191], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.193]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Takitani — The article's summary highlights the wife's compulsive shopping and the later film by Jun Ichikawa (2004).

## Weak candidates
- Tony's hyper-precise mechanical drawings, which his art-school peers scorn as lacking ideology (p.183-184). This supports strand 2 but is not an engine.

## Why 3 strands
A chronicle voice, an inherited loneliness and one obsessive object. Each is indispensable, and the father-son line and the wardrobe line come together in the empty room.

---

# DNA: The Rise and Fall of Sharpie Cakes (1981/82, published 1983 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A narrator who is fussy about sweets sees a newspaper ad from an old confectioner looking for a new version of its famous Sharpie Cakes, which he finds stale. He bakes a modern version, and the staff split along age lines. The company lets its sacred judges decide: more than a hundred huge, eyeless crows that live on Sharpies alone and shriek the cake's name. Fed his cakes, they tear each other apart. The narrator walks out, gives up the prize and resolves to make only what he wants to eat. It is a brief fable with a satirical target.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. A fable of the literary establishment — level: premise
- What it is: An institution worships its own tradition (invented history, a poem claimed from an ancient anthology, speeches that could have been one sentence) while asking for youth. It finally hands judgment to a cult that can tell only the real thing from the imitation. The newcomer's work splits old from young, and he leaves rather than live under such judges.
- Why it is specific to this work: Murakami himself identifies it as an allegory of how the literary world received him at his debut. The pastry company is a thin and comic mask.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.195], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.196], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.197], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.199]
- External: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.5] — Murakami says the story shows, as a fable, his impressions of the literary world when he debuted, a world he never fit into. Also https://themillions.com/2008/10/haruki-murakami-in-berkeley_14.html — a report of Murakami reading it aloud, described as a parable about the literary world's response to his first novel.

### 2. The Sharpie Crows — level: device
- What it is: Huge, bloated crows with white fat where their eyes should be live in a guarded storeroom. Raised for centuries on one cake, they chant its name, spit out impostors and, when given the new cake, peck one another to bloody pieces over what counts as a true Sharpie.
- Why it is specific to this work: These grotesque judges are the story's one image and its punchline. They turn a dispute over taste into a life-and-death frenzy.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.197], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.198], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.199]
- External: https://www.bookreporter.com/reviews/blind-willow-sleeping-woman-24-stories — Bookreporter calls it the collection's most entertaining piece, a fable satirizing the Japanese literary scene that ends with crow judges pecking each other to death.

## Weak candidates
- The narrator's deadpan running gag that the executives could have said their point in so many words (p.196). It is tone, not engine.
- A satire of consumer gullibility (Kirkus's reading). This is plausible, but Murakami's own comment points to literature.

## Why 2 strands
A one-idea fable needs only its allegory and its monster.

---

# DNA: The Ice Man (1991 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 4/1991)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A shy twenty-year-old woman meets an Ice Man reading alone in a ski-hotel lobby. He knows her whole past but has no past of his own, and he has no interest in the future. They marry against her family's wishes and live quietly while he works in a meat freezer. Bored by the sameness, she proposes a trip, and she picks the South Pole because she thinks it would please him. He changes as soon as the place is named. At the Pole he is at home, the planes stop coming, and she finds she is carrying an ice child. She is left crying frozen tears in the coldest place on earth. A marriage fable runs on a metaphor taken literally.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Married to a man made of cold — level: premise
- What it is: The Ice Man has frosted fingers, white breath and icicle eyes. He is cold but does not melt, and he has no family register, so no ceremony is possible. Society tolerates him but never accepts the couple. Their love exists only in the present tense, and lovemaking feels to her like sharing a vast block of ancient ice.
- Why it is specific to this work: The marriage metaphor is made physical and treated as plain fact from the first line, and every scene follows from that literal coldness.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.200], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.203], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.204]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley reads it as a symbolic exploration of marriage anxiety from a woman's viewpoint, one of Murakami's most sober and chilling stories. Also [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7] — Murakami says the story grew from a dream his wife had.

### 2. Ice keeps only the past — level: theme
- What it is: The Ice Man explains that ice seals the past and contains no future. Her recurring nightmare is to be frozen in a hole and watched as she turns into pure past. He tells her that dreams come from the past and should be controlled. At the Pole, the weight of an endless past grips the little family for good, and his words of love blow away into the past.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story defines a rule (past without future) and makes the heroine's fate obey it. She ends as one more thing preserved in ice.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.202], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.205], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.206], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.207], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.208]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley analyzes the ice/past contrast: he remembers her past but has none, while she cannot learn the Ice People's language. He finds the ice forming in her womb the most horrifying image.

### 3. Her own wish becomes the trap — level: structure
- What it is: She suggests the trip to cure boredom and chooses the destination to please him. Once it is named, he grows colder and immovable and has already bought the tickets. Even her request to cancel cannot undo it. At the Pole there are no penguins, no aurora and no language she can learn, then no more planes. The pregnancy closes the last exit.
- Why it is specific to this work: The plot is a one-way funnel triggered by a loving suggestion. The first-person wife tells it, and in places it slips into the present tense, so the reader hears her from inside the trap.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.204], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.205], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.206], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.207]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1989-1991/ — Walley notes that Murakami's female narrators still have anxieties that revolve around men, and he reads the ending as ice "colonizing" her.

## Weak candidates
- Speech without quotation marks throughout (p.201-202), so dialogue feels remembered. This is a strong texture but shared with other stories (e.g. Tony Takitani).

## Why 3 strands
A literal metaphor, the rule that governs it and the plot that springs the trap. That is the whole fable.

---

# DNA: Crabs (first published inside "Baseball Diamond" 1984, revised as a separate story 2003 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 2003)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A young Tokyo couple on vacation in Singapore stumble on a pink back-street crab restaurant. She brags about her sense for good places and says life depends on which side of a fence you fall. They eat crab there every night and call it paradise. On the last night he vomits white crabmeat into the toilet and sees it covered with tiny moving worms. She sleeps on, unaware that the same crab is inside her. He sits in a rattan chair until dawn, knowing he will never feel the same about her or himself, and vows never to eat crab again. One private, disgusting revelation ends a relationship that does not yet know it is over.

## DNA strands (2)

### 1. The worms only he sees — level: premise
- What it is: The nausea, the moonlit toilet and the moving surface of the crabmeat are given in close physical detail. He scrubs himself, sees an old ashen face in the mirror and decides not to wake her, because she did not notice, and that not noticing is the real problem. The world now seems to run in a new orbit, and the sleep he longs for stays out of reach.
- Why it is specific to this work: The break comes from an outside event that only one partner experiences, and nothing either of them does. The asymmetry is the whole story.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.211], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.212], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.213]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2002-2003 — Walley says the story shows how a couple's bond can strengthen or weaken through how each reacts to an outside event, not through anything either does. Because he is sickened and she is not, he becomes aware of things she never notices.

### 2. The fence, and her line turned against him — level: device
- What it is: On the first night she says life can change depending on whether you walk into the good restaurant or the bad one, which side of the fence you land on, and he agrees that they landed inside. At dawn he feels they have both fallen off the fence on the outside, silently, and she never noticed. Her cheerful question about loving crab comes back in his head, and he can only answer that he doesn't know.
- Why it is specific to this work: Her throwaway philosophy and her small talk become the frame of his disaster, so the ending is built from the opening dialogue.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.210], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.213], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.214]
- External: none found

## Weak candidates
- The comic-sad closing vow never to eat crab again (p.214). It is the signature final beat, but it is part of strand 2.
- The two silent old Chinese men and the dog at the restaurant, recalled at dawn as figures he has now come to resemble (p.209, p.213). This is a strong echo but a supporting detail.

## Why 2 strands
A short, tight story: the revelation and the phrase that frames it.

---

# DNA: Firefly (1983 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 1/83)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
Fifteen years on, the narrator recalls his first Tokyo year, 1967-68, in a right-wing-run dorm with a daily flag ceremony and a stuttering map-obsessed roommate. He meets the girlfriend of his best friend from high school, who killed himself at seventeen after beating the narrator at pool. The two walk across Tokyo almost without speaking, Sunday after Sunday. She cannot find her words, and he has learned that death lives inside life. On her twentieth birthday she talks for four hours, breaks down, and they sleep together. Then she leaves for a sanatorium. The roommate gives him a firefly in a jar, and he releases it from the water tower into the dark. It is a realist story of grief that the two survivors share but cannot speak.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. Walking with a dead friend's girlfriend — level: premise
- What it is: The friend used to carry every conversation among the three of them, and without him the two survivors have nothing to say. After a chance meeting on a train, they walk huge silent loops through Tokyo. Gradually she walks beside him rather than ahead, but he feels she wants someone else's warmth. The relationship peaks in one night and ends in her letter from the mountains.
- Why it is specific to this work: The bond rests entirely on an absent third person, and the walking replaces the conversation they cannot have.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.222], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.224], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.226], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.227], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.231]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley reads it as a love story about the narrator's strange year with his late best friend's girlfriend, and as the kernel of Norwegian Wood. Also [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.8] — Murakami says this story was later reworked into part of Norwegian Wood.

### 2. Death inside life, and words that won't come — level: theme
- What it is: The friend's suicide comes with no note or motive, the day after an ordinary pool game. The narrator tries to forget and is left with one idea that forms like air: death is not life's opposite but part of it, present in every object. She describes language as chasing her other self around a pillar. His own feelings sink before he can speak them, and her final letter apologizes that she still cannot find the words.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story states this idea as its core lesson (the same idea later carries Norwegian Wood) and joins it to the inability of both survivors to speak.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.223], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.225], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.226], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.227], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.232]
- External: https://www.bookreporter.com/reviews/blind-willow-sleeping-woman-24-stories — Bookreporter groups Firefly with stories whose characters try to connect, forget their mortality and end up alone, and notes that it appeared in Norwegian Wood.

### 3. The dorm's rituals and the firefly — level: device
- What it is: The dorm runs on fixed rituals: the flag going up and down to a taped anthem, and the roommate's unskippable radio exercises and compulsive cleaning. These give the comedy and the passing of seasons. The same roommate gives the narrator the firefly. On the roof, its light is dimmer than he remembers. He sets it on the rail, waits a long time, and watches it fly off, leaving a glow he cannot reach.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title creature is a farewell image in which memory, fading light and the lost girl merge. It comes from the comic roommate, out of the world that was meant only as background.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.216], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.220], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.227], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.232], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.233], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.234], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.235]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-1982-1984/ — Walley also reads the story as a portrait of being caught between hard left and hard right in the late 1960s, which the dorm embodies.

## Weak candidates
- The Henry Mancini record ("Dear Heart") played over and over on the birthday night, with her tears on its jacket (p.228-230, p.232). It is a vivid prop but folds into strand 1.
- The idea that the two of them belonged between eighteen and nineteen while the dead friend stays seventeen forever (p.228-229). It is a lovely line of thought within strand 2.

## Why 3 strands
A relationship built on absence, a stated idea about death and the setting that gives the final image. This is the minimum needed to explain why the story ends on a firefly.

---

# DNA: Chance Traveler (2005 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 3/2005)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
"Haruki Murakami" steps out like the narrator in front of a stage curtain. He tells two true jazz coincidences: Tommy Flanagan closing a set with the two obscure tunes he had silently wished for, and being asked the time just after buying an LP whose title names that exact time. Then he hands over to a friend's tale. A gay piano tuner reads Bleak House every Tuesday in a mall café and meets a married woman reading the same book. She falls for him and confides a breast-cancer scare. A mole on her earlobe like his estranged sister's prompts him to call his sister after ten years, and he learns she has surgery for breast cancer the next day. They reconcile. The author ends hoping that some god is watching over the woman from the café.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The author as prologue, then a friend's story — level: structure
- What it is: The story opens by stating that the "I" is the author himself. He complains that nobody believes his strange experiences and gives two small, verifiable-sounding jazz anecdotes with album details. He then introduces the friend's account, admitting he changed some facts and skipped a part that would take too long. He returns at the end to discuss the friend's theory and to admit he does not know how things turned out.
- Why it is specific to this work: Two layers of chance (the author's own and the friend's) frame each other, and the author's own name vouches for the strangeness.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.236], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.237], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.238], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.239], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.253]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus notes that Murakami often appears in these stories under his own name, and calls this one a virtuoso exploration of coincidence.

### 2. A chain of coincidences that leads home — level: premise
- What it is: The same unfashionable Dickens novel at neighboring tables, a woman's sudden attraction, her cancer scare, a mole in the same place as his sister's, and a phone call made on the eve of the sister's operation. The tuner's closing theory is that coincidences surround us like daytime fireworks and become visible only when we badly want something. The author offers a simpler belief in a god of jazz.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's events are nothing but linked chances, and the characters themselves name chance as the subject.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.242], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.244], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.245], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.247], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.251], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.252]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley calls it a story about chance.

### 3. A self found by giving things up — level: theme
- What it is: The tuner discovered at music college that he was gay, came out, lost friends and his sister's goodwill, and also gave up being a concert pianist. He tells his sister that both renunciations returned him to his real self and made music enjoyable for the first time. He wanted to be understood without explaining. His private rule is to choose what has no form over what has form. The reconciliation is sealed when he kisses her mole.
- Why it is specific to this work: The central consciousness is a contented gay man whose identity, not the coincidence, is what has to be accepted in the end, by the sister and even by the crude brother-in-law.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.239], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.240], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.246], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.249], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.250], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.252]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley sees the story as defending a gay man's perspective in contemporary Japan, part of Murakami's long move away from a straight salaryman viewpoint.

## Weak candidates
- The Tuesday routine (river crossing, café, tuna salad and Perrier, gym) that the strange event breaks (p.241). It is a strong texture but serves strand 2.

## Why 3 strands
An author's frame, a chain of chance and the identity that chance finally lets be accepted.

---

# DNA: Hanalei Bay (2005 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 4/2005)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
Sachi, a Tokyo piano-bar owner, learns that her nineteen-year-old son drowned after a shark took his leg while he surfed in Hanalei Bay on Kauai. She identifies him, has him cremated and then returns every autumn for three weeks to sit on the beach and watch surfers. Ten years in, she gives a ride to two clueless young Japanese surfers. They later tell her they saw a one-legged Japanese surfer standing near her usual spot. She searches the whole beach and never sees him, and she weeps at the unfairness before accepting the island as it is. The coda shows her playing every night, thinking only of Hanalei. Mourning is turned into a yearly ritual and a ghost meant for other people.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The annual vigil at the place of death — level: premise
- What it is: The opening states the death with clinical precision: properly speaking, he drowned. The policeman asks her not to hate the island, since nature takes no sides. Sachi sits in a vinyl chair watching surfers, with her mind fixed in the present, and returns every year for the same three weeks in the same cottage. A short present-tense coda makes the habit permanent, and the place name stands alone as the last line.
- Why it is specific to this work: The grief is not a story event but a repeated stay in one bay, and the title is both the place and the final word.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.254], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.256], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.257], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.259], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.273], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.274]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley counts it among Murakami's best stories and compares it to "Tony Takitani" for fitting a whole life into a few pages.

### 2. The one-legged ghost seen only by strangers — level: device
- What it is: The two boys she helped describe a Japanese surfer of their age, missing the right leg above the knee, with a red Dick Brewer board (the same brand as her son's, which the shark destroyed), standing under the iron trees near her chair. She never sees him. Alone, she rages at the son who appears to strangers but not to her, then resolves to accept the island fair or unfair.
- Why it is specific to this work: The supernatural comes through third-party witnesses whose details match facts the reader already knows, and the mother is the one person excluded.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.255], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.258], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.270], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.271]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley finds the ending deeply moving and reads it as the son appearing only to those who love what he loved.

### 3. Loving a son she did not like; a talent for copying — level: theme
- What it is: Sachi has perfect pitch and can copy any record but cannot improvise, a self-described musical chameleon who plays by ear in bars. She admits that she loved her son but never liked him as a person, and that only blood held them together. Sitting in at a Hanalei restaurant piano opens her up, and she imagines that surfing did the same for him. The tough, blunt way she treats the surf boys and the drunken ex-marine shows the mothering she never quite gave him.
- Why it is specific to this work: The story's emotional honesty, a mother not liking her child, is tied to music. The shared passion, not shared blood, decides who sees the ghost.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.263], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.264], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.265], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.266], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.267], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.269]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley concludes that we love those who share our passions more than those bound to us by blood, and admires Sachi's struggles as a single mother and musician.

## Weak candidates
- The Starbucks epilogue with the stocky boy, who is now job-hunting, and her three rules for winning a girl (p.271-273). It is a warm grace note.
- Officer Sakata's brother killed in the 442nd Regiment in 1944 (p.256). The war-versus-nature contrast supports strand 1.

## Why 3 strands
A ritual of mourning, a ghost withheld from the mourner and the uncomfortable truth that explains why. The ghost needs the other two strands to mean anything.

---

# DNA: Where I'm Likely to Find It (2005 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 5/2005)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
A woman in stiletto heels hires the unnamed first-person narrator. Her stockbroker husband phoned to say he was coming up from his panicky mother's apartment on the 24th floor and wanted his pancakes ready. Then he vanished somewhere on the stairs before the 26th. The narrator works for no fee, as a rule. He walks the staircase about two hundred times, rests on the vinyl sofa by a mirror, and talks with a stair runner, an old smoker full of aphorisms and a small girl who says this mirror makes people look happier. Twenty days later the husband turns up asleep in Sendai Station, thinner and with no memory. The narrator, left alone, resumes his own search for something that may be a door, an umbrella, a doughnut or an elephant. A missing-person case turns into the searcher's own unexplained quest.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A man lost between two floors — level: premise
- What it is: A two-floor walk up a broad, clean, well-lit staircase, which the husband valued as the backbone of the building, becomes a gap in the world. He leaves no wallet, no watch, no shave and a promise of pancakes, so running away makes no sense. He returns weeks later from far away, wearing the same clothes, with no memory of the missing time.
- Why it is specific to this work: The mystery is spatial and tiny (a few minutes of stairs), and the story's setting is that staircase.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.280], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.283], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.284], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.293]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus calls it a searching, Kafkaesque parable about disappearance, loss and coping.

### 2. A volunteer seeker of doors — level: theme
- What it is: The narrator takes no money on principle because payment would make his actions meaningless. He has another income, and his only interest is people who vanish in a certain way. He tells the girl he has been looking since before she was born for something he can't describe, maybe a door, though it could be anything, and that he will know it when he sees it. When the case closes, the husband goes back to his three-sided world and the narrator's search goes on, as the title says.
- Why it is specific to this work: The client's case is a pretext. The real engine is the detective's private, shapeless quest, which the ending (and the title) hands back to him.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.281], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.282], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.292], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.293], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.295]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley calls the narrator deliberately enigmatic, wonders whether he is displaced and seeking a way back, and notes his unexplained income.

### 3. Memo pad and staircase procedure — level: structure
- What it is: The narrator chooses the right pencil like a golf club and logs the client's facts as terse lists. He then patrols the stairs daily, checks his mirrored reflection (left and right swap as they should), whittles away twenty-five minutes on purpose and interviews whoever passes. At the end he lists the closing facts on a fresh pad, lets the blank page drift into a calico cat on clean sheets, and reads a star chart in ceiling stains.
- Why it is specific to this work: A deadpan detective procedure applied to something that cannot be detected. The comedy of method is how the story moves.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.275], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.279], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.285], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.286], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.288], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.294]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley calls it very much a product of the 1980s, with hard-boiled and surreal touches, like an outtake from Dance Dance Dance.

## Weak candidates
- Pancakes as a running craving and the joke about smoking for one's health (p.285, p.288, p.294). These are callback gags inside strand 3.
- The mirror on the 25th-floor landing that shows a plumper, happier self (p.290). It hints at a door, but the story drops it.

## Why 3 strands
The vanishing, the seeker's private quest and the procedure that links them. The ending depends on the second strand outgrowing the first.

---

# DNA: The Kidney-Shaped Stone That Moves Every Day (2005 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 6/2005)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
When Junpei was sixteen, his father declared that a man meets only three women who truly matter. Having lost the first to his best friend, Junpei keeps relationships pale so as not to waste the remaining two. At thirty-one he meets Kirie, an older woman who will not say what she does and who refuses serious ties. In bed he tells her his stalled story: a doctor's kidney-shaped paperweight that moves every night. Her remark that everything has its reasons unlocks it. After he finishes the story, Kirie's phone stops working. He hears her on the radio: she walks tightropes between high-rise buildings, alone with the wind. He decides she was number two and stops counting. In the last lines, the doctor's stone is gone for good. A love story and the story Junpei is writing advance together.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. The three-women countdown — level: premise
- What it is: The father's cool pronouncement becomes a curse that Junpei half believes, a tally that makes him fear playing his cards too early and choose partners who will be easy to leave. Each new woman raises the same question. With Kirie he finally decides she counts, and then that the counting itself means nothing.
- Why it is specific to this work: The whole romantic arc is measured against a numerical prophecy, and the resolution is to throw the scorecard away.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.296], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.297], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.300], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.311], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.315]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley notes that the father's prophecy echoes Kafka on the Shore but here seems meant to teach Junpei about commitment.

### 2. The story inside the story — level: structure
- What it is: Junpei is a short-story writer who has repeatedly failed at novels. His unfinished tale of a doctor whose kidney-shaped stone moves every night stalls. Kirie pushes it, and he finishes it in five days: the stone slowly takes over the doctor's life, she leaves her married lover, throws the stone into the sea and finds it back on her desk. Junpei realizes the stone expresses something inside her (and the reader sees it in him). A final two-line coda in the present tense, set off by a section break, makes the stone disappear just as Junpei lets go of his countdown.
- Why it is specific to this work: The title belongs to the inner story, and the two plots resolve in the same beat. It is a writer's tale about how a story gets written.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.304], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.307], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.308], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.310], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.311], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.315]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley finds it more openly allegorical than usual for Murakami but effective as a meditation on love and commitment. He also links Junpei to the writer in "Honey Pie".

### 3. Kirie, the wind and the high wire — level: device
- What it is: Kirie turns her job into a guessing game: something needing perfection from the start, with no second chances. She talks about balance and about the wind knowing everything inside you. She vanishes. Junpei then hears on a taxi radio that she runs a high-rise window-cleaning firm and walks ropes between skyscrapers without a lifeline, alone with the wind. He finds himself jealous of the wind.
- Why it is specific to this work: The withheld profession pays off as a literal image of her emotional life: total concentration with no room for anyone else. It also explains why she cannot stay.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.300], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.302], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.306], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.309], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.312], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.313], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.314]
- External: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman — The story table's summary notes that the narrator's girlfriend is a tightrope walker and that the stone keeps returning to the doctor's desk.

## Weak candidates
- James Taylor's "Up on the Roof" playing between parts of the radio interview (p.313). It is a sly clue but minor.
- Her phrase that work should be an act of love, borrowed from Junpei and repeated on air (p.302, p.313). It is a strong echo but serves strand 3.

## Why 3 strands
A prophecy, a nested story and the woman who breaks both open. This is the densest of the 2005 stories, but it does not need more.

---

# DNA: A Shinagawa Monkey (2005 — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman; Walley dates it 9/2005)
Type: short story (in Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman)

## Engine in one paragraph
Mizuki Ando, twenty-six, a car-dealership clerk who still goes by her maiden name at work, keeps forgetting her own name, and only her own. A doctor dismisses her, so she visits a cheap new counseling service in the Shinagawa ward office. The counselor, a slow, friendly woman, digs up a memory: in high school, the dorm's prettiest girl talked to Mizuki about jealousy, left her name tag with her (so a monkey wouldn't take it) and then killed herself. Mizuki finds the tag missing from her closet. The counselor's husband, a public-works section chief, catches the culprit in the sewers: an aging talking monkey who steals names he loves. Pressed, the monkey tells her what was stuck to her name. Her mother and sister never loved her, and she has never fully loved anyone. She gets her name back with the truth attached. A light bureaucratic comedy carries a harsh revelation.

## DNA strands (3)

### 1. A woman who forgets only her own name — level: premise
- What it is: The opening states the oddity in one flat line, then measures it: only her name, only when asked suddenly, more often over the year. She copes practically with an engraved bracelet that makes her feel like a pet, and hides it from her overly logical husband. Her life, as she recounts it, is a flat documentary with no drama. The ending returns her name as something that is hers alone.
- Why it is specific to this work: The symptom is precise, small and identity-shaped, and the whole plot is a search for its cause.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.316], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.317], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.319], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.322], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.343]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley notes how the story first seems to be about a dissatisfied wife. He wonders whether her sense of never having been loved is what cost her her name.

### 2. The dead girl's name tag — level: device
- What it is: A dorm board shows each girl's tag black side out when she is in and red side out when she is out. Before vanishing, Yuko visits Mizuki, asks whether she has ever felt jealous, describes jealousy as a growing tumor and private hell, and leaves her tag so that a monkey won't run off with it. Mizuki keeps both tags for years, untold. In the present, the envelope has disappeared from the box.
- Why it is specific to this work: An ordinary dorm object links the present symptom, a past suicide and the monkey. Yuko's apparent joke turns out to be literal.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.325], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.326], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.327], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.328], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.330], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.335]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley praises the misdirection, as the story seems at one point to be about jealousy before it becomes about a monkey.

### 3. The name-stealing monkey who tells the truth — level: premise
- What it is: In a basement storeroom, a bound, polite, penitent monkey confesses. He steals names he falls for, he loved Yuko, and he grabbed Mizuki's tag too. The Public Works men argue about disinfectant shots and branding with the ward seal in terms of liability. The monkey claims that taking a name also takes its darkness. At Mizuki's request he names hers: unloved by mother and sister, unable to love fully, her husband included. She accepts it as true, frees him to Mount Takao and gives him Yuko's tag.
- Why it is specific to this work: The comic surreal creature, framed by ward-office bureaucracy, delivers the story's one painful truth. The joke and the wound are the same scene.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.320], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.334], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.336], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.338], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.339], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.340], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.341], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.342]
- External: https://blogs.uoregon.edu/glynnewalleyjlit/other-modern-writers/murakami-haruki/stories-2005/ — Walley calls it classic Murakami pop surrealism and finds the sewer-dwelling, talking, penitent monkey very appealing.

## Weak candidates
- Mrs. Sakaki's unexplained "powers" and her offer to catch something else together next time (p.338, p.343). This is charm rather than engine.
- The married name versus the maiden name kept at work out of laziness (p.318-319). It is a quiet clue to divided identity that the story never presses.

## Why 3 strands
A symptom, the object that links it to the past and the creature that explains it. The story is built like a case file that ends in a confession.

---

# Collection-wide DNA: Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman (both halves)

The 24 stories span 1980 to 2005 (dates from https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_Willow,_Sleeping_Woman). Murakami presents the book as his first real story collection abroad in a long time, unlike the concept album of after the quake [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.6-7]. Because of that, shared DNA here means recurring mechanisms rather than a planned unity. Four stand out across both halves.

### C1. The strange tale told plainly and left open — level: stance
- What it is: An impossible or unexplained thing is reported in level, factual prose and is never solved. The owner's wish and room 604 (Part A), the mirror that was never there (Part A), the friend in the wave, the one-legged surfer, the husband lost between floors and the stone that comes back all work this way.
- Why it is specific to this collection: Murakami himself says the 2005 stories share the theme of strange tales and that, in a sense, everything he writes is one [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.7]. The collection is a catalog of ways to leave a mystery standing.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.35], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.64], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.166], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.270], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.293], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.311]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus describes strange experiences that deepen into mysteries, told in brisk sentences that withhold rational explanations.

### C2. A story told to someone — level: structure
- What it is: Many pieces are framed as one person telling another, at a bar, by a fireplace, in a story circle, over coffee, in a counseling office or in a client interview. Often the listener is a writer, sometimes named Murakami. Each frame sets a scene of listening, cuts back to it now and then and ends there.
- Why it is specific to this collection: The listening scene recurs from the earliest stories to the 2005 ones, and it is how the book makes strange events sound like testimony.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.35], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.70], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.160], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.239], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.252], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.323]
- External: https://kirkusreviews.com/book-reviews/haruki-murakami/blind-willow-sleeping-woman — Kirkus notes that Murakami often appears in the stories under his own name or as "the writer".

### C3. The one left behind — level: theme
- What it is: The protagonist is very often a survivor of someone's sudden death or disappearance: the friend killed soon after the hospital visit (Part A), K in the wave, Tony's wife in the crash, the friend in the garage, Sachi's son, Yuko in the woods. The story follows the long, quiet aftermath, not the event.
- Why it is specific to this collection: Across 25 years of writing, the book keeps returning to the same position, a living person facing an absence that will not explain itself.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.16], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.167], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.189], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.225], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.254], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.328]
- External: https://www.bookreporter.com/reviews/blind-willow-sleeping-woman-24-stories — Bookreporter says many stories show characters struggling to connect and ending up alone. Kirkus (link above) names loss, regret and alienation as the collection's recurring subjects.

### C4. An object that stands in for the absent person — level: device
- What it is: Grief and memory gather around one concrete thing the dead or missing person left: a box of chocolates (Part A), K's watercolors, a room of size-7 dresses and then a father's records, a Christmas record, a boy's belongings in one small bag and a shredded surfboard, a dorm name tag. Characters keep, dispose of, give away or lose these objects, and that act marks how the grief has changed.
- Why it is specific to this collection: In story after story, a single object carries the emotional turn: K's paintings bring the narrator home, Tony empties the room, Mizuki hands the tag to the monkey.
- In-text refs: [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.16], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.169], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.191], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.232], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.255], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.341]
- External: none found

## Weak candidates (collection level)
- The short story as a laboratory for novels. Murakami calls his stories an experimental lab and names prototypes ("Firefly" for Norwegian Wood and "Man-Eating Cats" for Sputnik Sweetheart) [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.6], [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman, Intro p.8]. Inside the book, Junpei is a born short-story writer whose novels keep stalling [Blind Willow, Sleeping Woman p.304]. This is true of how the book came about, but it is not a mechanism readers meet on every page.
- Jazz and records as identity markers (Tony's father, the Flanagan coincidence, Sachi's chameleon playing, the Mancini record). These are central in several stories, but jazz is a generic Murakami habit unless a story makes it structural, as Chance Traveler and Hanalei Bay do.

## Why 4 collection strands
A mixed career-spanning collection shares a voice and a situation rather than a plot: a plain report of the strange, a listener, a survivor and an object.

---

Stories covered: 12 of 24 (stories 13-24 in table-of-contents order: The Seventh Man; The Year of Spaghetti; Tony Takitani; The Rise and Fall of Sharpie Cakes; The Ice Man; Crabs; Firefly; Chance Traveler; Hanalei Bay; Where I'm Likely to Find It; The Kidney-Shaped Stone That Moves Every Day; A Shinagawa Monkey). Every story in this half was analyzed. Stories 1-12 are in /home/claude/murakami/dna/Blind_Willow_A.md. A collection-wide section covering both halves is included above.
